# <unclear>AELIA LA</unclear>ELIA
# <unclear>CRISP</unclear>IS
<note>original: "Aelia Laelia Crispis." This is the name given to the subject of the famous "Bologna Enigma," a mysterious Latin funerary inscription discovered in the 16th century at the Villa Volta. The inscription is famous for its paradoxical nature—describing a person who is "neither man, nor woman, nor hermaphrodite"—and has been interpreted by scholars as everything from an alchemical formula to a legal joke.</note>

## <unclear>NOT BORN, RES</unclear>URGING
<note>original: "Non nata resurgens." This Latin phrase refers to the paradoxical nature of the subject’s existence and suggests themes of rebirth or perhaps an alchemical "rising" of a substance.</note>

<image-desc>
An elaborate engraving of a commemorative monument. At the top of the structure, a winged sphinx with a human female head and breasts sits on the left, connected by a chain to a winged lion sitting on the right. One of the lion's wings has the word "LIBERTY" inscribed upon it. Between them is a circular emblem featuring a complex knot design. The body of the monument contains multiple inset panels with text. The large central panel contains the famous "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle. It is surrounded by several smaller boxes containing titles and names of authors associated with various interpretations of the enigma (such as Niobe, Rain, Love, the Rational Soul, and the Philosopher's Stone). At the base of the monument, a ribbon-like scroll contains a Latin dedication by Achilles Volta.

<note>original: "Aelia Laelia Crispis"</note>
# AELIA LAELIA
## CRISPIS <note>Aelia Laelia Crispis is the name of a famous, enigmatic Latin inscription found in Bologna. For centuries, it was considered one of the world's great unsolved riddles, prompting various legal, alchemical, and theological interpretations.</note>
### NOT BORN, RISING AGAIN

->In a Legal Interpretation<-
->By COUNT CARLO CESARE MALVASIA<- <note>Malvasia (1616–1693) was a significant Bolognese nobleman, legal scholar, and art historian, best known for his biographies of Baroque painters.</note>
->Doctor of both Civil and Canon Law and Doctor of the Sacred Scriptures, Member of the College,<-
->as well as in the Local University <note>The Archiginnasio of Bologna, one of the oldest universities in the world.</note> during the evening hours,<-
->PRIMARY ORDINARY INTERPRETER OF THE LAWS.<-
->TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS MAN<-
->LORD<-

## JEAN-BAPTISTE
## COLBERT <note>Colbert (1619–1683) served as the Minister of Finances of France under King Louis XIV and was a famed patron of the arts and sciences.</note>

->DEDICATED.<-

->BOLOGNA, from the Press of the Heirs of Domenico Barbieri. 1683. With the permission of the Superiors.<-
->At the expense of Giuseppe Antonio David de Turinis.<-

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS MAN, LORD JEAN-BAPTISTE COLBERT
## MEMBER OF THE KING’S PRIVY COUNCIL <note>original: "Regi ab intimis consiliis"</note>
->And of his Private Commands, Controller-General of the Treasury,<-
->Superintendent of Royal Buildings,<-
->Treasurer of the Royal Orders, etc.<-

### CARLO CESARE MALVASIA WISHES HAPPINESS.

<image-desc>An engraving of a tree with a long scroll intertwined through its branches. The scroll bears the motto: "ANOTHER IS NOT LACKING" <note>original Latin: "Non deficit alter." This is a quote from Virgil's Aeneid, referring to the Golden Bough; when one is plucked, another grows in its place. Here, it likely signifies the enduring nature of the dedicatee's family or the author's academic efforts.</note>.</image-desc>

As I myself was about to hatch this "egg"—my own commentary <note>original: "Ovum... commentum"</note> on the enigmatic epitaph of AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS <note>Malvasia uses the word "egg" (ovum) as a metaphor for his scholarly work, likely playing on the alchemical interpretations of the Aelia Laelia riddle, which often compared the mystery to a philosopher's egg.</note>—I did not have to search long for a name under which it should be published.

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Carlo Cesare Malvasia's scholarly work to Jean-Baptiste Colbert, the powerful French minister. It features Colbert's heraldic serpent and a classical motto, framing the author's commentary as a long-gestating intellectual "egg."</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Baptiste Colbert, Carlo Cesare Malvasia, Aelia Laelia Crispis, dedication, heraldry, Controller-General of Finances, Virgil, Golden Bough, legal commentary</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert. The previous page concluded with Malvasia stating he did not have to search long for a name to grace his work; this page picks up mid-sentence, explaining that he did not have "longer to delay" in showing his gratitude.</meta>

...longer to delay, **MOST ILLUSTRIOUS COLBERT**; for since those greatest favors, with which you wished me to be bound to you again and again very recently, urged and impelled me to give at least some sign of a grateful heart toward You, this present little work, now finally completed <note>original: "ad umbilicum jam deductum," a classical idiom referring to the finishing of a scroll, literally "brought to the navel."</note>, has opportunely satisfied my wish, which was impatient of further delay. It is, I confess, a slight and meager token of my eternal respect; yet it serves better as a swift acknowledgement of my immense obligation than as a means to discharge any part of so great a debt <note>original: "aes alienum," literally "another's bronze," the standard Latin term for a financial debt.</note>. For indeed, when—at the helpful encouragement of the most learned Abbot de la Chambre <note>Marin Cureau de la Chambre (1594–1669), a French royal physician and intellectual who served as a bridge between Italian scholars and the French court.</note>—I was at that time very humbly dedicating my **PAINTERLY FELSINA** <note>original: "PICTRICEM FELSINAM," the Latin title for Malvasia’s *Felsina Pittrice*, his most famous work celebrating the history of Bolognese painters. Felsina is the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna.</note> to the Unconquered and ever-Triumphant **LOUIS THE GREAT** <note>King Louis XIV of France.</note>, or rather the Thrice-Greatest—

<meta>catchword: poris (part of the word "temporis," meaning "at that time")</meta>

<summary>Malvasia expresses his urgent desire to thank Colbert for recent favors by dedicating this "little work" to him. He acknowledges his massive debt of gratitude and recalls his previous dedication of the Felsina Pittrice to King Louis XIV.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Baptiste Colbert, Louis XIV, Felsina Pittrice, Marin Cureau de la Chambre, patronage, gratitude, Bologna, art history</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert. The catchword "poris" from the previous page completes the word "temporis" (of that time).</meta>

...at that time <note>original: "temporis."</note> I did not fear, even when He himself—the most vigorous leader of his own armies—was away from the Royal Court to subdue new kingdoms; for during those years, this work lay hidden and almost forgotten in the cabinets of the famous **Library of Versailles** <term>Versaliensis Bibliotheca: The royal library of Louis XIV, which later became a core part of the Bibliothèque nationale de France.</term>. But you, **MOST ILLUSTRIOUS SIR**, with that incomparable kindness with which you cherish all followers of **Letters and the Fine Arts** <note>original: "Literarum, ac Bonarum Artium."</note> and honor them with every service, ordered this work to be sought out—a great task in itself—and brought to you. You examined and weighed its merits more than once, which was an even greater favor, and then—the greatest honor of all—you judged it not entirely unworthy of the King’s own gaze and ineffable grace. 

Hence, having been brought before the most merciful sight of such a Hero <note>A common Baroque rhetorical flourish referring to King Louis XIV.</note>, the work was graced by his delight and—far beyond its merit—by his recommendation. Under your auspices, it earned the right to receive a **Royal gift** <note>original: "Regale munus." This likely refers to a royal pension or formal patronage granted to Malvasia.</note>. And although...

<summary>Malvasia describes how his work, previously forgotten in the Library of Versailles while Louis XIV was at war, was rediscovered and championed by Colbert. Due to Colbert's influence, the work was presented to the King, earning his favor and a royal reward.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Baptiste Colbert, Louis XIV, Library of Versailles, royal patronage, Fine Arts, Felsina Pittrice</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert, describing a misfortune that befell the author and the subsequent royal restoration of his favor. The catchword from the previous page was "quem," though the previous translation provided ended with "munus."</meta>

...And although envious fate <note>original: "fors invida"</note> impiously stood in the way, when with cursed daring, the wicked hand of a villainous thief did not hesitate to snatch it away, you did not long allow me to live deprived of such splendor, or to waste away almost entirely with intolerable anguish. An Italian poem concerning that sacrilegious crime, which my anger and sorrow had dictated with a rushing Muse, was most humbly offered to You through the famous **Le Brun** <note>Charles Le Brun (1619–1690), the dominant figure in 17th-century French art and director of the Academy.</note>, truly the **Apelles** <note>Apelles of Kos was the most famous painter of ancient Greece; this comparison identifies Le Brun as the greatest artist of the modern era.</note> of our age. You received it kindly, and once received, you presented it to the **KING**. Having thus obtained a new gift, you prove the singular magnanimity of our Hero [the King], which—like the **Elysian Tree** <note>A reference to a mythical or paradisiacal tree that remains eternally fruitful; here, it symbolizes the King's inexhaustible generosity.</note>—is never lacking in precious branches, and you ordered it to be sent to me once again. 

While, therefore, I receive this second Image of our Monarch, which is encircled by a double ring of diamonds brighter than the stars themselves and crowned with a diadem—radiating more than the **SUN** itself, which represents the **KING**, or more truly...

<summary>Malvasia describes how a previous royal gift was stolen by a thief, causing him great distress. After he wrote a poem about the crime, Colbert and the painter Charles Le Brun intervened to show it to Louis XIV, who graciously replaced the stolen gift with a diamond-encrusted portrait.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Baptiste Colbert, Louis XIV, Charles Le Brun, Apelles, royal gift, patronage, theft, Elysian Tree</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the effusive dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert. The previous page ended with a description of a royal portrait—a gift from Louis XIV—set in diamonds, which Malvasia compares to the sun. The catchword "quem" from the previous page begins the first line of this page.</meta>

...which the **KING** <note>original: "REX"</note> represents, shines more splendidly, I receive with bent knee and a devoted heart, I gaze upon it, and I venerate it. Allow me (I also pray to You as a suppliant) not to delay any longer in acknowledging here, and briefly professing, so great an honor—which, though it belongs specifically to me, is nevertheless shared by my fellow citizens, sent down to us from on high through You.

There will be a time when, once this immense joy has subsided a little, I shall gather my strength, and having traversed the vast reaches of your merits, I shall finally stand before the humble altar of my devotion <note>original: "devotio"</note> toward You—now sprinkled only with salted grain <note>original: "salsa mola" — the *mola salsa* was a mixture of toasted flour and salt used by the ancient Romans to sprinkle on the heads of sacrificial victims before they were killed. Malvasia uses this to suggest his current thanks are just a small preliminary offering.</note>—where, having offered worthier incense, I shall place fatter victims <note>original: "victimae"</note> to be sacrificed to You. Meanwhile, while I set forth this brief declaration of the Inscription <note>The author is likely referring to the formal dedication or the title-page inscription of the book.</note>, I ask You, best of Patrons <note>original: "Mecoenas" — Gaius Maecenas was the legendary patron of the Roman poets Horace and Virgil; his name became a synonym for a generous supporter of the arts and sciences.</note>, again and again, that you receive this hurried boldness in the best light, and graciously excu-

<summary>Malvasia expresses his profound gratitude to Colbert for the royal gift, promising to provide a much more substantial tribute once his initial excitement has settled. He uses classical sacrificial metaphors to describe his current modest thanks as a mere prelude to a greater future work.</summary>
<keywords>Louis XIV, Jean-Baptiste Colbert, patronage, royal gift, sacrifice metaphor, Maecenas, devotion, inscription</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert, continuing from the previous page where Malvasia requested the patron to "graciously excuse" his boldness.</meta>

-se my boldness, and do not cease to favor me with the kindness you have shown me until now. Farewell, **Refuge of the Learned** <note>original: "Literatorum Asylum" — a title suggesting Colbert was a great protector and patron of scholars and scientists during the reign of Louis XIV.</note>: rare **Glory of the Age**. May **God the Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Deus Optum. Max." — an adaptation of the Roman title for Jupiter (*Iuppiter Optimus Maximus*), commonly used in formal Latin dedications to refer to the Christian God.</note> preserve you in health and prosperity, and keep you with us as long as possible. Again, farewell.

->BOLOGNA, CITY OF STUDIES <note>original: "BONONIAE STUDIORUM" — Bologna was famous for its ancient university, the oldest in the Western world.</note>, OCTOBER 8, 1682.<-

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Jean-Baptiste Colbert, following the prose address with a celebratory sonnet. It shifts from formal supplication to a poetic tribute to Colbert's influence and his relationship with King Louis XIV.</meta>

# ON THE EXPLANATION
## OF THE PRESENT MOST OBSCURE ENIGMA
### That the first and most effective motive be
### *Reflection, no less than upon the Great Genius and Sublime Talents*
### *of HIS EXCELLENCY,*
### *To the SUN, the emblem of His Majesty.* <note>original: "Al SOLE, impresa di S. M." — A reference to the "Sun King" Louis XIV, whom Colbert served as a primary minister.</note>

The penetrated secrets of the dark Enigma,
It is right that I consecrate in vow to that COLBERT
To whose vast intellect, sovereign inspirations
Know how to reveal that which lay most hidden.

Without his counsels, today even the wisest,
Work in vain within the Royal councils;
And only from his predictions, never in vain,
Does every great Enterprise take its motion.

With his intellect alone in such high design,
For the KING, who entrusts his whole self to him,
The Treasury <term>Treasury: "Erario," referring to the state finances and public wealth of France under Colbert’s management.</term> grows, and the Kingdom expands.

Whence now, in such dark excess, he may well
Like Prometheus <note>In Greek mythology, Prometheus stole fire from the gods to give to humanity; here, Malvasia compares Colbert to a bringer of divine light or knowledge.</note> obtain light for my genius,
Since he, more than any other, is close to our SUN. <note>Malvasia suggests that because Colbert is so close to the "Sun King," he can reflect that royal light to help solve the mystery of the "Enigma."</note>

->Your most Devoted and Obliged Servant<-
->Carlo Cesare Malvasia.<-

<vocab>Enigma: "Enimma," referring to the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription. Colbert: Jean-Baptiste Colbert, the French statesman. King: Louis XIV. Treasury: "Erario." Prometheus: the bringer of fire/light. Sun: "Sole," the King's emblem. Carlo Cesare Malvasia: the author and Bolognese nobleman.</vocab>

<summary>In this dedicatory sonnet, Malvasia praises Jean-Baptiste Colbert’s vast intellect and political influence, arguing that a statesman so close to the "Sun King" is the perfect patron to illuminate the secrets of an obscure enigma.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, sonnet, Jean-Baptiste Colbert, Louis XIV, Sun King, statecraft, Enigma, Aelia Laelia Crispis, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the record of honors bestowed upon Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia, specifically focusing on a gift from King Louis XIV of France. The previous context likely involved Malvasia’s diplomatic or scholarly merits which caught the French King's attention.</meta>

# LOUIS THE GREAT, 14TH KING OF THE FRENCH

### whose emblem is the SUN

->after the first was stolen on the journey, he sends a second<-
->precious Image of Himself—a gift for outstanding Virtue—<-
->to Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia,<-
->who received it in the month of May.<-

->Imagine the Sun rising from Gemini <note>The zodiac sign Gemini, "The Twins," which the sun enters in late May.</note> toward the Tropic,<-
->with the Inscription:<-

## That it might shine more brightly.

->By Doctor and Abbot Giuseppe Orsoni.<-

If hostile fate snatches away the first *KING’S Gift*,
O *CARLO*, and the great honor won through your toil:

*The KING himself becomes a second "first" to you through these setbacks,*
*And turns into profit what were previously losses;*

*For he doubles the Gift, while the Sun, acting like a Sun to the Sun,*
*Marks a twin day within a single Sun.* <note>A poetic play on Louis XIV, the "Sun King," whose second gift makes the "Sun" appear twice.</note>

*Truly, the Sun never shines more brightly in the high*
*Heavens, without first passing through the days of the Twins.* <note>original: "Geminos... dies." A pun referencing both the zodiac sign Gemini and the "twin" (double) gifts sent to Malvasia.</note>

*Thus did the SUN, LOUIS, wish to fulfill this Sign of double*
*honor for you, THAT IT MIGHT SHINE MORE BRIGHTLY.*

---

->By the same author.<-

The one who snatched the *KING’S* first *Gift* from you, *CARLO*,
Was no thief, but the creator of a charming profit:

*He sent that pledge of increasing honor to earn interest,*
*Which you would seek in vain if it came quickly; it is better to receive it late.* <note>The poet wittily suggests that the theft of the first portrait was actually a "loan" that resulted in more honor when the King sent a replacement.</note>

<summary>This page contains two Latin poems by Giuseppe Orsoni celebrating King Louis XIV's gift of a second portrait to Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia after the first was stolen in transit. The poems use solar and zodiacal metaphors—specifically the transition of the sun into Gemini—to frame the replacement as a "doubling" of the King's favor.</summary>
<keywords>Louis XIV, Carlo Cesare Malvasia, Giuseppe Orsoni, Sun King, Gemini, royal gift, portrait, occasional poetry, 17th century, panegyric</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of laudatory poems celebrating the second portrait sent by King Louis XIV to Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia. The previous page established that the first gift was stolen in transit, leading the King to send an even more magnificent replacement. This page contains three short Latin epigrams reflecting on the themes of loss, divine providence, and the artistry of Bologna.</meta>

Why do you complain of the dealings regarding a lost gift,
Which, had you not lost it, you would then have lost even more?

If it is delayed, it is not taken away; he delayed that which
He took—rather, in taking it away, he himself added more:

He turned the loss into a profit, and taught LOUIS
To wish to give more, and you to have deserved no less.

---

->By Carlo Antonio Busio.<-

We rejoice for you, **CAESAR** <note>Carlo Cesare Malvasia.</note>, in the grand
Second gifts of **LOUIS**, returned by his generous hand.

These show that the High KING did not allow his
First Image to be stolen, nor could he endure it.

And while the "GOD-GIVEN" <note>original: "DEO-DATUS." A reference to Louis XIV’s name, Louis-Dieudonné (Louis the God-given), punning on the idea that what God gives, he does not usually need to "re-give," yet the King chose to do so.</note> does not send back stolen goods,
He, being skillful, wished those things he gave to be given twice.

This noble Portrait—in Countenance, Gold, Diamond, and Colors—
Captures Men, Gods, and Kingdoms by its Majesty.

Hence, with you as the author, **BOLOGNA** <note>original: "Felsina," the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna, often used in poetic and academic contexts.</note> the **PAINTER** of Kings
Shall also know, by this example, how to paint **SOULS** from life.

---

->By Doctor Francesco Ferrari.<-

The Royal Face which shines with many a diamond,
Restores and doubles the radiance of the stone.

This, **LOUIS**, is yours: he who brings gifts of light to you,
Receives gifts of light back with much interest.

<summary>A collection of three Latin poems praising King Louis XIV for replacing a stolen gift to Carlo Cesare Malvasia with a more magnificent one, framing the theft as a fortunate event that allowed the King's generosity to shine.</summary>

### ON THE SAME SUBJECT
->*By Signor Benedetto Giuseppe Balbi.*<-

To a noble Prometheus <note>A reference to Count Malvasia, who is compared to the mythological figure for bringing the "fire" of artistic life to Bologna through his writings.</note>, who delighted in giving
Soul to the efforts of **BOLOGNESE** <note>original: "FELSINEI," referring to Felsina, the ancient name for Bologna.</note> brushes,
The French **SUN** <note>King Louis XIV.</note> brought as a gift
A famous ray of his within a gem-studded image.

When Envy, seeing in this the courteous virtue
Of **AUGUSTUS** <note>The King is compared to the Roman Emperor Augustus, a legendary patron of the arts.</note> returning to this age,
Cried for help from the most unworthy and wicked
Fury of the Abyss, and seized the noble gift.

But she stole it in vain, leading to a generous excess,
For by renewing the rewards with an open arm,
The King steals the memories of the first theft from himself.

You were deceived, O wicked and inexperienced thief;
The theft was foolish, for there remained behind
The great heart in **LOUIS**, and the merit in **Carlo**.

---

->*By Signor Doctor Giovanni Antonio Rubini.*<-

Could then a barbarian hand snatch away
The likeness of a **KING**, who rules over souls?
The **SUN** of the Gauls, at the moment he extended to you
His shining orbits in a golden sphere?

Did not the lightning of the Divine Idea
Restrain that impetuous and fierce hand?
Nor did the proud Eagle <note>Often a symbol of imperial power or divine justice; here perhaps suggesting that the King’s own majesty should have protected the gift.</note> hasten to amend
The wicked deed of the new Prometheus?

No; for human outrages did not snatch it away:
Rather, it was the Sun's own enviable shame,
For he wanted the portrait as a companion on his travels.

But why? The high horizon hoped for it in vain;
Since he returns to you amidst the gold of double rays,
Like a new Castor <note>One of the mythical Gemini twins; his "return" symbolizes the arrival of the second, even brighter portrait.</note> to brighten your brow.

PER

### FOR THE SAID PORTRAIT SENT AS A GIFT TO THE AUTHOR OF THE PAINTERLY FELSINA. <note>original: "Felsina Pittrice," Malvasia's famous biographical work on Bolognese painters.</note>

->By Signor Senator Vincenzo Maria Marescalchi.<-

To those to whom **BOLOGNA** <note>original: "FELSINA," the ancient Etruscan name for the city.</note> gave immortal life
With the light of brushes and colors,
You describe, O Sir, their lives, and their wings
You gild with a golden pen for their Fame.

Therefore, to the Sacred and Royal Majesty
Of **LOUIS** <note>King Louis XIV of France.</note> you consecrate your labors,
And from him you have the **IMAGE**, to which no other equal
Work did Royal Painters ever sweat to produce.

Of the golden **LILY** <note>The fleur-de-lis, symbol of the French Monarchy.</note> here Nature and Art
Compose the Idea, which has the adornments of Adamant,
And shares a doubled Treasure with you.

Ah, for you are **CAESAR** <note>A play on Malvasia's middle name, Cesare.</note> in name, and **AUGUSTUS** in merit,
If, united to crown your papers,
The Flower of Kings <note>The King of France.</note> comes with the King of Gems <note>The diamond.</note>.

---

->By Signor Canon Ulisse Gozzadini.<-

Your learned Daughter <note>A metaphor for Malvasia's book, *Felsina Pittrice*.</note> now ignores,
Carlo, your speech, which invites her to modesty;
Since she fled from her father's walls,
She goes boldly to procure new lovers.

She tries every art of pleasing, nor does she care
For that blush which your Virtue points out to her,
And she already obscures the merits of a hundred Beauties
Now that she is welcomed by the **FRENCH JOVE**. <note>King Louis XIV.</note>

To withdraw her from the applause, from which you shy away,
You tried in vain for a long season the same trials
By which the Argive King <note>King Acrisius, who locked his daughter Danae in a bronze tower to keep her from suitors.</note> enclosed **DANAE**.

But to what end, if with a new appearance
His Great **JOVE** would have descended, so as not to be deprived of her,
In a tempest of gems. <note>Referring to the myth where Zeus/Jove reached Danae by turning into a shower of gold; here, the King reaches the author with a gift of jewels.</note>

->*By Signor Carlo Antonio Bedori.*<-

Taken from the Heaven of the **LILIES**, <note>original: "Gigli." The fleur-de-lis, the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note> the August Face
Decks with flowers the banks of the **ITALIAN RHINE**, <note>original: "Italico Ren." Refers to the Reno river which flows through Bologna, Malvasia's home.</note>
And though enclosed within a narrow field, <note>The "narrow field" refers to the small physical dimensions of the portrait or medal.</note>
It honors its triumphs with the hearts of at least a thousand souls.

Every eye being burdened with wonder,
It gives strength to triumphal Arches for the exalted Image,
And adores the trophies of every ancient century
Summarized within a single Circle.

Here the **ARABIAN GEM** <note>original: "Arabica Gemma." A poetic term for the diamond, as the East was historically considered the source of precious stones.</note> confuses its rays;
For Virtue, in a more noble reflection,
Diffuses flashes of light from the Royal Countenance:

Wherefore every heart, at such luminous excess,
If the one treasure hides the other greater one,
Consecrates itself to the greater Treasure. <note>The poet suggests the King's inner virtue is a "greater treasure" than the physical diamonds surrounding the portrait.</note>

---

->*By Signor Alessandro Gargieria.*<-

To gemstone the noble labors <note>original: "alti sudori," literally "high sweats," referring to the intense effort Malvasia put into writing his history of art.</note> of Carlo,
With that Hand, which elsewhere brandishes the sword,
The **FRENCH KING** <note>original: "FRANCO RE." Louis XIV.</note> upon the **BOLOGNESE** <note>original: "Felfinee." Felsina was the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna.</note> shores
Scatters ample Treasures, gathered in Silver.

A single Ray, a single Flash of those Splendors,
Which the great Royal Gift, through deep paths,
Infuses into the breasts of others more than in their eyes,
Enlivens the hearts of the whole Universe.

No spirit is found, nor is there any thought,
That is not—without having any envious trouble—
Both satisfied and proud of Carlo's fortune.

Thus, while it happens that **ENVY** <note>original: "Invidia." Personified Envy was the traditional enemy of the successful artist or scholar.</note> dies,
A tiny World <note>The portrait.</note> animates an entire World,
And the Gem of one man is a Joy to all.

<meta>This page continues the series of encomiums—poems of high praise—dedicated to Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia in celebration of the diamond-encrusted portrait he received from King Louis XIV. This page contains two sonnets: one by Count Ercole Agostino Berò and another by Count Angelo Antonio Sacchi.</meta>

->*By Signor Count Ercole Agostino Berò.*<-

While circled by jewels and brilliant gold
I gaze upon your Portrait, O great Monarch, <note>Referring to King Louis XIV of France.</note>
It seems to me that in that gemmed ring
Your Royal Countenance shines like a Deity.

I seem to see expressed in diamond
Hercules, who reigns in an orb of sapphire,
And if I turn my gaze to the rays you scatter,
You seem like Apollo, who gilds a starry sky. <note>Apollo was the Greek and Roman god of the sun, often used as an allegory for Louis XIV, the "Sun King."</note>

If I saw the warrior’s spear in your hand,
I would acclaim you as Mars in sonorous style, <note>Mars was the Roman god of war.</note>
Who triumphs over the stars within the sphere.

But if you give yourself as a Treasure,
Ah, you are Jove, who in true form
Descends into the lap of Virtue in golden clouds. <note>original: "Giove." An allusion to the myth of Jupiter (Jove) descending to Danae as a shower of gold; here, the King’s gold and gems descend upon Malvasia as a reward for his "Virtue" (his scholarly work).</note>

---

->*By Signor Count Angelo Antonio Sacchi.*<-

The August hand, which holds empire over the **LILIES**, <note>original: "Gigli." The fleur-de-lis, the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note>
Grants his own Face as a reward for the labors of others.
Of hard enamel is that proud Countenance,
And fine diamonds have welcomed it into their lap.

To express the constancy of a Warrior Hero
The craftsman chose that strong matter; <note>Refers to the durability of the enamel and diamonds used to create the portrait.</note>
And to emulate the faith of a sincere heart
He gathered the flower of the shining gems.

But an envious veil hides the internal merits,
Nor can the art of **APELLES** depict <note>Apelles of Kos was the most renowned painter of ancient Greece, famous for his portraits of Alexander the Great.</note>
The strength and zeal of the Magnanimous **KING**.

For if such beautiful virtues could be revealed,
Heaven would have placed this Royal Gift
Among its own images, girded with stars. <note>The poet suggests the portrait is so divine it should be a constellation in the heavens.</note>

<meta>This page continues the series of encomiums—poems of high praise—commemorating the gift of a diamond-encrusted portrait sent by King Louis XIV to the Bolognese art historian Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia. These sonnets shift the focus toward the artistic challenge of depicting such a monarch and the intellectual feat of interpreting royal symbolism.</meta>

# TO THE PAINTER IN THE ACT OF MAKING THE SAID PORTRAIT.
->By Signor Doctor Giovanni Turchi.<-

Painter, you who have already painted so many Heroes,
Yet despair of portraying the great **LOUIS**, <note>King Louis XIV of France (reigned 1643–1715).</note>
Because you do not possess colors so vivid and true
To show all his distinct merits:

Let your brushes be dipped in the blood
That his Warriors shed for the Faith; <note>A reference to Louis XIV’s military campaigns and his role as "Most Christian King," particularly his suppression of Protestantism.</note>
Then take from Glory its fierce lights,
And the shadows from the shadows of his extinguished enemies.

Take gray and green colors from the Sorrow
Of the **Heresy** that moved strife against him: <note>original: "Eresia." Likely refers to the Huguenots; green was sometimes associated with the "bitterness" of heresy in Catholic iconography.</note>
Let his own strength give strength to the work, I desire.

Form the Background so that it bears the vestiges of Honor;
Paint Love and Majesty together on a single Throne;
Paint Valor, and you will have painted **LOUIS**.

# TO THE AUTHOR OF THE EXPLANATION OF THE PRESENT ENIGMA.
->By Signor Canonico Bernardino Tinti.<-

Let the scholarly stones of Memphis cease <note>Referring to Egyptian hieroglyphs in Memphis, Egypt, once considered the ultimate "unknown" or "encoded" language.</note>
To boast of their high study of unknown Ciphers,
Since now to new and more remote figures
A desperate understanding is given.

Let the sharp style no longer go beyond the Theban monster
That strikes the passers-by, <note>An allusion to the Sphinx of Thebes and her riddle, which killed those who could not solve it.</note>
For you, like a wise Oedipus, by interpreting,
Bring down the pride of these profound notes. <note>Oedipus was the Greek hero who famously solved the Sphinx's riddle. Here, Count Malvasia is compared to him for "solving" the complex symbolism of the King's gift.</note>

From this your worthy praises advance,
Which the Seine heard in **PAINTERLY FELSINA**, <note>original: "FELSINA PITTRICE." Felsina is the ancient name for Bologna. This refers to Malvasia's most famous book, *Felsina pittrice* (1678), an essential history of Bolognese painters. The Seine refers to Paris/the French Court.</note>
Now that you give light to these ingenious deceptions.

Everyone points to you as equal to the Great Pellaean, <note>original: "Magno Pelleo." Referring to Alexander the Great, who was born in Pella, Macedonia.</note>
If not greater; for while to untie the knots
He used the Sword, You used the Pen. <note>Refers to the legend of the Gordian Knot. Alexander the Great cut the impossibly tangled knot with his sword; the poet argues Malvasia solved a similarly complex intellectual "knot" with his writing.</note>

<sig>Hoe</sig>

<meta>This page contains the official ecclesiastical and legal approvals (approbationes) required for the publication of Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia's work. These documents serve as a formal "clearance," confirming that the text contains nothing contrary to the Catholic faith or public morality, while also offering high praise for the author's intellectual rigor. The image shows some bleed-through from the reverse side, common in 17th-century printed works.</meta>

# APPROVALS.

I have more than once inspected and admired this elaborate work by the Most Illustrious Lord Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia of Bologna, which is titled **AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS**, etc. Admiring the weight of such sharp insight, I wished to survey it again and again from every angle. I have seen that the most learned Author (as far as I was able to judge) has not produced something foreign or borrowed, but has instead extracted a sense truly innate to the letters and consistent with the original sepulchral Epitaph. <note>The "Aelia Laelia Crispis" is a famous, real-world cryptic inscription found in Bologna that baffled scholars for centuries with its contradictory statements.</note> He has reached the very essence of the Inscription’s meaning, confirmed by the weight of so much erudition. Since nothing has emerged from these many words that is discordant with the Catholic faith or the integrity of morals, or anything unseemly, I therefore judge it entirely worthy of the light of publication, and indeed, that it should be sent to the press as soon as possible (if it so pleases those whom it concerns).

---

I, **Lord Charles Gorran**, <note>original: "D. Carolus Gorranus."</note> Penitentiary of the Metropolitan Church of Bologna, on behalf of the Most Eminent and Reverend **Lords Cardinal Boncompagno**, <note>Girolamo Boncompagni (1622–1684), the Archbishop and Prince of Bologna.</note> Archbishop and Prince, etc.

---

With a sword borrowed from Astraea, <note>The Greek goddess of Justice, whose sword symbolizes the power to cut through confusion to find the truth.</note> the Gordian knot <note>A legendary metaphor for an intricate, unsolvable problem; to "cut the Gordian knot" is to solve a complex riddle with a bold stroke.</note> with which the sepulchral Epitaph of **AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS**—placed by **LUCIUS AGATONE PRISCIUS** <note>The name traditionally attributed to the creator of the mysterious stone inscription.</note>—was bound, has been loosened by this legal exposition by the Most Illustrious Lord Count Carlo Cesare Malvasia, the sole guardian of the laws. I have read this work with great delight of the soul, and I have wonderfully admired **AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS NOT BORN RISING AGAIN**, <note>original: "Aeliam Laeliam Crispidem non natam resurgentem." A reference to the paradoxical nature of the riddle, which suggests the subject was never born yet exists.</note> contemplating her as she is adorned with every kind of erudition. I have found nothing in it contrary to Piety, the Christian Religion, or good morals. Wherefore, this book is declared worthy of both the press and of Cedar <note>original: "praelo et Cedro dignum." A Latin idiom meaning a work is worthy of being preserved for eternity, as cedar oil was used in ancient times to protect precious scrolls from decay.</note> by your Reverend Paternity’s witness,

->Peter<- <note>Likely the signature of a secondary censor or licensing official.</note>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal legal and religious permissions (the Imprimatur) to the author's direct address to the reader. It serves as a combination of a preface and an "errata" list, where the author and printer apologize for typographical errors and clarify technical choices made during the production of the book in Bologna while the author was away in Rome.</meta>

# DEAR READER.

Since the Author, on account of his annual absence from his *Homeland* <note>original: "Patria"; here referring to Bologna.</note> during the winter season, was staying in *Rome* while this present little work was being printed in *Bologna*, he wishes and earnestly requests that you know that, among other inscriptions sent from there to the Printer to be added, the one seen at the bottom of page 164 under Chapter IX should have been inserted into Chapter X. This was intended to prove more strongly that the diphthong Æ is not infrequently found carved joined together in this way in *Ancient Inscribed Marbles*, which is the point undertaken for proof in that chapter.

Furthermore, the Printer <note>original: "Chalcographus," literally a copper-engraver or printer using metal type.</note> attributes the blame for the inconsistency and variation of the vowel **U** in place of the consonant **V**—no less a departure from the modern custom among us Italians, who have hitherto adhered most scrupulously to those same Ancient Inscriptions—to a shortage of lead characters of that kind, and not without reason.

Moreover, the Printer himself confesses and asks pardon for certain other things, for the most part slight, which occasionally escaped him: such as on page 15, *immedia* for *immediatè* [immediately]; 21, *qum* for *quum* [when]; 27, *divisio literis* for *divisis literis* [with divided letters]; 39, *necquiquam* for *nequicquam* [in vain]; 40, *Rethoricae* for *Rhetoricae* [Rhetoric]; 61, *mihi* for *mi* [to me]; 62, *1550* for *1150*; *LVCCIA* for *TVLLIA*; *SIGNORIS* for *SYNORIS*; *difficilimorum* for *difficillimorum* [most difficult]; 66, *seu ut adhuc* for *seu quum adhuc* [or when still]; 68, *sub ascia dedicati* for *sub ascia dedicabantur* [they were dedicated under the adze]; 81, *ex Smetii* for *Smetii* [of Smetius]; 85, *ex antiquo* for *antiquo* [ancient]; 103, *ibid.* for *idib.* [the Ides]; 104, *ycones* for *icones* [images]; 105, *Sphingorione* for *Sphingocorione*; 119, *porò* for *porrò* [furthermore]; 127, *Latus thesauris* for *Latum theatris* [Wide in theaters]; 142, *Mevius* for *Memius*; 161, *Aringhio* for *Aringo*; 165, *Panvinus* for *Panvinius*; 166, *CXORINTHO* for *CORINTHO* [Corinth]. Often *musaeo* for *museo* [museum], and other similar instances. The Author likewise asks pardon for the more serious errors with which the whole Work teems; for, being a pure and simple Interpreter of the Laws, <term>stepping onto the roof with unwashed feet</term> <note>original Greek: "Ἀνίπτοις ποσὶν ἀναβαίνων ἐπὶ τὸ σέγος." This is a classical idiom meaning to take on a specialized or sacred task without proper preparation or being a novice in the field.</note> he has now for the first time given his name to this elegant and learned study of Inscribed Stones.

<summary>This preface contains the author's apologies for typographical errors and a misplaced inscription, explaining that he was in Rome while the book was printed in Bologna. It also includes a list of errata and a humble admission that the author is a lawyer by trade, making his first foray into the study of ancient inscriptions.</summary>
<keywords>errata, typography, Bologna, Rome, epigraphy, Latin inscriptions, diphthong, printing errors, Carlo Cesare Malvasia</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the printer's apologies for typographical errors and a note regarding the author's absence from Bologna during the printing process. This page marks the formal beginning of the main text, opening with a philosophical justification for why ancient knowledge was often written in a cryptic or hidden manner.</meta>

<header>Rising again, unborn, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &amp;c." This likely refers to a motto or the central enigma of the work.</note></header>
<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: Neither</meta>

# Rising again, unborn, etc.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter 'A'. The letter is set against a landscape background featuring a sun or moon in the upper right, a small figure seated at the base of the letter, and various plants and trees. The letter itself is stylized with architectural and floral elements.</image-desc>AMONG THE ANCIENTS, it was a solemn custom to hand down the Sciences and Arts—which they had developed through tireless late-night study <note>original: "lucubrationibus," literally work performed by lamp-light.</note> and deemed worthy of the public record—shrouded in such darkness that they could not be understood by students unless much "oil" was consumed <note>A common classical metaphor meaning "unless much effort and time were spent in study," referring to the oil in a scholar's lamp.</note>. Thus did Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a body of secret wisdom (Hermeticism) supposed to have originated in Egypt.</note>, thus did Plato, thus did Aristotle, and others of that kind, who rightly followed the examples of the most ancient Arabs, Egyptians, and Hebrews. For these men decided that the sacred secrets of Wisdom should not be prostituted to all like rations distributed to soldiers or trinkets thrown to the common people, but should be reserved for more eminent men and more sublime minds, never to be revealed to the profane rabble.

For this reason, we read in Cicero (in the first book of *On Divination* and the fifth book of *On the Ends of Good and Evil*) that among the Persians, no one was ever elevated to Imperial rule who had not first been steeped in the abstruse and hidden disciplines of the Magi. We also find that when choosing Kings, the Egyptians preferred either Priests—who naturally cared for the cultivation of secrets—over warriors, or they joined the warriors themselves (once promoted to that highest peak of power) with those same Priests to be instructed in these hidden mysteries. Plutarch left this truth written in greater detail, most wonderfully proving it in his work *On Isis and Osiris*, as translated by Xylander:

*Furthermore, Kings were chosen either from the Priests or from the warriors; since the former class was held in honor and authority for its virtue, and the latter for its Wisdom. However, he who was made King from the warriors immediately gave himself to the Priests and became a participant in philosophy. This philosophy hides most things in fables, using stories that contain obscure signs and arguments for the truth. This they themselves clearly declare by commonly placing Sphinxes <note>Ancient Greek and Egyptian mythological creatures; here they represent the guardian of riddles and hidden knowledge.</note> before their temples, by which they hint that their doctrine of sacred matters consists of a complex Wisdom lying hidden under veils.*

<vocab>Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Aristotle, Cicero, Plutarch, Egyptians, Magi, Wisdom, Priests, Sphinxes</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces the concept of esotericism, arguing that ancient civilizations and philosophers intentionally hid their most profound knowledge behind symbols and myths to ensure it was only accessible to the worthy.</summary>
<keywords>esotericism, ancient wisdom, Egyptology, philosophy, secret knowledge, Sphinx, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the idea that ancient scholars like Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, and Aristotle purposefully hid their wisdom in "darkness" to ensure it was only accessible to the dedicated and worthy, rather than the "profane rabble." This page continues that argument by applying it to the Roman tradition.</meta>

<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>
<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: Neither</meta>

Nor indeed did such a practice seem unique only to foreign nations; rather, it is well known that the **QUIRITES** <note>A formal term for Roman citizens in their civil capacity.</note> publicly followed the same custom. We see this especially when they were most eagerly seeking Empire: they prepared a path for themselves toward the anxiously desired dominion of the whole world no less through the office of the **PRIESTS** than through the titles of **PRINCES OF YOUTH** and of **CAESARS**. 

Ancient coins present this everywhere to our eyes. They show Nero while still a boy as **PRIEST ADOPTED INTO ALL THE COLLEGES BEYOND THE USUAL NUMBER** <note>original: "SACERD. COOP. IN. OMN. CONL. SVP. NVM." This refers to an honorary appointment to Rome's four main priestly colleges.</note>; or they show him and many others raised to such heights, likewise decorated with the addition of **HIGHEST PONTIFF** <note>original: "PONTIF. MAX." or Pontifex Maximus, the chief high priest of the College of Pontiffs.</note>. Many others are displayed in a toga reaching down to their ankles, with heads veiled, making sacrifice at an altar. This is seen alongside the tripod, the libation bowl <note>term: <term>patera</term> (a shallow ceramic or metal libation bowl used in rituals)</note>, the ladle <note>term: <term>simpulum</term> (a small vessel or ladle used by priests to taste wine before a sacrifice)</note>, the augur's staff <note>term: <term>lituus</term> (a curved wand used by Roman augurs to mark out a ritual space in the sky)</note>, and other instruments—both priestly and augural—repeated thousands of times on the reverse side of the coins. 

For it was fitting that, through the blending of the roles of Priesthood and Empire, the ingeniously veiled manifestation of our mind should also be decorated and extolled. This practice separates these venerable words from the messy speech of the unlearned common people; it rouses greater attention in everyone, lifts the mind higher, and sharpens the intellect. Furthermore, it offers such delight that even the Sages themselves and Kings were drawn to pursue it diligently, gaining much grace and honor from both the proposing and the solving of riddles <note>original: "gryphorum," from the Greek 'griphos,' meaning a fishing net or a complex riddle.</note>, as Plutarch notes in the *Banquet of the Seven Sages* and Lycophron among the Illustrious.

To this end properly belong all those things which keep something hidden—not by any organ of the voice or sound of words, but by the simple traces of gestures, figures, or symbols—to be revealed only to the wise. Of countless examples, let these alone suffice: specifically the motions or gestures of the hands, about which our **Accursius** <note>A famous 13th-century Italian jurist known for his "Great Gloss" on Roman law.</note> writes in the second law, section 'exactis' concerning verbal obligations. **Forcatulus** <note>Etienne Forcadel, a 16th-century French jurist.</note> does not doubt in his *Penetralia Juris* that Roman ingenuity was truly tested by these gestures during the handing down of the **Law of the Twelve Tables** <note>The earliest foundation of Roman law, traditionally dated to 451–450 BC.</note> from the Greeks—regardless of how much **Zasius** and **Budaeus** <note>Ulrich Zasius and Guillaume Budé, two prominent Renaissance legal humanists who often mocked the earlier medieval legal commentators for their historical inaccuracies.</note> might mock this little story told by the commentator.

<summary>The author argues that the Romans used religious titles, priestly symbols on coins, and non-verbal gestures to hide deeper truths, suggesting that the "veiled" communication used by sages was essential to Roman power and law.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Empire, numismatics, priesthood, Pontifex Maximus, enigmas, legal history, Accursius, Twelve Tables, symbolism</keywords>

# She who is not born rises again, etc.
<sig>A 2</sig>

...[rejecting] the same practice as if it were an old wives’ tale. We might consider the dove of Taurosthenes, which Aelian records in his ninth book: he decorated it with the purple of victory and sent it back to the offspring it had left behind, flying all the way from Pisa to Aegina. Or there is the frog, the mouse, the bird, and the five arrows sent as gifts to Darius; Gobryas interpreted these for the King, who was puffed up with the pride of a haughty mind <note>Darius interpreted these gifts as a sign of submission, but Gobryas correctly read them as a warning: unless the Persians could fly like birds or hide in the earth like mice, they would be destroyed by the arrows.</note>. Finally, there are the well-known taller poppies cut down with a long rod by King Tarquin <note>King Tarquin the Proud signaled to his son to execute the leading men of Gabii by silently striking the tallest poppies in his garden with a stick.</note>. These examples are perhaps too well-worn and daily encountered—much like the secret names used by the **Chemists** <note>i.e., Alchemists</note>, the technical terms of the **Philosophers**, the shorthand notes of our **Lawyers**, the abbreviations of **Physicians**, the symbols of **Astronomers**, and six hundred other things of that kind.

Maximus Planudes provides brilliant evidence for these sayings in his *Life of Aesop*—unless you prefer Aesop himself within Planudes' work; as do Plutarch in his *Life of Alexander*, Hermippus in the writings of Athenaeus, and Aulus Gellius in his *Attic Nights*. Indeed, by their testimony, we perceive that the Ancients competed among themselves with **riddles** <term>gryphi</term> <note>From the Greek word for a fishing net; these were intricate intellectual puzzles meant to "entangle" the opponent.</note> and problems, especially at festive banquets. From these, the name "symposiac" or "convivial enigmas" was borrowed (the Greeks call them "cup-questions" <note>original Greek: ζητήματα κυλίκεια (zetemata kylikeia). These were intellectual puzzles proposed while drinking.</note>). 

Because of this, we recognize that King Amasis of Egypt once sparred with the King of the Ethiopians; Lyserus (or Labynetus) with Nectanebus, likewise an Egyptian; the aforementioned Aesop with the Seven Sages of Greece; and the **Great Macedonian** <note>Alexander the Great</note> with ten **Gymnosophists** <note>term: <term>Gymnosophists</term> (literally "naked wise men," a group of ancient Indian philosophers known for their asceticism and riddling wisdom)</note>, who were condemned to death if they did not solve his puzzles. Finally, the Ancient Sages agree on this and seal it with their own example: among the sophists, **Pythagoras** used **Symbols**, which Athenaeus testified were actually enigmas in the final chapter of the tenth book of the *Deipnosophistae* <note>The "Banquet of the Learned."</note>. **Hippocrates** wrote enigmatically in his work *On Nutriment*; **Heraclitus** was praised for this by **Aristotle** in the sixth book of *On the Universe* and the eighth book of the *Ethics*; **Plato** did the same in his second letter to Dionysius; and finally **Varro**, **P. Nigidius**, and other Roman leaders did not hesitate to sometimes battle with those same Sophists, seeking the same palm of victory in peace that they had so often sought with arms...

### Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<sig>A</sig>

...having vigorously and successfully gathered [the riddles], they boasted that they had won the prize. Indeed, this custom grew so prevalent that certain men—and by no means from the common crowd—soon wasted away from constant sleeplessness and intense study, finally meeting a death that would release them <note>The author suggests that scholars literally died from the exhaustion of trying to solve ancient enigmas.</note>. Gyraldus writes of Philetas of Cos <note>A Greek poet and scholar rumored to have grown so thin from studying logic and riddles that he had to wear lead weights in his shoes to keep from blowing away.</note> in his *History of the Poets*, volume one; Lycophron in his *Alexandra* and Eustathius in his *Iliad* write of Calchas, the son of Thestor <note>A mythical seer who was destined to die when he met a prophet more skillful than himself.</note>; Pliny in Book 7, chapter 53, and both Laërtius and Fulgosius in Book 9, chapter 12, write of Diodorus Cronus <note>A philosopher who reportedly died of shame in the 4th century BCE because he could not immediately solve a riddle posed by Stilpo.</note>. Furthermore, Plutarch left a testament regarding Homer himself, and Justin Martyr, in his *Admonition to the Greeks*, wrote the same of Aristotle.

But why do we dwell upon profane matters and seek out the execrable rites of the Pagans <note>original: "Gentilitatis"</note> and empty arguments, when we can bring forward things that are worthy, just, and truly sacred? Indeed, it has always seemed right and fitting to us that one should not approach all sacred or divine matters—nor the sciences (which, as Ecclesiasticus <note>chapter 1</note> hints to us and St. James <note>Epistle, chapter 1</note> agrees, ought to savor of a certain divinity since they are from God)—with the same unrefined hands used for domestic chores. It seems utterly contrary to Nature, and indeed to God, that these things should be easily shared; such ease breeds contempt and hatred rather than the required veneration and esteem. For this reason, Divinity itself has mostly brought forth its meanings through a certain mist <note>original: "caligine"</note>; it set the Truth, though overshadowed by mysteries, against that thickest darkness in which the Demons once held the deceived Nations through the Oracles of Delphi, Dodona, and Hammon <note>The most famous prophetic sites of the ancient Greek and Egyptian worlds.</note>. Yet, after the birth of our Lord and Savior, these oracles were forced to be perpetually terrified, as Occo observes in his *Imperial Coins* concerning Augustus, following Nicephorus <note>Book 1, chapter 17</note>. I now consider those truly mystical predictions by which our truthful God—once speaking obscurely to the Fathers through the Prophets (as St. Paul says to the Hebrews)—has most recently, in these days of our Redemption, spoken to us through His Son. Although He spoke clearly and explicitly, as St. Augustine brilliantly notes in *The City of God* <note>Book 1, chapter 18</note>, He did not do so without the clothing of figures or parables, since He did not disdain to dwell among mortals and deliver the Gospel...

# Rising again without having been born, etc.
## 5

...divine <note>original: "evangelicâ"</note> preaching might show us the way to salvation. Thus with Him [Christ] leading the way, in every later commentator of Holy Scripture, that passage from Proverbs chapter 1 became known and established: *that the prudent man may understand a parable and its interpretation, the words of the Wise, and their enigmas* <note>Proverbs 1:6</note>. It is the same as what Ecclesiasticus chapter 39 instructs: namely, that *he will enter at the same time into the subtleties of parables, seek out the secrets of proverbs, and dwell in the hidden meanings of parables* <note>Ecclesiasticus 39:2-3. This book is part of the Biblical apocrypha or deuterocanon.</note>. Sacred History fully testifies to this custom, whether we present the archetype of Strength or of Wisdom in the Holy Pages; namely Samson on one hand, and Solomon on the other.

Of the former [Samson], that most famous Enigma exists, which Judges chapter 14 describes as being proposed to thirty men of Timnath: *namely, food out of the eater, and sweetness out of the strong* <note>Judges 14:14. Samson’s riddle refers to honey found in the carcass of a lion.</note>. Of the other [Solomon], there are those riddles, now unknown to us, which he most learnedly proposed to King Hiram and solved for the Queen of Sheba, as hinted in 1 Kings <note>The Latin text cites "3 Reg. cap. 10" because in the older Vulgate tradition, 1 and 2 Samuel were called 1 and 2 Kings, making our modern 1 Kings the "Third Book of Kings."</note> chapter 10, and reported by Josephus <note>Titus Flavius Josephus, a 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian.</note> in Book 8 of *Antiquities* and Book 1 of *Against Apion*.

I pass over here the most learned contest stirred up during a banquet by three of King Darius’s bodyguards: as to which thing of all was the strongest. Zerubbabel was the one who achieved victory after all the others. For he, while asserting that Truth surpasses Wine, the King, and Women, and should therefore be judged the strongest of all, would have walked adorned with a crown, a triumph, and a portion of the Kingdom, had he not refrained from such great gifts and honors in favor of finishing the Sacred Temple and restoring the city of Jerusalem <note>original: "Solymorum"</note>.

I set aside those things which were once proposed by Ptolemy Philadelphus, King of the Egyptians, to the Seventy Translators of Holy Scripture <note>The "Septuagint" scholars.</note> summoned from Jerusalem—to test their grasp of such great matters—as Aristeas and the historian Josephus have recorded. Nor do I recount the three common words which, during nocturnal feasts and overly joyful banquets, were written by an unknown hand, and by Daniel’s interpretation, announced the imminent destruction and plague upon Belshazzar, King of the Assyrians, that very night <note>Refers to the "writing on the wall" (Mene, Mene, Tekel, Upharsin) from Daniel 5.</note>.

Since these things are so, and the dignity of Enigmas has so greatly increased...

...[by public] preaching he might show us the path to salvation. For he, while asserting that Truth excels over Wine, the King, and Women—and therefore should be judged the strongest of all—would have proceeded decorated with a crown, a triumph, and a portion of the Kingdom, had he not refrained from such great gifts and honors for the sake of completing the Sacred Temple <note>The rebuilding of the Temple in Jerusalem.</note> and restoring the city of Jerusalem <note>original: "Solymorum"</note>.

Since these things are so, and the dignity of riddles has grown so much...

<meta>This page continues from page 4, where the author discussed biblical precedents for riddles. # Aelia Laelia Crispis.

It is well-established that the art of the Enigma has grown to such a degree that the most excellent minds are now devoted to it. Therefore, I should not be blamed if I linger longer than might seem proper on a subject that appears light, while I attempt, to the best of my ability, a new exposition of our local and most celebrated Riddle. Indeed, this very reason should commend my effort: namely, that the greatest minds of both our own and previous centuries have struck so fruitfully against this one Stone <term>Lapis: Latin for "stone," referring specifically to the marble slab in Bologna bearing the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription.</term> that it may rightly and deservedly be considered a whetstone for the intellect rather than a mere stumbling block to learning. And although no true "Oedipus" for our "Sphinx" <note>In Greek mythology, Oedipus was the only one capable of solving the riddle of the Sphinx; the author uses this as a metaphor for a scholar solving a difficult puzzle.</note> has yet appeared, why should we despair of a glory that has been granted to no one so far? Why should I be denied the risk that has been permitted to everyone else until now?

---

->*Why should I be envied, if I can<-
->acquire even a few things?<-
->Has not<-
->God placed all things in the open field?<-

---

But if I should labor in vain, or if I should achieve this with less praise than so many others, I will at least protect myself with the same words with which that eminent writer of Roman History <note>The author is quoting the historian Livy from the preface of his work *Ab Urbe Condita*.</note> encouraged himself: *And if in such a crowd of writers my fame should remain in obscurity, I will console myself with the nobility and greatness of those who will overshadow my name.* <note>original: "Et si in tanta Scriptorum turba mea fama in obscuro sit, nobilitate ac magnitudine eorum, qui nomini officient meo, me consolabor."</note>

In truth, either the love for the task I have undertaken deceives me, or the obvious truth of this oracle shone clearly before me from the very day that this Inscription, skillfully carved in capital letters, first presented itself to me. For it seemed to shine so brightly in my mind that I have not ceased to wonder more and more every day how a matter so clear could be wrapped in such tangles and labyrinths. It is a wonder that something which appears more splendid than the noonday light is considered more obscure than Platonic numbers. <term>Platonic numbers: a reference to notoriously difficult mathematical passages in Plato's writings, often used by Renaissance scholars to describe anything incomprehensibly complex.</term> I marveled, I say, that this Epitaph—implying nothing other than the memory of someone deceased, a true Sepulchral Inscription—is believed to be a record of supernatural effects, is judged to be a compendium of hidden powers, and...

<summary>The author introduces his study of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription, arguing that while many great scholars have failed to solve it, he feels a sudden clarity about its true meaning as a simple funeral epitaph.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's introductory justification for his interpretation of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle. ### Not born, Rising again, etc.
<meta>catchword: cona-</meta>

...and that an obvious and sincere meaning—far from being fetched with too deep a search from the Portico <term>Porticus: Referring to the Stoic school of philosophy, which met in the "Stoa Poikile" (Painted Porch) in Athens.</term>, from the Academy <term>Academia: The school of philosophy founded by Plato.</term>, or from the Lyceum <term>Lyceum: The school of philosophy founded by Aristotle.</term>; indeed, far removed from the Air, from Meteorological phenomena, and even from the seats of the Heavens—can be led forth by me immediately from the crossroads, from domestic matters, and from common occurrences. This alone was the reason why I did not fear to undertake this duty, even if at first glance it seemed so far removed from my usual professional pursuits.

That which causes me some fear in this dust <note>original: "in hoc pulvere"; a classical metaphor for the "arena" or "field" of scholarly competition and hard work.</note>—which I nonetheless enter so spiritedly—and which almost makes me pause, is this one thing which I immediately recognize and frankly confess: that I am certainly not endowed with that purity or elegance of speech that would befit this inquiry, which is at once serious and pleasant. For having been made a Public Lecturer <term>Lector publicus: An official university professor or lecturer, in this case at the University of Bologna.</term>, through the entire course of my life spent in the learning and teaching of the Law, I have utterly abandoned all clarity and beauty of speech (if I ever possessed any). For the entire nation of our "Respondents" <note>original: "Respondentium"; referring to the medieval and Renaissance jurists who wrote "Responsa" or legal opinions.</note> (as they are called) has always recalled and continues to recall us from the ancient brilliance and nobility of Latium <note>original: "Latii"; referring to the pure, classical Latin of the Roman heartland.</note>. We cannot help but drink deeply from them with our whole hearts, and follow them entirely, whenever we teach in the Schools, debate in circles, or contend in the Forum. 

Since, therefore, we cannot cast off the barbarism of speech—which is almost innate and already clinging to our bones—of the First Glossator Accursius <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263) was a celebrated Italian jurist whose "Great Gloss" became the standard authority for interpreting Roman Law.</note>, of Bartolus, the "Lamp of Law" <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357) was perhaps the most influential jurist of the Middle Ages.</note>, of Castrensis, Salicetus, Alexander, Socinus, and so many others; nor can we put on the polished eloquence of the most learned Alciatus <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), a jurist who combined law with humanistic literary elegance.</note>, Duarenus, Cujas, and a few others of that caliber; I must be forgiven (as I earnestly pray) if, in this legal exposition of the Enigma, I—a rough speaker—pour out not a few exotic terms, like Apuleius <note>Apuleius was a Roman writer known for his flowery, idiosyncratic, and non-classical style.</note>, and the language of the courtroom. This has been, for the most part, the unbridled license of legal experts <term>Iurifperiti: Jurists or practitioners of the law.</term>, which—whether they are interpreting, advising, debating, or deciding—everywhere constantly rages and runs wild, while the elegant and polished responses of the ancient Jurisconsults <term>Iurifconditi: The classical Roman legal authorities (like Ulpian or Papinian) known for their refined Latin style.</term> are neglected. From these ancients, since whatever of their doctrine...

<summary>The author explains that his interpretation of the riddle will be based on common, everyday life rather than abstract philosophy. He apologizes for his "barbarous" writing style, blaming his career as a legal scholar, which has steeped him in the technical, unpolished Latin of medieval jurists rather than the elegant prose of classical authors.</summary>

<keywords>Bologna, legal history, Latin style, Accursius, Bartolus de Saxoferrato, Alciatus, Aelia Laelia Crispis, jurisprudence, academic humility</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's justification for using legal reasoning to solve the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma. He transitions from discussing the limitations of legal language to his own retirement and the spiritual goals of his work.</meta>

### Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...strive, they do not cease to distance themselves entirely from common speech. For since it rings in their ears daily, as <term>Ulpian</term> <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), a celebrated Roman jurist whose writings formed a large part of the Digest, the foundational collection of Roman law.</note> proclaims in the law *si quid* in the Digest on the ademption of legacies: *it is better to embrace the meaning than the words* <note>original: "melius esse sensum, quàm verba complecti"</note>; and as the same author warns in the law *Natura* in the Digest on the prescription of words: *there are more affairs than there are words* <note>original: "Plura esse negotia, quàm vocabula"</note>, they therefore most constantly swear by and persist in the common axiom: *the Law is handed down in things, not in words* <note>original: "Legem rebus, non verbis traditam esse"</note>.

However, just as the Law is not without its own Enigmas—which <term>Juvenal</term> <note>A Roman poet known for his Satires, which often critiqued the complexities and hypocrisies of Roman life.</note> alluded to there:

> One who might loosen the knots of the Law and the enigmas of the Statutes; <note>original: "Qui Iuris nodos, et Legum Aenigmata ſolvat"</note>

so it does not in the least misbecome me, an interpreter of the Law, to loosen in whatever way the Gordian knot of our Epitaph, relying purely on the protection of the Laws rather than any artifice of sophistry—or indeed any scholarly ornament (as this subject might perhaps demand). Whether I receive from <term>Astrea</term> <note>The virgin goddess of Justice in Greek and Roman mythology, often associated with the constellation Virgo.</note> the balance with which to weigh it, or the sword with which to cut it through. Truly, after finally completing a course of nearly forty years in this legal arena (like a soldier released by an honorable discharge <note>original: "honesta missio"; the formal discharge given to a Roman soldier who had completed his service with credit.</note>), it pleases me to pass my leisure in this laudable way, or to play in a manner that is not useless. I mean to play, I say, with Inscribed Stones, which are kin and neighboring to our own pursuits, recounting the various and wonderful fates of the dead. These stones move me more, and more soothingly stir and uplift me, to draw the final line of my days in due turn and to conclude them rightly. May God grant that it happens according to my wish; and let this recent argument of an unaccustomed exercise profit me more toward contemplating the promised and hoped-for Immortality of the soul than toward acquiring any fame of an immortal name. And may this Monument of condemned <term>Paganism</term> <note>original: "Gentilitatis"; the author refers to the ancient, non-Christian origins of the enigma.</note> be for me a step toward the harbor of longed-for Blessedness; where, having laughed at so many triple entanglements of that Labyrinth <note>original: "Maeandri"; referring to the winding river Meander, a symbol of complex mazes or confusion.</note>, it may finally be granted to look face to face upon that One True and Triune Being, toward whom we now grope blindly, as if looking into a mirror in an enigma. <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12: "For now we see through a glass, darkly [in an enigma]; but then face to face."</note>

<meta>catchword: Quot (How many)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that legal principles prioritize substance over literal words and announces his intention to use his forty years of legal expertise to solve the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle as a spiritual exercise in his retirement.</summary>

# Chapter I.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring floral motifs and a central rose-like flower within a circular frame, surrounded by ornate scrollwork.</image-desc>

Since the weight and dignity of scholarly Questions are mostly assessed by the number and excellence of the Authors who have either thrown themselves into them or undertaken to treat them, I therefore believe that no subject should be preferred to this Aelian Inscription of ours. We understand that so many and such great talents, both of the present and the past century, have at least touched upon it incidentally, if not explained it specifically. Behold, therefore, how many in number and what kind in learning they are, who, by undertaking either to record or to explain it, have ascribed so much prestige to it. And first, the very first of all who seems to have preceded all others is

**MARIUS MICHAEL ANGELO** <note>Mario Michele Angelo (also known as Mariangelo Accursio), a 16th-century Italian humanist and critic known for his work on ancient texts.</note>, an Italian, a man born for all the flowers of eloquence, as Verani says of him in *The Sharp Eloquence of the Pantheon* <note>original: "Panth. argut. elocut."</note> page 553. He was then the only one from the most celebrated University of Padua (to whom the inscription had been sent for explanation by the most learned Academicians of Milan) who dared to explain it. He achieved great praise from his colleague Antonio Catharo, and through his help, from the Most Eminent **Cardinal Madruzzo** <note>Cristoforo Madruzzo (1512–1578), a powerful Prince-Bishop of Trent and a central figure in the Council of Trent.</note>, to whom that elegant little commentary was dedicated. Second came the learned and renowned

**RICHARD WHITE OF BASINGSTOKE** <note>Richard White (1539–1611) was an English jurist and historian who lived in exile in Europe due to his Catholic faith. "Basinstocchius" is the Latinized form of his birthplace.</note>, an Englishman, who brought a triple solution to this puzzle <note>original: "scrupi" – literally a small, sharp stone, used metaphorically for a difficulty or a source of anxiety/puzzle.</note>: The first to a most acute Philo-

to the philosopher Christopher Janson <note>original: "Philosopho Christophoro Iansoni"</note>, headmaster of the Winchester School <note>original: "Vicamensis Scholae apud Vintomiam summo moderatori"</note>, to whom it was dedicated; another to the outstanding Marco Mantua Benavides, three times Count and three times Knight, a Jurisconsult <note>A legal expert or advisor on the law.</note> of Padua, who was a public lecturer in that famous University for over sixty-two years and a most prolific commentator of twenty-eight volumes; and finally a third to the most learned Johannes Turrius, his own keenest rival in untying this same knot <note>original: "scirpo enodando." The phrase "seeking a knot in a bulrush" (in scirpo nodum quaerere) was a common Latin proverb for looking for difficulty where there is none, or here, solving a complex riddle.</note>. 

The same **JOHANNES TURRIUS** of Bruges, a man of sharp wit and an excellent Doctor of Laws (says Licetus), who, in the same manner that he had sent his own explanation to Vitus, received a similar one back from Vitus out of mutual courtesy, though differing in opinion. 

**REMIGIUS FLORENTINUS** <note>Remigio Nannini (1521–1581), a Dominican friar and scholar from Florence.</note>, of no obscure name, in his *Familiar Letters*.

**FRANCISCUS SCOTTUS** of Antwerp, a most diligent compiler of Enigmas.

The distinguished Lecturer in Pontifical and Imperial Law <note>original: "Iur. Pontif. & Caesar. Lector." This refers to Canon Law (the Pope's law) and Civil Law (the Emperor's law).</note>, **ZACCHARIAS PONTINUS**, in his exposition of this Inscription, which was sent to the most learned Licetus by Johannes Thuilius, the most illustrious Professor of Humanities in the famous University of Padua.

### Rising again without being born, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This refers to a common interpretation of the riddle as representing something that exists without having a traditional birth, such as a soul or a chemical element.</note>

B 2
<meta>catchword: Pur</meta>

**JEAN-GASPARD GEVAERTS** <note>original: "IOANNES GASPERIVS GEVARTIVS" (1593–1666), a famous Flemish philologist and friend of the painter Peter Paul Rubens.</note>, in his books titled *Selections* <note>original: "Electorum"</note>, asserts in choice words that he was **"Excellently trained in every kind of the most distinguished arts."**

**FORTUNIO LICETO** <note>original: "FORTVNIVS LICETVS" (1577–1657), a celebrated doctor and philosopher.</note>, Ordinary Professor of Natural Philosophy <note>original: "Physiologus Ordinarius"</note> at the same University of Padua, gathered together the opinions of nearly all the aforementioned writers (excepting Remigius and Reusner)—or rather, the opinions of everyone who had written on this matter. He presented these opinions most diligently in the public schools during the lectures following the Easter Holidays, examining them at length, refuting them most sharply, and finally publishing them in his first book of *Peripatetic Allegory on Generation, Friendship, and Privation* <note>original: "Allegoriae Peripateticae de Generatione, Amicitiâ, & Priuatione Libro"</note>. He did this so that he might more effectively establish his own opinion in his second book, having already set aside the ideas of these others. These men were followed later by:

**GEORG REMUS**, a most distinguished Jurist <note>original: "I. C. Clarissimus" (Iuris Consultus)</note>, who translated the riddle into verse as follows:

> *Neither man nor woman was Aelia Laelia Crispis,*
> *Not the offspring of Hermes and Venus, but Everything; furthermore,* <note>original: "Non Hermæ & Veneris proles" — a reference to a Hermaphrodite.</note>
> *Neither an old woman nor a wife, nor a young woman, nor unchaste (a marvel!),*
> *For she had been all things at once, and at one time: She by neither sword*
> *Was taken away, nor by hunger, nor poison: By all these*
> *Alas, she perished; neither in water nor in earth, nor among the stars does she lie*
> *Buried, but in whatever place, she is surely in all places;*
> *No mound, no pyramid, no crumbling tomb,*
> * (I tell the truth, I hate lies) but everything, and for her*
> *Someone built it, neither grieving nor rejoicing,*
> *Indeed, all things at once. Is this not a Gordian knot?* <note>A "Gordian knot" refers to an intricate and seemingly unsolvable problem.</note>

To which I would harmonize in our own mother tongue <note>Italian</note> as follows:

*Aelia Laelia Crispa was I called;*
*I was never Man, Woman, or Hermaphrodite,*
*Maid, Young, or Old, and I was never*
*Chaste, Modest, or a woman for hire,* <note>original: "Donna da partito." A phrase often implying a woman of lower virtue or a courtesan.</note>
