# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

<meta>catchword: IOAN-</meta>

*Through all things I was. Neither Iron, Hunger,*
*Nor Poison, though I felt them, dared to touch me.*
*In Sky, in Waters, and on Earth is not the site*
*Of my repose, yet in all of them I rested.*

*Lucius Agatho Priscius, neither Husband,*
*Nor Lover, nor Heir, and who thereafter*
*Neither rejoiced, nor grieved, nor wept for his fate;*

*This mass, or Pyramid, or what you will,*
*A tomb raised, and not raised in death,*
*Knows, and knows not for whom he set it: and shall we know?*
<note>This is an Italian poetic rendering of the famous Latin "Bologna Enigma." The riddle describes a mysterious entity (often interpreted as primordial matter, the soul, or a chemical element) that exists in contradictions.</note>

**MARCUS ZUERIUS BOXHORN** <note>original: "MARCVS ZVERIVS BOXHORNIVS" (1612–1653), a Dutch scholar and professor at Leiden known for his work on linguistics and history.</note>, of Bergen op Zoom, a most diligent pursuer of ancient memories, in his *Monuments of Illustrious Men and Eulogies*. A learned and ingeniously pious man.

**ANDRÉ DE NESMOND** <note>original: "ANDREAS NESMONDIVS" (1553–1616), a French jurist.</note>, Chief President in the Parliament of Bordeaux. Endowed with a rich and pleasant intellect.

**FRANS SWEERTS** <note>original: "FRANCISCVS SVERTIVS" (1567–1629), a Flemish historian and author.</note> of Antwerp, in his *Jocular-Serious Epitaphs*. Most learned and most religious.

**JEREMIAS DREXEL** <note>original: "HIEREMIAS DREXELLIVS" (1581–1638), a Jesuit writer of immensely popular devotional books.</note>, of the Reverend Fathers of the Society of Jesus, famous for so many and so brilliant spiritual works worthy of an eternal name. A man of great reputation.

**WILLIAM BAROLDON** <note>original: "VVILIELMVS BAROLDONVS"</note>, an Englishman, in his book *On the True and Genuine Interpretation of Holy Scripture through the Cabalistic Art* <note>The "Cabalistic Art" refers to a method of interpreting the Bible through mystical number and letter symbolism.</note>; a work of which the fame has reached me, but not yet a physical copy. He has served the Republic of Letters <note>The "Republic of Letters" was the long-distance intellectual community of late Renaissance and Enlightenment scholars.</note> most excellently.

**LAURENTIUS BEYERLINCK** <note>original: "LAVRENTIVS BEYERLINCK" (1578–1627), a Flemish encyclopedist.</note>, in his most elaborate *Theater of Human Life*. Gian Vittorio Rossi <note>original: "Ianus Nicius Erythraeus" (1577–1647), an Italian scholar famous for his biographies of contemporaries.</note>, the restorer of Ciceronian eloquence, names him in his *Pinacotheca* (Part 1) as one *so devoted to ancient inscriptions that he seemed almost to die while investigating and interpreting them.*

# Rising without being born, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens." This phrase is often associated with the phoenix or the soul, reflecting the paradoxical nature of the riddle's subject.</note>

To this most kind friend, let me finally add our former most sharp-sighted pupil, now truly a master above masters, the distinguished Doctor 

**PIETRO ERCOLE BELLOIO** <note>original: "PETRVM HERCVLEM BELLOIVM"</note>, in *Bologna Shown to Foreigners*, composed in most elegant verse according to custom, where he writes:

> *In the Tower of the Voltas, the Sphinx is Aelia Laelia Crispis:*
> *Foreign talents wander through foreign lands to foreign things,*
> *Here to be sharpened; yet thus far no Oedipus exists.*
> *Malvasia, who taught Law to my youth, etc.*
> <note>The "Tower of the Voltas" refers to the location of the inscription in Bologna. Oedipus is the mythological figure who solved the Sphinx's riddle; the author suggests no one has yet truly solved this one. Malvasia is the author of this book.</note>

But because among so many authors, the individual opinions of those who have interpreted the riddle teem with such wisdom and overflow with such cleverness, it would be an injury to them—and might easily provoke resentment against us—if they were buried in silence. Therefore, I deem it worthwhile to set down each of them immediately below. I do not do this because I, a Doctor of Laws, suddenly wish to act the Philosopher in refuting them; rather, I will touch upon them in passing and, as if through a lattice-window, lest I take it upon myself to judge their respective excellence. # Aelia Laelia Crispis

...experience <note>The text begins with a fragment, likely "experientia," completing a thought from the previous page.</note> confirmed elsewhere: for as everyone tends to abound in their own sense <note>A reference to the biblical proverb in Romans 14:5, suggesting people are often set in their own opinions.</note>, as the common proverb goes; and as everything similar is accustomed to generate its like, nor does a cause produce an effect alien to itself, so it can now be seen that almost every author draws an explanation of the Riddle from that faculty which they profess. Indeed, they sometimes strive to demonstrate that the riddle is nothing else than the very art in which they excel. Thus the Philosopher shows Philosophy, the Lawyer shows Law, the Mathematician shows Mathematics, the Chemist shows Chemistry, and the Musician shows Music to be hidden beneath that obscurity of words.

**MARIUS MICHAELANGELUS**, asserting this to be the dark water of the sea from the clouds of the Air, believed he could fish for and extract Philosophical Truth from the deep well of the Democritean Tomb <note>A reference to the philosopher Democritus, who famously claimed that truth lies hidden at the bottom of a deep well.</note>, since he formed a hook from those first five letters **A. M. P. P. D.** thus: *Aquam Maris Pluviam Pluit Deus* <note>original: "God rains the rain of the sea-water."</note>.

**RICCARDUS VITUS** <note>Richard White of Basingstoke (1539–1611), an English jurist who lived in exile in Italy.</note> brought a threefold solution to the difficulty (as we read the most clever Aesop once did, when his master broke faith and denied him the half of a revealed treasure and a freedom more precious than gold). Vitus, I say, having been deceived by the last three verses of Agathias Scholasticus <note>A 6th-century Greek poet whose epigrams were often adapted into Renaissance puzzles.</note>, had understood it in his first explanation as Niobe turned into stone; but having been informed that these verses were added by the Milanese, he wove together two nobler interpretations: one regarding the **Rational Soul**, the other regarding **Plato's Idea**.

**IOANNES TURRIUS**, emulating the heights of towers <note>The author makes a pun on the name "Turrius," which is Latin for "tower."</note> so that he might elevate himself with a higher investigation, meditated that the subject was **Prime Matter** <term>Materia Prima: In ancient and medieval philosophy, the fundamental, formless substance that underlies all physical reality.</term>.

Another subtle **SCHOTTUS**, named **FRANCISCUS**, reported that a **Eunuch** was depicted under this veil of words. Although **NICOLAUS BARNAUDUS** <note>A French alchemist and physician (c. 1538–1604).</note> had initially agreed with him, he immediately separated himself from that view; he suspected that there was hidden there—if not prime matter like Turrius—a matter even prior and more excellent than the first (if one may say so), namely **gold**; though perhaps his lot was not unlike that of the Alchemists, whom

# Not born, Rising again, etc.
<sig>C</sig>
<meta>catchword: Boldo-</meta>

...[he] believed that [someone] addressed only this obscure Stone with silent names, and thus the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>The legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> was signified by this.

**ZACHARIAS PONTINUS** <note>An author who wrote a commentary on the riddle in the 16th century.</note> thought that three human bodies were indicated in the same position, and likewise buried together by three men, similar to those about whom **Swertius** <note>Francis Sweerts (1567–1629), a Belgian historian and author of a collection of epitaphs.</note> writes in his *Humorous Epitaphs*, page 124:

> *Both GHENUS, and TRILIUS, and NINUS are buried in this urn;*
> *This one died by the rope, this one by the sword, this one by the waves;*

especially since among the ancient Romans many were buried together in this way, as seen in **Appian** <note>An ancient Roman historian of Greek origin.</note> (page 273, book 1) and elsewhere, and from **Gruter** <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627), a scholar famous for his massive collection of ancient inscriptions.</note> in a single entry for the three Emperors, **Vespasian**, **Titus**, and **Domitian** (page 244, 2). Thus Pontinus argued: the first body is that of **Aelia**, a woman, an old prostitute, pierced through with a sword and buried by **Lucius** (who rejoiced because he was her necessary heir); the second is **Laelia**, an androgynous girl, chaste, taken by poison and cast into the waters by her weeping husband **Agathon**; the third is a man, young and lewd, killed by hunger and buried in the same place by his mourning lover, **Priscus**, etc.

**ANDREAS NESMONDIUS** <note>André de Nesmond (1553–1616), a French jurist and president of the Parliament of Bordeaux.</note> suggested that the riddle refers to **Lawsuit**—that is, the shameful passion for litigation—or <term>the Malicious Character</term> <note>original: "τὸ Κακοήθες" (to Kakoethes), a Greek term referring to a spiteful or ill-natured disposition.</note>, which he argued was not a single vice, but a monster fashioned from many vices. He was convinced of this by that repeated heap of opposites which our Inscription <note>original: "Elogium," here referring to the text of the riddle itself.</note> stubbornly uses (or rather, abuses), with implacable discord in denying what it affirms and affirming what it denies.

But on the contrary, **JEAN GASPARD GEVAERTS** <note>Johannes Caspar Gevartius (1593–1666), a Flemish philologist and jurist.</note> showed with very strong reasoning that it is not discord or hatred here, but rather **Love**, and its nature, capable of producing so many contrary effects, that is described enigmatically. Just as "Love conquers all," Gevaerts seems to have surpassed all others who have written on this matter. For he is the one whom others now follow, and whose opinion even the most distinguished lights of refined literature do not disdain to support in these times—not to mention...

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<meta>catchword: from the—</meta>

**Boldoni** <note>Octavio Boldoni (1600–1680), a Barnabite scholar.</note> and the Father of the Society of Jesus, **Gaetano Felice Verani**, [have written on this]: the former in his praised *Palace of the Queen of Eloquence*, the latter in the *Pantheon of Sharp Elocution*. These are certainly sublime works, deservedly decorated with those spoils which **Fortunio Liceto**, the glory of the Lyceum of Padua, fought to erect as a trophy for himself. Liceto claimed victory over many physiological interpreters <note>Scholars who sought natural or scientific explanations for the riddle.</note> and his Paduan colleagues—supplanting them in his first book and triumphing over them in his second. Yet perhaps by chance it was not permitted for Liceto to capture his *Fugitive Satyr* <note>original: "FVGITIVVM SATYRVM"; a reference to one of Liceto's works.</note>; here it was prostrated and crushed underfoot by the "Love" of Verani, rather than being detained by the hand of the *Following Mercury* <note>original: "INSEQVENTIS MERCVRII"</note> as he had imagined.

**Marcus Zuerius van Boxhorn** <note>A Dutch scholar (1612–1653) known for his work on linguistics and history.</note> wished for a **Shadow** to be represented through these obscure and perplexed words. He notes that a shadow is nothing, yet it is everything, since it reflects all things and turns all things into itself. He likewise suggests **Time**, which, although it is nothing [tangible], contains all things within itself; as the father of all things, Time eventually devours and destroys them.

Finally, the wise **Father Ferroni** demonstrates and proves that **Music** is represented here. Music, through its three genres—namely the **Chromatic**, **Diatonic**, and **Enharmonic** <note>The three types of scales used in ancient Greek music theory.</note>—stirs and achieves these effects that are contrary to one another. It touches and embraces all sexes, every age, and any condition expressed there. It insinuates itself everywhere, but remains nowhere; and while it promotes these various outcomes, it simultaneously drives them away and overturns them.

And here I, as I advised before, completely decline the disagreement of opinions. I am not moved by any zeal for these parties to award the victory—which they so eagerly seek—to one or the other by my own judgment. Whether this glory should be granted to the Trans-alpines, the Italians, or, among those mentioned, our own fellow citizens:

> *Whether he be Trojan or Rutulian, I shall hold them with no distinction.* <note>original: "Tros, Rutulusve fuat, nullo discrimine habebo." A quote from Virgil’s Aeneid (10.108), expressing total impartiality.</note>

But if something ought to be added on behalf of our local scholars, as is proper, he who first comes to mind is the one who, in order to extricate himself from these labyrinthine windings, sought for himself an **Ariadne's thread** <note>In Greek myth, the thread used to navigate the Minotaur's labyrinth; here used as a metaphor for a logical guide through a complex problem.</note>...

# Not Born, Rising Again, etc.
<sig>C 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Unhappy</meta>

...[Boxhorn] wove [his interpretation] not unlearnedly from hemp. From here I should celebrate the never-wandering **Ulisse Aldrovandi** of Bologna, raised to the very stars for his *Monsters* <note>original: "MONSTRIS"; likely referring to his famous *Monstrorum Historia*, a massive encyclopedia of nature's wonders and anomalies.</note>; and his fellow soldier, our **Achille Bocchi**, who does not hide among the private but royal household gods of the **Bocchian Hermathena** <note>The *Hermathena* was the academy founded by Bocchi in his palace in Bologna, named for the union of Hermes (learning) and Athena (wisdom).</note>. Bocchi is equally recognized as active and prudent in symbolically conquering vices by the virtue of his *Questions* <gloss>referring to his book *Symbolic Questions*</gloss>. 

For there are those who report that both men, being of one mind, recognized **Lucius Agathon** as a real man, but detected a fictitious woman in **Aelia Laelia**. Nay, they saw under the appearance of a woman an **Evil Genius** or a **Wicked Spirit**—one wandering through the region of the Air, as Aldrovandi wrote; the other persisting on Earth, fixed and adhering to the **Junonian Oak**, as Bocchi argued. A more recent author added to this, explaining her as a **Sylvan Nymph** and a **Hamadryad** <note>A wood nymph whose life is bound to a specific tree.</note> of Casaraltula <note>A locality near Bologna, now known as Casaralta.</note>. This nymph, forced to live elsewhere after the oak she inhabited was finally cut and burned, is designated as "dead" and described in this sarcophagus by her beloved and diligent Agathon. 

Finally, I would venerate that admirable **Image of Jurisprudence** which our colleague **Bordocchius** <note>Giovanni Battista Bordocchi, a legal scholar.</note> wonderfully constructed from this "Aelian marble" while leading us all with his first lecture at the start of our studies. Likewise, I would honor the **Rule of Chastity**, which the aforementioned distinguished interpretation by our **Mengoli** <note>Pietro Mengoli (1626–1686), a Bolognese mathematician and priest.</note> taught me was mystically proposed here to others by the Knight-Brother **Loderigo Andalò** <note>A 13th-century Bolognese nobleman who founded the Order of the Blessed Virgin Mary (the "Frati Gaudenti").</note>.

I would certainly not deprive all these men, or any others, of their due honors. Nor do I fear that they, adorned with the highest praise, would hinder my own small glory (should I prove worthy of any by this exposition). I do not seek such glory through competition in learning, erudition, or elegance, as I have hinted elsewhere; rather, I seek it through a naked, fortuitous, and simple divination, to be expressed in an entirely unrefined style. This shall be done after I have cleansed the riddle of the errors with which it teems in every other version (as will be demonstrated in the following chapter); for from all sides:

> *So that Ceres* <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture; here representing the grain harvest or the fruitful truth of the investigation.</note> *may walk heavy with new fruit.*

## Chapter II.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring floral motifs and vines within an oval frame, surrounded by a rectangular border with scrollwork.</image-desc>

Just as rivers, the further they are carried in their course, the more they overflow with water as small streams from all sides flow into the main channel, and the more turbid <note>Meaning muddy or thick with sediment.</note> they become; so it is with matters of fame, which
> *thrives on movement, and gains strength by going,* <note>original: "mobilitate viget, viresque acquirit eundo"; a famous quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (IV, 175) describing how rumors grow as they spread.</note>

carried here and there, the more they are made public by the mouths of men, the more they are circulated in an increased and altered state. Everyone dares to offer their own opinion, and each person seems to become a participant in the deed being described when they either add something of their own or at least adorn the narration with their own grandiloquence <note>Pompous or extravagant language.</note>. Moreover, this phenomenon which we understand to happen daily with rumors tossed about by the public voice of the fickle common people, we also very often experience in more dignified <term>characters</term> <note>In this context, "characters" refers to the literal letters or inscriptions.</note>, even those carved into hard stone. I am speaking of the Ancient Inscriptions themselves, for which their marble solidity was never so beneficial that their integrity was not harmed more than was right by a certain carelessness in the <term>Transcribers</term> <note>The scholars or clerks who copied the text of the stone onto paper.</note>—whether I should call it ignorance or craftiness, I do not entirely know. Who would deny that this has happened to the Aelian inscription <note>The Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle.</note> above all others? For as many times as we have seen it written out by the authors listed above, just as many times have we seen it recorded incorrectly. The more industriously it was transcribed by them, the more uselessly it was described for us—nearly always corrupted by new words, and nearly always [interrupted/altered...]

## Chapter II.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring floral motifs and vines within an oval frame, surrounded by a rectangular border with scrollwork.</image-desc>

Just as rivers, the further they are carried in their course, the more they overflow with water (as small streams from all sides flow into the main channel) and the more turbid <note>Or "clouded." The author is comparing the clarity of a fresh spring to the muddiness of a long-running river.</note> they become; so too it is with matters of fame—which 
> thrives on movement, and gathers strength as it goes, <note>original: "mobilitate viget, viresque acquirit eundo"; a famous line from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (IV, 175) describing how Rumor (Fama) grows as it travels.</note>

carried here and there, the more they are broadcast by the mouths of men, the more they are increased and even altered as they circulate. Each person dares to put forth their own opinion, and seems to become a participant in the deed itself once it has been set out, when they either add something of their own or at least decorate the narrative with their own grandiloquence. This phenomenon, which we understand happens daily in rumors tossed about by the public voice of the fickle crowd, we also very often experience in more dignified characters, even those carved into hard stone; I mean in those very Ancient Inscriptions, which never benefited so much from their marble solidity that a certain carelessness in the Transcribers—whether I should call it ignorance or craftiness, I do not quite know—did not harm their integrity more than was fair. Who could deny that this has happened with the Aelian inscription <note>The *Aelia Laelia Crispis*, a famous enigmatic inscription from Bologna that has puzzled scholars for centuries.</note> above all others? For as often as we have seen it recorded by the authors reviewed above, so often have we seen it full of errors; the more industriously it was transcribed by them, the more uselessly it was described for us? It is almost always so distorted by new words, and almost always 

<meta>catchword: inte- [Likely 'interpolatam' (interpolated) or 'integram' (entire)]</meta>

<summary>The author explains how the famous Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription has been corrupted over time by various scholars and transcribers, comparing the accumulation of textual errors to the way a river grows muddy as it flows far from its source.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, inscriptions, textual corruption, transcription, Virgil, rumor, philology, Bologna enigma</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous chapter, moving from the general concept of how rumors and texts degrade over time to the specific case of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription. He explains that the many variations of the text exist because previous scholars have "corrected" or added to the verses to support their own theories.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>
<sig>A M.</sig>

...augmented with entire small verses, so that as soon as it took on a new appearance and meaning, it completely lost its original form. From this, as many different readings of AELIA LAELIA are circulated as there are transcriptions published by the aforementioned writers—versions so conveniently altered and polished by these changes that the inscription is gradually pulled toward the writer’s own desire. Thus, different interpreters are forced by these added words to explain this stone in contradictory ways. Therefore, it is my intention to review these versions individually and briefly, and to point out their variations in some order (albeit cursorily), once I have presented here those two older and more common examples. These serve as the twin hinges upon which the entire weight of this Enigmatic Mass <note>The author refers to the physical stone monument as a "mass" or "structure."</note> rests, and around which almost all other minor variations revolve. Here are the first and the second:

->To the Departed Spirits<- <note>original: "D M," an abbreviation for *Dis Manibus*, a standard Roman funerary dedication to the spirits of the dead.</note>
### AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS
->NEITHER MAN NOR WOMAN NOR ANDROGYNE<-
->NEITHER GIRL NOR YOUTH NOR OLD WOMAN<-
->NEITHER CHASTE NOR WHORE NOR MODEST<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->TAKEN AWAY<-
->NEITHER BY HUNGER NOR BY SWORD NOR BY POISON<-
->BUT BY ALL OF THEM<-
->SHE LIES NEITHER IN THE SKY NOR IN THE WATERS NOR IN THE EARTH<-
->BUT EVERYWHERE<-

### LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS
->NEITHER HUSBAND NOR LOVER NOR KINSMAN<-
->NEITHER MOURNING NOR REJOICING NOR WEEPING<-
->FOR HER<-
->[HE BUILT] NEITHER A MOUND <note>original: "molem"</note> NOR A PYRAMID NOR A TOMB<-
->BUT ALL THREE<-
->HE KNOWS, AND KNOWS NOT, FOR WHOM HE RAISED IT<-

<term>Aelia Laelia Crispis</term> (The subject of a famous archeological riddle found in Bologna); <term>Lucius Agatho Priscius</term> (The supposed dedicator named in the second part of the riddle); <term>Enigma</term> (A puzzling or paradoxical statement); <term>Inscription</term> (Text carved into a durable surface); <term>Stone</term> (The physical tablet); <term>Epitaph</term> (A commemorative inscription on a tomb).

<summary>The author presents the two most authoritative versions of the famous Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, a paradoxical Latin funerary inscription that has puzzled scholars for centuries. He argues that these two versions are the foundation upon which all other corruptions and interpretations are built.</summary>

# A. M. P. P. D.
<note>These initials are a mystery; the author later contrasts them with the standard Roman funerary dedication "D. M."</note>

## AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS

->NEITHER MAN NOR WOMAN NOR ANDROGYNE<-
->NEITHER GIRL NOR YOUTH NOR OLD WOMAN<-
->NEITHER CHASTE NOR WHORE NOR MODEST<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->TAKEN AWAY NEITHER BY HUNGER NOR BY POISON<-
->BUT BY ALL OF THEM<-
->SHE LIES NEITHER IN THE SKY NOR IN THE WATERS NOR IN THE EARTH<-
->BUT EVERYWHERE<-
->LAELIA CRISPIS, OTHERWISE IN THE SHARP HOLLOW<- <note>original: "alias in cavo acuto." This phrase is highly debated; "cavo acuto" could refer to a specific geographical feature, a grave, or a wordplay involving a "sharp" or "shrill" void.</note>

## LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS

->NEITHER HUSBAND NOR LOVER NOR KINSMAN<-
->NEITHER GRIEVING NOR REJOICING NOR WEEPING<-
->THIS, NEITHER MONUMENT NOR PYRAMID NOR TOMB<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT WHAT HE HAS PLACED FOR WHOM<-
->THIS IS A TOMB HAVING NO CORPSE INSIDE<-
->THIS IS A CORPSE HAVING NO TOMB OUTSIDE<-
->BUT THE CORPSE AND THE TOMB ARE THE SAME TO ITSELF<-

The first version exists among us in Bologna, while the second was published by the people of Milan. The former is carved into flint with a chisel <note>original: "celte," a term for a stone-cutting tool.</note>; the latter was once recorded on parchment. The former is inscribed in what they call Roman characters, whereas the latter is written in Gothic letters, as they are commonly called. The former is, as is well known, intact and simple, while the latter is clearly corrupted and expanded. Therefore, the former, being the earlier version, is deservedly to be preferred over the latter, as it abounds with all those signs that reflect venerable antiquity and ancient elegance. For what do those unusual and never-before-seen characters **A. M. P. P. D.** have in common with the ancient and most common funerary markers **D. M.** <note>To the Departed Spirits; original: "Dis Manibus."</note>? And what of those superfluous words: **LAELIA CRISPIS**?

<summary>The author presents two competing versions of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle—one from Bologna and a longer one from Milan—arguing that the simpler Bolognese stone inscription is the authentic ancient original.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Lucius Agatho Priscius, Bologna vs Milan versions, funerary inscriptions, epigraphy, riddle, Latin paleography</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as an introduction to the specific textual variations of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, providing the foundational versions of the inscription before the author explores later additions on the following page.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

...augmented with whole new verses, so that as soon as it took on a shape and meaning, it completely lost its original sense. Thus, as many different readings of AELIA LAELIA are circulated as there are copies of it published by the aforementioned writers, sometimes so conveniently altered and refined that the inscription is gradually pulled toward the writer's desired meaning, and the commentators are forced to interpret this Stone <note>original: "Lapidem," referring to the marble slab upon which the riddle was famously inscribed in Bologna.</note> differently based on the added words. My intention, therefore, is to briefly review these variations one by one and indicate their differences in some order, and indeed quickly, after I have presented here those two older and more common versions upon which, as if on a double hinge, the entire weight of this Enigmatic Mass <note>original: "Aenigmaticae Molis"</note> rests; and around which all other minor variations primarily revolve. Here are the two versions.

->D. M.<- <note>original: "D M," an abbreviation for "Dis Manibus," a traditional Roman funerary dedication to the "spirits of the departed."</note>
->**AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS**<-

<header>Not born, She rises again, etc.</header>

# A. M. PP. D.
<note>original: "A. M. PP. D." These initials are highly debated; while "D. M." is a standard Roman funerary dedication, these letters are unique to the Milanese version of the riddle and their meaning remains a mystery.</note>

# AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS

->NEITHER MAN NOR WOMAN NOR ANDROGYNE<-
->NEITHER GIRL NOR YOUTH NOR OLD WOMAN<-
->NEITHER CHASTE NOR WHORE NOR MODEST<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->CARRIED OFF NEITHER BY HUNGER NOR BY POISON<-
<note>original: "veneno." Note that the version on the previous page listed "sword" (ferro) instead of "poison." Such variations are central to the author's critique.</note>
->BUT BY ALL OF THEM<-
->SHE LIES NEITHER IN THE SKY NOR IN THE WATERS NOR IN THE EARTH<-
->BUT EVERYWHERE<-
->LAELIA CRISPIS ELSEWHERE IN A SHARP HOLLOW<-
<note>original: "alias in cavo acuto." This is a cryptic addition found in the Milanese text, possibly referring to a specific burial site or a metaphorical "sharp" wit.</note>

# LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS

->NEITHER HUSBAND NOR LOVER NOR KINSMAN<-
->NEITHER MOURNING NOR REJOICING NOR WEEPING<-
->THIS, NEITHER A MASS NOR A PYRAMID NOR A TOMB<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT WHAT HE HAS PLACED FOR WHOM<-
->THIS IS A TOMB HAVING NO CORPSE WITHIN<-
->THIS IS A CORPSE HAVING NO TOMB WITHOUT<-
->BUT THE CORPSE IS THE SAME AS THE TOMB TO ITSELF<-

The first version exists among us in Bologna; the other was published by the Milanese. The former is certainly carved in flint <note>original: "filice," likely referring to the hard stone slab in Bologna.</note>, while the latter was once committed to parchment. The former is incised in the Roman script, as they call it; the latter is written in Gothic letters <note>A common distinction in the Renaissance: "Roman" script was seen as elegant and authentic to antiquity, while "Gothic" (blackletter) was often viewed as a medieval corruption.</note>, as is commonly said. The former is intact and simple, as is well known; the latter is corrupted and augmented, as is obvious. For that reason, being the older of the two, the former is deservedly to be preferred to this one, since it abounds with all those signs that reflect venerable antiquity and ancient elegance. For what do those unusual and never-before-seen characters **A. M. PP. D.** have to do with the ancient and very common tomb markers **D. M.**? <note>original: "Dis Manibus," meaning "To the spirits of the departed."</note> And what of those superfluous words: **LAELIA CRISPIS**?

<summary>This page presents the Milanese version of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, contrasting it with the older, more "authentic" stone version from Bologna. The author argues that the Bologna version is superior due to its classical Roman lettering and simplicity, whereas the Milanese version appears corrupted by later additions.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Bologna, Milan, epigraphy, Gothic script, Roman script, Lucius Agatho Priscius, funerary enigmas, manuscript traditions</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 23, the author continues to analyze the discrepancies between the original inscription found in Bologna and the expanded version circulated in Milan, arguing that the additions in the latter are irrelevant borrowings from ancient Greek literature.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

...CRISPIS ELSEWHERE IN A SHARP HOLLOW <note>original: "CRISPIS ALIAS IN CAVO ACVTO"</note>, which clearly demonstrate a completely useless redundancy, overstepping the bounds and achieving nothing more than what has already been said? Finally, what of the last three lines:

> THIS IS A TOMB HAVING NO CORPSE WITHIN
> THIS IS A CORPSE HAVING NO TOMB WITHOUT
> BUT THE CORPSE IS THE SAME AS THE TOMB TO ITSELF,

which are entirely foreign to our subject? They are, I say, an enigmatic epitaph—or if you prefer, a riddle about Niobe turned to stone—which the famous Agathias Scholasticus <note>A 6th-century Byzantine historian and poet.</note> recorded among so many others in his volume of various Greek epigrams by noble poets titled *Daphnica*. Agathias lived during the time of the Emperor Justinian and his generals Belisarius and Narses, and wrote of their deeds following the historian Procopius. Is it not true that all these things were foolishly or cunningly added to our Bolognese original <note>original: "autographo," referring here to the physical stone inscription.</note>, striving only to defile its ancient purity?

Nevertheless, both versions count their own followers and boast their own supporters, who may be divided into two classes. The first consists of those who, clinging to our Bolognese archetype, have not strayed from it even a hair's breadth. The second consists of those who, never having inspected the original or having disregarded it, preferred instead to follow the Milanese version. 

There is no doubt that all the Bolognese stood by their own Felsine <note>Felsina was the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna; the author uses "Felsineum" to emphasize the city's ancient roots.</note> prototype—with the exception of Gherardaccio, who, failing to preserve historical truth very successfully and inquiring too little, joined himself to the Milanese manuscript. Yet even among foreigners, there were not a few who arrived here from distant regions specifically to inspect the stone with their own eyes. They did this so that they might not strike against this Stone as if against a sandbank—the common fate of many writers— and by faithfully transcribing it, they transmitted a version to posterity that was entirely free of errors. These men include Turrius, Draudius (though his version was altered and enlarged toward the end by Solinus), Gevartius, and von Pflaumern; to whom must be added the equally faithful transcri-

<meta>catchword: transcri- (continuing to "transcriptor" or "transcriber")</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Milanese version of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle is a corrupted text that incorrectly incorporated an ancient Greek epigram about Niobe. He defends the authenticity of the original Bolognese stone inscription, citing several scholars who traveled to see and record it accurately.</summary>

<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Bologna, Milan, Niobe, Agathias Scholasticus, Justinian, epigrams, Felsina, Gherardaccio, transcription, authenticity</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 24, the author continues to categorize scholars who have studied the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription, specifically focusing on those who preferred the expanded Milanese version over the original Bolognese stone.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>She who is not born, rising again, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &amp;c."</note></header>
<sig>D</sig>

the writer Zaccaria Pontino, and others, whom I shall pass over so as not to appear like an "Arabian messenger" <note>original: "Ἀράβιος ἄγγελος" (Arabios angelos). A Greek proverb referring to a long-winded or tedious messenger.</note>.

However, many others—especially philosophers—have preferred the Milanese version to this solid marble. They assumed that the more the pure antiquity of our inscription was distorted and corrupted by fraudulent additions, the broader the path opened for their own speculations. Even one of our own, Montalbano <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), a Bolognese polymath who wrote under the pseudonym Wolframo.</note>, believed this; he admitted that the Milanese <note>original: "Insubrium," referring to the Insubres, the ancient inhabitants of the Milan region.</note> additions (from which our stone is entirely free) wonderfully supported his own interpretation, such as that already mentioned line:

->LELIA CRISPIS ELSEWHERE IN A SHARP HOLLOW,<-

and that riddle by Agathias Scholasticus <note>A 6th-century Byzantine poet and historian.</note> concerning Niobe <note>A figure in Greek mythology who wept for her children until she turned to stone; the riddle describes a stone that is both a body and a grave.</note>:
> THIS IS A TOMB HAVING NO CORPSE WITHIN
> THIS IS A CORPSE HAVING NO TOMB WITHOUT
> BUT THE CORPSE IS THE SAME AS THE TOMB TO ITSELF.

All these men—Michelangelo, Vitus, Draudius (corrupted as noted above), Schott, Barnaud—are both cited and refuted based on their own words by the most learned Liceto <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a prominent physician and philosopher who wrote a famous treatise on this riddle.</note> in his first book. However, when he began his second book to declare his own opinion, even he embraced and followed the Milanese version. To all these, we add Reusner, Zuerius, Drexelius, Beyerlinck, Castellini, Labbe, Nicoli, Verani, and Spon; along with our own Gherardacci and Montalbano, in the manner described above. Yet, all these individual discrepancies will appear more fully and clearly in a single glance if they are set before your eyes in some orderly fashion. I shall repeat our epitaph here line by line, describing the differences already indicated (and countless others) beneath each part, and attributing each to its author. In this way, I will show how many ways the text has been mutilated, varied, or expanded, presenting it in the form of a table for your direct inspection, so to speak, in the following manner:

<summary>The author lists various scholars who have analyzed the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, noting that many—including Fortunio Liceti—erroneously preferred the corrupted Milanese version because its additions allowed for more complex philosophical speculation. He proposes a comparative table to clarify these textual variations.</summary>

# Not Born, Rising Again, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." These are phrases from the riddle used as headings to categorize how different scholars transcribed the text.</note>

| | |
|---|---|
| TONIOLA | MASINI |
| BOLDONI | NERO |
| PALACE OF ELOQUENCE | MONTALBANI |
| RICHEA | BORDOCCHI |
| FERRONI | MENGOLI |
| ALIDOSI | |

<column-break/>

->They Omit<- <note>original: "Omittunt." Referring to scholars who leave out specific words in their versions of the inscription.</note>

| | |
|---|---|
| MICHELANGELO | GHERARDACCI |
| VITO | <gloss>That word **AND**, namely: **HE KNOWS KNOWS NOT FOR WHOM HE PLACED IT.** <note>original: "Illud ET, videlicet: SCIT NESCIT CVI POSVERIT." These scholars omit the conjunction "and" (et) between "knows" and "knows not."</note></gloss> |
| REMIGIO | |
| CASTELLINI | |

<column-break/>

->They Change<- <note>original: "Immutant." Referring to scholars who alter the wording of the final line.</note>

| | |
|---|---|
| REUSNER | BARNAUD |
| ZUERI | PONTINO |
| NICOLI | LICETI |
| SPON | LABBE |
| <gloss>That **FOR WHOM** into **WHAT**, thus: **HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT WHAT HE PLACED.** <note>original: "Illud CVI in QVID, ita: SCIT ET NESCIT QVID POSVERIT."</note></gloss> | <gloss>Adding **WHAT** in this manner: **HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT WHAT FOR WHOM HE PLACED IT.** <note>original: "Addentes QVID hoc pacto: SCIT ET NESCIT QVID CVI POSVERIT."</note></gloss> |
| SCHOTT | |

<column-break/>

And these are all the principal discrepancies which, being completely foreign to the original and authentic context of our archetype, could corrupt its ancient and pure reading, were it not that the reading stands supported by the testimony of the Stone <note>The physical marble slab in Bologna upon which the riddle was inscribed.</note>. It utterly rejects so many introduced mutilations, additions, and changes of others, casting them back upon their new authors; instead, it turns those variations into evidence of its own unblemished antiquity. For "this so varied reading of the Monument" (says Liceti himself in the previously mentioned *Peripatetic Allegory*, book 2, chapter 2) "proposed by Mario, Torri, Barnaud, Schott, and others, makes its antiquity sufficiently evident. For new writings are read in only one way, not having yet suffered—as it is held in the 4th book of the *Physics*, volume 117—the heavier injuries of most devouring time; but those which have come down to us from antiquity..."

<meta>catchword: ne- (beginning of "nevertheless" or a similar transition)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the comparative analysis of the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription, listing scholars who omit or change the final dedication. It argues that the very existence of these textual variations proves the monument's great age, citing Aristotle's theories on time's decay.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, textual criticism, scholars, inscriptions, antiquity, Fortunio Liceti, Aristotle, Bologna, epigraphy</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the textual variations of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle. The sentence begins mid-word with "nerunt" (likely the end of "pervenerunt," meaning "arrived" or "reached us"), continuing the thought that various records of the riddle have been handed down with significant discrepancies.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...[have reached] the records of writers, it is surprising how varied and how diverse they are found to be everywhere. Some are mutilated in certain parts, others are altered in different ways, whether by the fault of the transcribers—who did not exactly understand the most ancient forms of the letters <note>original: "literarum notas"</note>—or even by the age of the material being corroded and corrupted. Since, therefore, the readings of *Mariana* and *Turriana* <note>These refer to versions of the text published by Juan de Mariana and likely Francisco Torres (Turrianus), both 16th-century scholars.</note> lack many of the elements found in the *Arnaudina* version <note>Referring to the version published by Jean de Basmaison Pougnet, often called the "Barnaud" or "Arnaudina" reading.</note>, and in this latter version some things are missing that exist in the former; and since they are even read as being arranged in various ways within the same sources; is this not a clear sign of the antiquity of this *Agathonian* enigma? <note>original: "ænigmatis huius Agathonij." The name Agatho or Agathonius was sometimes associated with the authorship or the dedication of the riddle.</note>

Let us therefore retain this version of ours, setting all others aside. Let us take it alone to be interpreted as the uniquely true and legitimate one, and let us subjoin our own slender and earthy opinion—brought forth entirely without contempt for others, and not contentiously, but rather dutifully and submissively—to those other opinions which are so grand and ethereal.

> *You will not find Centaurs here, nor Gorgons, nor Harpies;*
> *Our page tastes of man.* <note>original: "Non hic Centauros, non Gorgonas, Harpiasque / Invenies; hominem pagina nostra sapit." A quote from the Roman poet Martial (Epigrams 10.4), suggesting that the author’s interpretation deals with human reality rather than mythological monsters.</note>

# Chapter III.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

If anyone were to ask me in a moment of leisure what the reason is that the sudden, spontaneous counsels of women are usually considered superior to those produced by their long meditation, I would conclude that it happens entirely because, in those moments, the artifice of speech <note>original: "elocutionis," referring to the formal style of rhetoric.</note> does not corrupt the sincerity of the thought. In such cases, the straining of the mind gives way to the light of truth—a light implanted in our soul, which is scented with divinity, from the first threshold of creation. Therefore, we seek the innate light of truth in hidden places in vain, since it is better to wait for it as a windfall from Heaven. 

Indeed, this is added to the rest of the hardships of our mortal life: that the more laboriously we strive to achieve something excellent, the more easily we are frustrated in our wish. The eloquent Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian artists.</note> used to remind us that this very thing happened time and again to Raphael Sanzio and to Andrea, commonly called "del Sarto." For as often as either of them tried to produce something beyond their natural strength in the creation of divine images, just as often did they strip away much of the excellence from those same works. Since, therefore, those who strive the hardest often accomplish the least—as I noted in my own *Lives of the Bolognese Painters* <note>original: "Felsinâ Pictrice," the author Malvasia’s most famous work, published in 1678.</note> according to the opinion of that same author—and since those who reach for more sublime heights often fall with a greater crash, by their very own daring:

<summary>Malvasia introduces Chapter III, arguing that spontaneous insight is often superior to labored study. He identifies his solution to the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle as "Abortion" (Ektrosis) and cites art historians like Vasari to explain how over-exertion can ruin a work.</summary>

...have reached <note>Completing the word "pervenerunt" from the previous page.</note> us as the monuments of writers, it is a wonder how varied and how diverse they are found to be everywhere; some are mutilated in certain parts, others are altered in others, whether by the fault of transcribers who did not exactly understand the very ancient forms of the letters, or even by the antiquity of the material being eaten away and corrupted. Since, therefore, in the *Mariana* and *Turriana* <note>These refer to specific transcriptions or editions of the riddle by scholars such as Juan de Mariana or Torres; the author is comparing different versions of the text to prove how inconsistent they are.</note> readings, most of those things which are read in the *Arnaudina* version are missing, and in this one [the *Arnaudina*] some things are also missing that exist in the others; and since they are even read arranged in various ways in the same one; is this not a clear sign of the antiquity of this *Agathonian* <note>original: "Agathonij"; likely referring to Agathon, a name sometimes associated with the authorship of the riddle in early modern legends.</note> enigma?

Therefore, setting all others aside, let us hold onto this version of ours; let us take it up to be interpreted as the only true and legitimate one, and let us subjoin our thin and earthly opinion—brought forth without any contempt for others—not contentiously, but rather dutifully and submissively to those other interpretations so grand and heavenly.

# Chapter III.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

If anyone should ask me in a moment of leisure why the advice of women, which bursts forth spontaneously, is often considered superior to the advice that comes from their long reflection, I would conclude that it happens because, in the former, the artifice of speech does not spoil the sincerity of the thought. At such times, the deliberate effort of the mind gives way to the light of truth—a light that has been planted in our soul, which smells of divinity, from the very first moment of creation. It is useless, therefore, to search studiously for the innate light of truth in dark corners, when it is better to wait for it as a gift sent from Heaven. 

Truly, it is one more of the burdens of our mortal life that the more we labor to achieve something extraordinary, the more likely we are to be frustrated in our desire. The eloquent Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian Renaissance artists whose "Lives of the Artists" shaped art history.</note> used to remark that this happened quite often to Raphael Sanzio and to Andrea, commonly called *del Sarto*. For every time either of them tried to produce something beyond their natural strength while painting divine images, they ended up stripping away much of the excellence from the work. Since those who exert themselves the most often accomplish the least (as I noted in my *Felsina Pictrix* <note>The major work by Carlo Cesare Malvasia, published in 1678, documenting the lives of Bolognese painters. "Felsina" is the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna.</note> using that same author’s opinion), and since those who reach for the heights often fall harder because of their own boldness:

<meta>catchword: tollun- (likely beginning the word "tollunt," meaning "they lift up" or "they take away")</meta>

<summary>The author introduces a new interpretation of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle as a "miscarriage" (Ektrosis), arguing that spontaneous intuition is often more truthful than labored intellectual effort, citing examples from the lives of Renaissance painters.</summary>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

-> they are lifted on high, <-
-> *So that they may fall with a heavier crash* ; <note>original: "tolluntur in altum, Ut lapsu graviore ruant." A common Latin sentiment, famously used by the poet Claudian, warning that the higher one rises, the more dangerous the fall.</note> --- <-

I decided that more careful scrutiny must be applied, and I believe that the solution to the Enigma should be established as the one which, as I mentioned, presented itself to me immediately upon the first inspection of the Stone. For this reason, I publish this solution—as befits the Truth—without any disguise of philosophical sophistry or the embellishment of rhetorical exaggeration. If anyone should despise these findings as being "sought from the crossroads" <note>An idiom meaning common or unrefined.</note>, let them know that I preferred to crawl along the ground rather than fly through the clouds or the Heavens, or to be carried away and wander among *rainwater*, *Plato’s Ideas*, *evil spirits*, *wandering shadows*, or the *deliriums of love*. I did this so that I might not, like him

-> *who gave his name to the Icarian waters*, <note>original: "Icariis nomina fecit aquis." A reference to Icarus, who flew too close to the sun and fell into the sea.</note> <-

suffer the penalty for my own audacity through a headlong fall. Therefore, let us set aside whatever escapes common sense; for I am well aware of that Socratic maxim, *what is above us is nothing to us* <note>original: "quae supra nos nihil ad nos." A phrase often used to discourage idle speculation about things beyond human experience.</note> (which I wish to assert without any impiety).

And these things which are more hidden are made manifest through things that are more open, regarding which *Alciatus* <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), the author of the first "Emblem Book," which combined images and riddles to convey moral lessons.</note> speaks similarly in his emblems, and specifically our own *Bocchius* <note>Achille Bocchi (1488–1562), a Bolognese humanist whose book of symbols was famous in the author's circle.</note> in symbol 54 of his second book.

For what, I ask you, does the laying down of a lifeless body have to do here with an exposition on the <term>Rational Soul</term> <note>The highest part of the human soul in Aristotelian philosophy, responsible for reason.</note>? What does this final line of one's days have to do with a description of <term>Prime Matter</term> <note>The formless, fundamental substance of the universe according to ancient science.</note>? What does this Stone inscribed for the dead have to do with a description of the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold or granting immortality.</note>? Indeed, this misfortune of the Laelian birth is a whole heaven away from the idealized concepts of *Generation*, *Friendship*, and *Privation* <note>Aristotelian terms used to explain how things are created and change.</note>. It is, as I believe, the dissolution of the friendship between the Laelian and Agathonian families—which was to be bound even more tightly through a marital bond—and the loss of all the goods of Laelius that were to be transmitted to the Agathonian family as a dowry and inheritance, which provides the legitimate subject for our inscription. To put the matter briefly and explain myself more clearly: it speaks of a daughter to be born to Laelius, who was also the designated bride for Agatho;

<summary>The author rejects mystical and alchemical interpretations of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, arguing instead that it describes a real-world tragedy: a miscarriage or the death of a child that broke a planned marriage alliance between the Laelius and Agatho families.</summary>

->D M<-

This stands for *To the Departed Spirits* <note>original: "DIS MANIBUS"; a standard Roman funerary invocation found on countless gravestones</note>; a mark so frequent for those buried, or at least memorialized, that it sufficiently proves by itself that our Inscription pertains entirely to a sarcophagus <term>sarcophagus: a stone coffin, typically adorned with a sculpture or inscription</term>.

<meta>Continuing the author's argument from page 41, where he identified the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription as a funerary monument for a miscarried fetus. <page-num>42</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

Since none of the previous writers could deny this fact, many decided that this obstacle <note>the presence of the 'D.M.' dedication to the spirits of the dead</note> had to be avoided; and this was done by either omitting or changing the letters mentioned. Both of these actions (as far as historical accuracy is concerned) were, at the very least, ill-advised, if not reckless. But perhaps the omission is less to be criticized, since the Ancients did not always show themselves to be strict observers of this mark—common though it was—and they often omitted it even on the most notable stones, as seen in the works of Gruter <note>Janus Gruterus (1560–1627), a famous Dutch scholar known for his massive collection of ancient Roman inscriptions</note> and other collectors of inscriptions. However, if they did occasionally change such letters, they substituted them with others like these:

->I. O. M. <note>Jupiter, Best and Greatest</note>, I. O. M. D., I. D. I. M. S., C. S. F., M. D. M. I., M. D. M. S., M. D. S. M., MM. S., D. S. D. S., I. M. S., I. M. S. S. S., I. N. H. D. D., D. H. D. D., Tutelae P. L. M., M. L. D. P., In H. D D. N., T. M. S. A. S., H. V. D.<-

and others of a similar kind. But they *never* prefixed these letters: **A. M. PP. D.** From this we can recognize that these letters were devised and substituted by the Milanese, according to their own wit. Some have interpreted this quite gracefully as: **A**cademici **M**ediolanenſes **P**atavinis **P**roponunt **D**iſſoluendum <note>original Latin: "The Milanese Academy proposes to the Paduans that this be solved"</note>; or (to use their very own words): **A**cademici **M**ediolanenſes **P**hiloſophis **P**atavinis **D**onant <note>original Latin: "The Milanese Academy gives this to the Paduan Philosophers"</note>. To these, allow me to joke in a similar fashion: **A**enigma **M**alè **P**er **P**atavinos **D**eclaratum <note>original Latin: "The Enigma badly explained by the Paduans"</note>.

### ÆLIA LÆLIA CRISPIS

These were the names to be given to this awaited and desired offspring, if it had been brought to a timely and full birth <note>if the pregnancy had reached full term</note> and, being delivered, had appeared as a female. This was the wish of Agatho, who had been designated as the husband on that condition <note>referring to an arrangement where Agatho was betrothed to the child if it were born a girl</note>. Just as we read of the name of a hero not yet existing being mentioned by Anchises <note>A reference to Virgil’s *Aeneid*, where Anchises shows Aeneas the souls of his future descendants in the Underworld</note> in olden times:

<meta>Continuing the discussion from page 42, the author is providing his interpretation of the famous "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle. Having established that the monument is a funerary one for a miscarried fetus, he now begins to break down the paradoxical language of the inscription itself, explaining how a being that never truly lived can be described through contradictions.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>Rising again without having been born, etc.</header>
<sig>F 2</sig>

> *Alas, pitiable boy, if only you could break through the harsh fates,*
> *You shall be Marcellus.*
<note>original Latin: "Heu, miserande puer, si quà fata aspera rumpas, Tu Marcellus eris." These famous lines from Virgil’s Aeneid (Book VI) refer to the young Marcellus, nephew of Augustus, who died young. The quote highlights the tragedy of a life cut short before its potential could be realized.</note>

Since, however, this female fetus was not truly conceived—or if it were conceived, it turned into a miscarriage, or rather an abortion, in its very first days—it was unable to assume the name decreed for it, nor even to receive a soul, and could hardly take on any form at all. Thus, this soulless and formless <term>Embryo</term><note>The author uses "Embryo" here in the classical sense of an unformed or early-stage fetus.</note> provided a rare and unheard-of theme for an <term>Inscription</term> to our perceptive fellow citizen. Because he greatly desired to hand down this failed event to the memory of posterity, and was eager to entrust it to the sepulchral marble, he enriched it with these many <term>antitheses</term><note>An antithesis is a rhetorical device that puts two opposing ideas together to create a contrasting effect.</note>. He presented it in the form of a <term>Riddle</term><note>original Latin: "Aenigmatis"</note> to be read and unraveled by all those in the future who might be ignorant of such an event. He first gathered those qualities in order which regard Sex, Age, and Status; and since one of these must necessarily belong to an adult, yet none could fit this Embryo, he expressed this through negation:

->NEITHER MAN NOR WOMAN NOR HERMAPHRODITE<-
->NEITHER GIRL NOR YOUTH NOR OLD WOMAN<-
->NEITHER CHASTE NOR PROSTITUTE NOR MODEST;<-

Yet it could have carried and represented all these things within itself, if only it had happened to be born and to live; therefore, the inscription adds:

->BUT ALL THINGS.<-

However, it was "all things" in the process of becoming, not in a finished state; in <term>potentiality</term> rather than in <term>actuality</term>. Or, to use <term>Scholastic terms</term><note>The language of medieval and Renaissance philosophy, heavily influenced by Aristotle.</note>, it existed in the "first and designated act" but not in the "second and exercised act." While in actuality it was none of the aforementioned things, in potentiality it was all of them. "All things," indeed, not joined together or collectively (as we distinguish in <term>our Law</term><note>original Latin: "Iure nostro," referring to Roman or Civil Law</note>...

<meta>catchword: distinguish...</meta>

<summary>The author explains the paradoxical "neither/nor" language of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle by arguing it refers to a fetus. He uses Scholastic philosophy to show that while the fetus was "nothing" in reality, it was "everything" in terms of what it could have potentially become.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the analysis from the previous page, the author focuses on the identity and motivations of the supposed author of the riddle, Lucius Agatho Priscius, and explains the contradictory emotions and descriptions of the monument itself.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

# LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS.

These are the names of that enigmatic Inscriber who—because he was unable to marry this woman who was never born, nor to love her who was not living, nor to succeed as the heir of one who was not dead, and consequently could neither grieve, nor rejoice, nor weep over her passing—therefore was:

->NEITHER HUSBAND, NOR LOVER, NOR RELATIVE<-
<note>original Latin: "NEC MARITVS NEC AMATOR NEC NECESSARIVS"</note>
->NEITHER MOURNING, NOR REJOICING, NOR WEEPING;<-
<note>original Latin: "NEQVE MOERENS NEQVE GAVDENS NEQVE FLENS"</note>

He was "Not Mourning," moreover, but rather playing with this <term>Epitaph</term><note>A short text honoring a deceased person, strictly speaking, "on a tomb."</note>; "Not Rejoicing," because of the lost hope of <term>succession</term><note>In a legal sense, inheriting the property or status of the deceased.</note>; "Not Weeping," because it is a light thing—as Petrarch wrote in *Remedies for Both Fortunes*, book 2, dialogue 16—*to lose a bride, though not so a wife; for the loss of hope is lighter than the loss of the thing itself*;
<note>original Latin: "sponsam ammittere, non ita coniugem, est enim levior spei jactura, quam rei"</note>

->THIS<-
->NEITHER MOUND, NOR PYRAMID, NOR TOMB,<-
<note>original Latin: "NEC MOLEM NEC PYRAMIDEM NEC SEPVLCRVM"</note>

but a simple and pure Inscription, to be placed duly and at his own discretion upon a <term>Mound</term><note>The original "Molem" refers to a massive structure or heap of stones used as a monument.</note>, a Pyramid, or a Tomb; and thus it is fit to represent "All things"—that is, any of the aforementioned structures alternatively; and so:

->BUT ALL<-
->HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT TO WHOM HE HAS PLACED IT;<-
<note>original Latin: "SCIT ET NESCIT CVI POSVERIT"</note>

because he indeed knows the name which, derived from the parents, ought to have been given to this offspring; yet he knows it not, because she had not yet been gifted with that same name.

This man, however, was the true and unique author of this Inscription...

<summary>The author explains the paradoxical identity of the riddle's dedicator, Lucius Agatho Priscius, who cannot be a husband or heir to a fetus that never lived. The inscription is described as a versatile text that could belong to any type of monument.</summary>
<keywords>Lucius Agatho Priscius, Petrarch, epitaph, succession, paradox, miscarriage, tomb types</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>Continuing the legal and philosophical analysis of Agatho's intent from page 46, where the author argued that the monument’s creator was not mourning a literal death but rather playing with the paradoxes of hope and loss.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header># SHE WHO WAS NOT BORN RISING AGAIN, ETC.
<note>original Latin: "Non nata Resurgens &c."</note></header>

...the sense of the most ancient author. I think there is no reason to doubt the sincere intention of our Agatho, provided that—as I have advised elsewhere—we set aside the <term>ideal speculations</term><note>Abstract or overly complex theories that ignore the literal meaning of the text.</note> of others and resolve to persist in our plain and genuine meaning of the words. In this way, these obscure words say only as much as they sound, just as we are commanded by the law. I say this whenever we wish to attend to nothing else in them but the common and frequent usage of speech, following the <term>Jurist</term><note>A professional expert in law; the author cites several famous Roman legal authorities here.</note> in the law of *Labeo* (joined with law 3 of the <term>Digest</term><note>The *Digest* or *Pandects* (often abbreviated as "ff.") was a massive collection of legal opinions by Roman jurists compiled under Emperor Justinian.</note> regarding the legacy of household goods); or when we seek the more obvious meaning with **Ulpian**; or the same with **Marcellus**, **Proculus**, **Paul**, **Julian**, and others. Finally, we should seek the authentic or proper interpretation. According to the aforementioned laws and the <term>Doctors of Law</term><note>Learned commentators on legal texts.</note>, only these three things should be considered regarding the words.

For to abandon the aforementioned custom of speaking, the more fitting significance, and the propriety of speech when understanding words and interpreting discourse is to abandon one's "Good Spirit" <note>The "Genius" or guiding spirit.</note> only to cling to a depraved one. The most learned **Bocchius** <note>Achille Bocchi (1488–1562), a humanist from Bologna known for his book of "Emblems" which combined poetry and moral philosophy.</note> noted this in his *Emblems*, regarding the principle that *words must be adapted to things, and no violence should be done to what has been discovered*:

> The best words clearly stick to things for the most part,
> And are thus seen by their very own light.
> Yet, as if they were hidden and always receding,
> We search for them and seek them from other places—
> Things which kind Nature commands to be ready for us.
> What do you seek? To do this is to violate the Good Spirit.
> The best things are not summoned at all; they are most like the truth.
> The worst things stand in the way of the sincere and the simple.
<note>original Latin: "Optima nimirum rebus plerumque cohaerent..."</note>

Therefore, let us not depart from this sincere, simple, and "domestic" <note>An explanation that is straightforward or "close to home" rather than far-fetched.</note> explanation of ours. Rather, let us support it with an argument drawn from the custom of the Nations. For it sometimes happens (as...

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis <note>A famous enigmatic inscription found in Bologna, which has puzzled scholars for centuries with its contradictory descriptions of a person who was "neither man, woman, nor hermaphrodite."</note></header>

# LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS

The names of that enigmatic inscriber, who—since he was unable to marry this woman who was not born, to love her who was not living, or to succeed her who was not dead <note>Referring to the inheritance rights of a child who died in the womb or at birth.</note>, and consequently could neither grieve, nor rejoice, nor weep over her passing—therefore [wrote]:

->NEITHER HUSBAND, NOR LOVER, NOR RELATIVE<-
->NEITHER MOURNING, NOR REJOICING, NOR WEEPING;<-

Moreover, he is not mourning, but rather playing with words in this epitaph; he is not rejoicing, because of the lost hope of an inheritance; and he is not weeping, because it is a minor thing, as **Petrarch** <note>Francesco Petrarca (1304–1374), the famous Italian Renaissance poet and scholar.</note> wrote in *Remedies for Both Fortunes*, Book 2, Dialogue 16: *To lose a bride is not the same as losing a wife; for the loss of a hope is lighter than the loss of the reality;* <note>original Latin: "sponsam ammittere, non ita conjugem, est enim levior spei jactura, quam rei"</note>

->THIS<-
->NEITHER MOUND, NOR PYRAMID, NOR TOMB,<-

...but a simple and pure inscription, to be appropriately and at his own discretion applied to a mound, a pyramid, or a tomb alike; and thus it is fit to represent "All Things"—that is, any one of the aforementioned options alternatively; and so:

->BUT ALL THINGS<-
->HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT FOR WHOM HE HAS PLACED IT;<-

...because he certainly knows the name which, derived from the parents, ought to have been given to this offspring; yet he "knows it not," because she had not yet been formally gifted with that name <note>In Roman custom, a name was officially bestowed on a specific day after birth; if the child never reached that day, the name was technically never "given."</note>.

That this man was the true and sole author of this inscription...

<header>She who was not born, Rising again, etc.</header>
<meta>catchword: and he himself</meta>

...of this most ancient meaning; and I believe there is no reason to doubt the sincere intention of our **Agatho** <note>Lucius Agatho Priscius, the name inscribed as the author of the riddle.</note>, provided that we set aside the idealistic speculations of others (as I have warned elsewhere) and resolve to persist in the bare and genuine meaning of the words. In this way, these obscure words say only as much as they sound, just as we are commanded by the law. We should, I say, follow the **Jurisconsult** <term>Jurisconsult (I.C.); a professional legal scholar in Ancient Rome whose opinions carried the weight of law.</term> in the law regarding **Labeo** <note>Marcus Antistius Labeo, a famous Roman jurist of the Augustan age.</note> joined with the third law of the *Digest* regarding the legacy of household goods <note>original Latin: "l. Labeo juncta l. 3. ff. de supel. leg." This refers to the Pandects or Digest of Justinian, the foundation of Roman civil law.</note>. We must attend to nothing in these words except the common and frequent usage of speech.

Following **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), one of the great Roman jurists.</note> in the law "Whenever in stipulations," we should seek the most ready and obvious meaning; with **Marcellus** in "Not otherwise"; with **Proculus** in "Grandson to Proculus"; with **Paulus** in "If it is said" at the beginning of the law on evictions; with **Julian** in "Thus wounded" in the Aquilian law; and with the same **Julian** in the law "Likewise the Praetor" regarding natural and legal heirs, and others. Finally, we must seek the authentic or proper interpretation. According to the aforementioned laws and the **Doctors of Law**, only three things should be considered regarding words:

1. The common custom of speaking.
2. The most suitable meaning.
3. The propriety of the expression.

To abandon these in understanding words or interpreting speech is to abandon one's "good genius" <note>The *Genius* was a tutelary spirit in Roman religion; here it refers to one's natural good sense or guiding intelligence.</note> only to cling to a depraved one. The most learned **Bocchius** <note>Achille Bocchi (1488–1562), a Bolognese humanist who published a famous book of emblems.</note> observed this in his *Emblems*, regarding the principle that *words must be accommodated to things, and no violence should be done to what has been discovered*, writing thus:

> The best things, of course, usually cling to the facts themselves,
> And are thus seen clearly by their own light.
> Yet we search for them as if they were hidden, or always receding,
> And we seek them from other, distant places,
> Even though kind Nature commands them to be ready at hand for us.
> What are you seeking? This is to violate the good Spirit.
> The best things are those least summoned by force, being most like the truth;
> The worst things only stand in the way of what is pure and simple.

Let us not, therefore, depart from this sincere, simple, and domestic explanation of ours. Rather, let us support it with an argument drawn from the customs of Nations: for it happens sometimes (that which...

# LUCIUS AGATHO PRISCIUS.

These are the names of that enigmatic Inscriber, who—since he was unable to marry this girl who was never born, nor love her who was not living, nor succeed her who was not dead, and consequently could neither mourn, nor rejoice, nor weep for her passing—therefore [is]:

->THIS<-
->WHICH IS NEITHER MONUMENT NOR PYRAMID NOR TOMB,<-

but a simple and pure Inscription, to be appropriately placed at his discretion upon either a Monument, a Pyramid, or a Tomb; and thus it is fit to represent "All Things," that is, any of the aforementioned options alternatively; and so:

->BUT ALL THINGS<-
->HE KNOWS AND KNOWS NOT TO WHOM HE HAS PLACED IT;<-

because he knows indeed the name which, derived from the parents, ought to have been given to this child at birth; yet he knows it not, because she had never actually been granted that name.

That this was the true and unique [intent] of this Inscriber...

I believe there is no reason to doubt the meaning of the most ancient author, nor the sincere intention of our Agatho. # Aelia Laelia Crispis.

And as **Carerius** <note>Ludovicus Carerius, a 16th-century Italian jurist.</note> <note>original Latin: "de Sponsal. lib. 2. cap. 4." referring to his work *On Betrothals*.</note> himself noted, and **Alciatus** <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), a renowned legal humanist.</note> <note>original Latin: "de legib. Connub." referring to his work *On Marriage Laws*.</note> mentions throughout his work, family lineages joined by bonds of friendship are bound even tighter through these decreed betrothals of children and these prescribed marriages of descendants. By this "handing over" of women (where affection turns into a family alliance), goodwill and harmony are extended and propagated to future generations. And just as the **Spartans** and **Messenians** <note>Two rival powers in ancient Greece; the author refers to historical attempts to create peace through intermarriage.</note> were accustomed to exchanging their own maidens to foster this same harmony, so too did the Roman Nobles give their sisters, daughters, and granddaughters to one another as wives to foster such alliances. Most majestic examples can be drawn from the ancient Emperors, such as **Caesar** betrothing **Julia** to **Pompey**, or **Mark Antony** joining **Claudia** to **Octavian** <note>Octavian later became the Emperor Augustus; this marriage was a political attempt to stabilize the Second Triumvirate.</note>. From these, it is argued that the same spirit existed between the **Laelian** and **Agathonian** families; namely, that the long-standing faith of mutual friendship between the fathers might be extended through a marriage entered into by these children.

However, because these events are shrouded in the mists of antiquity—granting us the freedom to speculate and a desire to reach the truth—I would consider it not improbable that this occurred solely from the generosity of **Laelius**. Perhaps he was bound by some kindness toward this Agathonian family, or was moved by the beauty, integrity, and virtue of this **Agatho**, or perhaps he even took pity on his meager wealth. While still living, he may have decided to offer this unborn **Laelia** to him, or left instructions in his **will** <note>original Latin: "testamento"</note> for her to be betrothed to him upon his death. 

But of all the conjectures that come to mind, I believe the following is to be preferred: that innate love and common desire to perpetuate one's own **Clan** <note>original Latin: "Gens"</note> by any means possible. Therefore, if male offspring is lacking, any female child is given in marriage to someone who will take upon himself the name of the father-in-law (which would otherwise be extinguished with him) and transmit it to the grandsons to be born, so that it remains forever. This is the theory that pleases us most: that Laelius chose Agatho as his son-in-law through this unborn daughter, so that he might bolster nature through art <note>A philosophical concept of using human systems (like law or marriage) to fix a "failure" of nature (the lack of a male heir).</note>, while this

# Non-born Resurrecting, etc.
### G

...by inviting this foreign successor to himself, he might avoid the collapse of a declining family through this clever supplement. To express this, I will use the most elegant words of the highly eloquent Father Petiot, written once for the birth-celebration of our invincible King **Louis the Great** <note>Louis XIV of France (1638–1715).</note>.

Indeed, we have heard of this being done a thousand times by many people. For this reason, **Honorius** <note>Western Roman Emperor (r. 393–423 AD).</note> seems to rightly complain in the works of **Claudian** <note>Claudius Claudianus, a court poet.</note> against **Stilicho** <note>A high-ranking Roman general and regent.</note> regarding the delayed marriage to **Maria**, which had long been owed to him:

> *Stilicho, I confess I have asked for much: but certainly I, a Prince born of a Prince, deserve this; he who bound you to him as a son-in-law through his brother’s offspring. To whom you owe Maria, fulfill your father’s pact for me.* <note>original Latin: "Stilico fateor, nec pauca poposci: / Sed certè mereor Princeps hoc Principe natus, / Qui sibi te generum fraterna prole revinxit. / Cui Mariam debes, fœdus mihi solve paternum."</note>

And a little earlier:

> *I do not snatch headlong the treaties of another's wedding torch, but those which were long ago promised to me as a bride, and left by my father's commands.* <note>original Latin: "Non rapio præceps alienæ fœdera tædæ, / Sed quæ sponsa mihi pridem, Patrisque relicta / Mandatis."</note>

Nor are examples lacking from ancient or modern times, gathered from every side: 

From **Numerius Otacilius Maleventanus**, who handed over his very wealthy daughter to **Quintus Fabius**, the sole survivor (after three hundred and six members of that family were killed by the Etruscans) <note>A reference to the legendary near-extinction of the Fabian clan at the Battle of the Cremera in 477 BC.</note>, on the condition that he give the name "Numerius" to the first son he fathered. 

From **Appius Claudius** and his wife **Antistia**, who were both eagerly striving to give their daughter **Claudia** in marriage to **Tiberius Gracchus**, who was exceptionally dear to them. 

From **Theodoric**, King of the Ostrogoths, who was so captivated by the wonderful character of **Eutharic** that he adopted him as a son-in-law through his only daughter, **Amalasuntha**. 

From **Getia**, King of Pannonia, who was so struck by the brilliance of the virtues of **Otho Urseolo** <note>Ottone Orseolo (992–1032), Doge of Venice.</note> that he gave him his daughter in marriage. 

From **Nicolò Priuli**, moved by such great magnanimity toward **Francesco Foscari** <note>Francesco Foscari (1373–1457), the longest-reigning Doge of Venice.</note> that he joined his daughter to this patrician—who possessed by no means a large fortune—with a royal dowry, and by this same means successfully promoted him to the Dukedom of the Venetians. 

Among our own people, by the Count and Senator **Filippo Aldovrandi**, who in his final will made provisions for a child yet to be born; for if a daughter were born, [she should be married] to the Count and Senator Henricus...
