# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...that she should be given in marriage to Herculanus, who was very dear to him. Finally, by Francesco de' Vecchi (to pass over others), who likewise died among us without children, leaving only a pregnant wife; he decreed in his will that if a son were born, he should succeed as heir to all his goods; but if a daughter, she should receive the entirety of the property as a dowry, provided she married Enea de' Bonzi at the proper time, yet in such a way that he would be bound to take up the family's heraldic shield <term>gentilitiam tesseram: a coat of arms or family insignia</term> as well as the surname of the testator and father-in-law.

Furthermore, those things which the studious reader could extract from the ancient lawgivers agree with these sorts of final dispositions protected by such conditions. For the same thing will immediately occur to him in the works of **Gaius** <note>A celebrated Roman jurist of the 2nd century AD.</note> in the second book on testamentary trusts, recorded in the law *Facta*, the section beginning "if under a condition," at the line: *If indeed it is a condition of carrying the name.* <note>original Latin: "Si verò nominis ferendi conditio est."</note> And also in *if you had sworn that you would carry my name.* <note>original Latin: "si jurasses te nomen meum laturum."</note> This is also supported by **Alciatus** <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), an Italian jurist and founder of the legal humanism school.</note> in the fifth of his *Connubial Laws*, page 100, number 27, where he states: *Many wills appear in which legacies were left on the condition that the legatees would carry the names and arms of the testator.* <note>original Latin: "Plura, afferens, testamenta videri, in quibus legata relinquebantur ea lege, ut legatarij ferrent nomina & arma testatoris."</note> He demonstrates that even philosophers were sometimes moved by this same desire to propagate their own name, using the example of **Lyco** <note>Lyco of Troas, a Peripatetic philosopher.</note>, who wrote the following in his will among other things: *What property I have in the City and in Aegina, I give to my brother Lyco especially, because he carries our name.* <note>original Latin: "Quae in Urbe & Aegina habeo, Lyconi fratri praecipuo do, quòd is nomen ferat nostrum."</note> To this is added the very well-known decree of **Augustus Caesar**, who appointed his primary heirs, **Tiberius** (to one half and one sixth part) and **Livia** (to one third part), whom he also ordered to carry his name, etc. Nor is the response of King Latinus to the ambassadors of Aeneas in the 7th book of the *Aeneid* <note>Virgil’s epic poem concerning the founding of Rome.</note> dissimilar:

> Now report my commands back to your King:
> I have a daughter, whom the oracles from my father’s shrine
> and many signs from Heaven do not allow me to join
> to a husband of our own people. They prophesy that sons-in-law
> will arrive from foreign shores (this, they say, remains for Latium)
> who, by their blood, our name... <note>original Latin: "Vos contra Regi mea nunc mandata referte: / Est mihi nata, viro Gentis quam jungere nostrae / Non patrio ex adyto sortes, non plurima Coelo / Monstra sinunt, Generos externis affore ab oris / (Hoc Latio restare canunt) qui sanguine nostrum / Nomen"</note>

# The Unborn Woman Rising Again, etc.

<column-break/>

> Let them bear his name to the stars: I believe that the fates demand him,
> And if my mind augurs any truth, I desire it.
<note>A modified quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, emphasizing the importance of carrying on a name and legacy.</note>

->G 2<-
<meta>catchword: matri-</meta>

For although such final wills might seem to oppose the freedom of contracting marriages, they have always been permitted; because through them, men are said to be enticed into marriage not by fear of punishment, but rather by the hope of profit. Thus, we have seen it decided many times by the ancient <term>Jurisconsults: legal experts whose written opinions carried the weight of law</term> under this distinction. It was decided by **Gaius** in the law *When a legacy is so left* in the Digest on conditions and demonstrations. By **Papinian** in the law *To Titius a hundred*, section one, and in the law *To the woman and Titius* in the same section. In the law *He who from the brothers* in the Digest on the institution of conditions. In the law *To Titia if she does not marry*, and in the law *The father to Severina* in the Digest on conditions and demonstrations. By **Ulpian** in the law *As often as*, the section *if anyone thus*, the first, in the Digest on the institution of heirs; in the law *This condition*, and in the law *When it shall have been* in the Digest on conditions and demonstrations. By **Marcellus** in the law *Which of the two* in the Digest on the institution of conditions. By **Pomponius** in the law *If to my female slave* in the Digest on conditions and demonstrations, and by others. For those most wise legislators recognized how consistent with the law—and indeed how useful and beneficial to the public good and the entire Commonwealth—these last and posthumous liberalities <note>Acts of generosity or gifts specified in a will.</note> would be. Through these, not only is love spread and families preserved for eternity (as we have previously noted), but even ancient disagreements are settled, and the funeral torches of discord are extinguished as the sacred torches of **Hymen** <note>Hymen was the Greek god of marriage; his torches symbolize the wedding ceremony</note> approach.

Thus peace was once entered into between **Alexander III** of the Scots and **Magnus** [VI], King of the Norwegians, on the condition that **Margaret**, daughter of Alexander—hardly a year old—should be joined in marriage to **Eric** <note>The original text uses "Horgofano," a Latinized reference to Eric II of Norway, son of Magnus VI</note>, the son of Magnus, who was still occupying his cradle, once both had reached the proper age. Thus the Emperor **Frederick I** [Barbarossa], after confirming peace with King **William** of Sicily, sought his cousin **Constance**—to whom the Kingdom of Sicily was to devolve—as a wife for his son **Henry** [VI], and obtained her without difficulty. Finally, so that we do not wander too far afield, and to touch upon recent events in our own century, through these...

# Chapter IV.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring floral motifs and vines within a square frame.</image-desc>Just as iron is sharpened by iron, a nail is driven by a nail, and a diamond is polished and broken by a diamond, so too, once the internal and somewhat obscure mind of Agatho <note>Agatho is the name traditionally attributed to the author of the enigma</note> has been revealed, it clearly clears away and cuts through the external and imperceptible collection of words in which the truth was wrapped. For since our ingenious architect of this Eulogy <note>original: *Elogii Structor*</note> decided to raise a funeral mound for this Laelia—a woman he desired in vain—he consequently and necessarily intended, using those same words which are both highly artistic and highly obscure, to present us with a true Sepulchral Inscription <note>original: *Inscriptionem Sepulcralem*</note>. This was conceived enigmatically, to be sure, but it is not a simple Enigma, as it has been considered and published by all others until now; for it consists of all those members or parts that are entirely required to compose a true and perfect Inscription, and which refer to two main headings to be examined here in succession: namely, the Person in whose honor it is inscribed, and the Person by whose command it is placed. Therefore, the first will be dealt with in the present chapter, and the second in the following.

And first, regarding the Person in whose memory the Stone is seen to be inscribed, six customary qualities occur—though not all are always necessary—which are: Name, Sex,

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the mysterious "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription. # Aelia Laelia Crispis.

->Sex, Age, Condition, Death, and Location.<-

The name is that already praised triple form: *Aelia Laelia Crispis*. AELIA LAELIA is formed in the same manner as found in Gruterus <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627) was a scholar famous for his massive collection of ancient Roman inscriptions</note> on page 409, Inscription 6: FIRMIA FIRMA. Page 730, 4: GRATIA GRATA. Page 839, 8: VERRIA VERVLA. Page 1179, 14: SEVERA SEVERIA, and others of the same pattern; and this is because of the harmony or conformity of the surname with the name, which was not unpleasing to the Ancients. Indeed, there is a certain repetition of the same word here, with the addition of a small letter or syllable, which the Latins call redundancy or abundance, and the Greeks call <term>pleonasmus</term> <note>original: *πλεόνασμος*; a rhetorical device using more words than necessary for emphasis</note>. Or finally, and perhaps more truly, it is because her father was called Aelius Laelius; from whom, among the Ancients, not only sons but daughters—especially those unmarried—were usually named after the father; as in inscription 445, 2: OCTAVIA GRACILLA from T. OCTAVIUS GRACILLUS her father. 75, 8: FLAVIA MODESTINA from her father FLAVIO MODESTO. 42, 3: AEMILIA MATERNA from L. EMILIO MATERNO her parent, and so with countless others.

The name CRISPIS was added, perhaps from CRISPA, or from a mother named CRISPIS; since offspring sometimes took a name combined from both the father and the mother simultaneously; as seen below on page 190: CERVARIVS THALAMVS from CERVARIA and THALAMO. Thus in Gruterus they report a name taken from AVRELIVS PERSIDIANVS the father and AELIA ADIECTA the mother: AVRELIA ADIECTA PERSIS (1131, 1). From L. VARIVS FAVSTVS the father and AELIA HILARA the mother: P. VARIVS HILARVS the son (1036, 1). From C. ANTONIVS AGATHOPVS the father and ANTONIA AMPLIATA the mother: CN. ANTONIVS AMPLIATVS the son (668, 12). And specifically in Bologna <note>original: *Bononiae*</note>, from Alidosius <note>Nicolò Alidosi was a 17th-century historian who documented the nobility and monuments of Bologna</note> page 78, 2: from T. MARCIVS PAVLINVS the father and CREPERIA SABINA the mother: T. MARCIVS SABINIANVS the son. For to suspect that the stonecutter here made an error in the original draft <note>original: *authographo*</note>, and carved CRISPIS in place of PRISCAE or PRISCIS, would perhaps be too slight a conjecture; especially

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

->GAIUS CORNELIUS, FULLONIA CORNELIA<-
->GAIUS HERMIA (FREEDMAN OF LUCIUS), OFFICIOSA (FREEDWOMAN OF SALVIUS), PRISCA (FREEDWOMAN OF GAIUS)<-
<note>original: "C. CORNELIVS. FVLLONIA. CORNELIA / C. L. HERMIA. SAL. L. OFFICIOSA. C. L. PRISCA". These are names from a Roman funerary inscription, likely another example the author found in Bologna to compare the name "Prisca" or "Priscus" to the "Crispis" of the riddle.</note>

Indeed, to wish to investigate whether the same Agatho <note>A scholar or figure previously discussed in the text</note> wrote *CRISPIS* in place of *PRISCIS*, so that by this anagram of his own surname, the same surname—to be shared with Aelia Laelia, his fiancée—might be hidden and obscured, is merely to quibble and perhaps to prophesy far too boldly.

Whatever the case may be—for in examining these ancient names (to speak at least as elegantly as the most learned Chimentellius <note>Valerio Chimentelli (1620–1668), an Italian philologist and professor of Greek</note> in his *On the Honor of the Bisellium*): *we take risks, we guess, we draw lots, in the manner of those who, groping between darkness and light, crawl rather than walk*—let us say with a surer and more plain confidence in our conjecture that "Aelia Laelia Crispis" presents us with a first name, a family name, and a surname. *Aelia* acts as the first name <term>praenomen: the personal name that precedes the family name</term> in this instance, and it will be noted below in Chapter 9 how it was able to serve this function here, even though it is commonly placed and accepted as a family name. In truth, it is a name of a Clan <term>nomen Gentis: the name shared by all members of a Roman family or "gens"</term>, and indeed the same as *Ailia*, *Alia*, or even *Halia*, as the celebrated Patinus <note>Charles Patin (1633–1693), a French physician and numismatist</note> infers from a new coin in his work on Ursinus's *Plebeian Families of Rome*. It was certainly a plebeian family, which is why Martial, in the twentieth epigram of his first book, did not hesitate to joke about it (or at least the name is not far removed) in this way:

> *If I remember, Aelia, you had four teeth:*
> *One cough spat out two, and another spat out two;*
<note>original: "Si memini, fuerant tibi quatuor, Aelia, dentes: / Expuit una duos tussis, & una duos;" — Martial, Epigrams 1.20. Martial uses the name Aelia to mock an old woman who has lost all her teeth.</note>

But it is nevertheless an ancient name and of illustrious reputation due to the distinguished magisterial offices held by its members. For this reason, Horace writes in the seventeenth Ode of his third book:

> *Noble Aelius, descended from ancient Lamus*
> (*Since they say both the earlier Lamiae were*
> *Named from him, and all the race of descendants*
> *Through the mindful records of the family)*
> *You derive your origin from that founder,*
> *Who is said [to have first held] the walls of Formiae*
<note>original: "Aeli vetusto nobilis ab Lamo..." — Horace, Odes 3.17. Horace is addressing his friend Aelius Lamia, attributing to him a grand lineage stretching back to the mythical founder of the city of Formiae.</note>

<summary>The author dismisses the idea that the name "Crispis" is a mere anagram for "Priscus" and begins a genealogical and philological analysis of the name "Aelia." He cites classical poets Martial and Horace to illustrate both the plebeian and noble associations of the Aelian name.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Agatho, Roman naming conventions, praenomen, nomen gentis, Martial, Horace, genealogy, philology, Bologna inscriptions</keywords>

### Rising again without being born, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." – a reference to the enigmatic nature of the subject of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle.</note>

> Prince, and to have held the Liris,
> flowing through the shores of Marica,
> 
> a ruler far and wide ——————
<note>This is a quotation from the Roman poet Horace (Odes 3.17), addressing Aelius Lamia. The author uses it to trace the noble lineage of the families he is about to list.</note>

From this source, therefore, many families emerged, such as the Cati, the Galli, the aforementioned Lamiae, the Ligures, the Paeti, the Stolones, and the Tuberones.
Laelia is truly a name—a <term>gentilitium: a Roman name belonging to a specific "gens" or clan</term>—and although no mention of it is made by Ursinus <note>Fulvio Orsini (1529–1600), a famous Renaissance humanist and antiquarian who studied ancient names.</note>, the marble inscriptions testify to it sufficiently. This is especially true of those found primarily in our own regions, so much so that Verona alone—as reported by Abbot Ferretti in his *Museum of Stones* (page 92) and Count Muscardo in his *Museum* (page 393)—boasts one or another:

->TO LAELIA CLEMENTINA, AN INCOMPARABLE WIFE.<-
->Q. F. SECUNDUS, HER HUSBAND, FOR HIMSELF AND FOR HER.<-
->YOU LIE HERE DECEASED, BUT YOU LIVE AND SHALL LIVE FOR YOUR SECUNDUS;<-
->LAELIA, YOUR GOOD DEEDS OUGHT NOT TO DIE.<-
->I PRAY TO YOU, EARTH, AND TO THE HOLY SPIRITS OF THE DEAD ON BEHALF OF MY SPOUSE:<-
->MAY YOU DEPART PEACEFULLY; YOU, EARTH, SHALL COVER HER BONES LIGHTLY.<-
->SHE LIVED 30 YEARS, 5 MONTHS, 10 DAYS.<-

->MARCUS LAELIUS<-
->LAELIANUS<-
->VETERAN OF THE 8TH PRAETORIAN COHORT<-
->FOR HIMSELF AND<-
->FOR AIA<-
->PROCLA<-
->ORDERED THIS TO BE MADE WHILE LIVING<- <note>original: "V. F. I." (Vivus Fieri Iussit), a common Roman funerary abbreviation.</note>

<summary>The author argues that "Laelia" is a genuine Roman family name, citing historical lineage from Horace and archaeological evidence from funerary inscriptions found in Verona.</summary>
<keywords>Laelia, gentilitium, Roman inscriptions, Verona, Horace, Aelius Lamia, epitaph, genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Laelia," arguing for its status as a noble Roman family name through archaeological evidence and literary satire. It concludes by analyzing the grammatical structure of the name "Crispis" to determine its origin and form.</meta>

Furthermore, such evidence is easily available everywhere, but especially for those seeking out funerary stones in Rome. As the same Ferretti <note>Abbot Gabriele Ferretti, a 17th-century scholar of ancient inscriptions.</note> observes in the same passage of his notes, this same Clan—from which the wise man <note>Caius Laelius Sapiens (c. 188 BCE), a Roman statesman and philosopher.</note> emerged—and to which he adds Balbus, was of Consular rank in Rome; though it was of Plebeian origin, it was nonetheless not unequal to such high dignity. I infer this from this stone inscribed to Marcus Laelius Laelianus, a man to whom it would not have been fitting to entrust the prefecture of the eighth cohort unless he had shown himself to be of noble birth—if we place our trust in Vegetius’s <note>Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus, a late Roman author of a famous treatise on military matters.</note> *On Military Matters* on this point. Martial <note>A Roman poet known for his witty and biting epigrams.</note> may mock his "Laelia" as he pleases, and spit these two verses upon her as if she were a commoner:

> *Laelia, because she married you, Quinctus, for the sake of the law,* <note>original: "Quae legis causa nupsit tibi Laelia Quincto." A pun on "legis" (law) and "legitima" (lawful wife).</note>
> *You can call this woman a "lawful" wife.*

> *You use bought teeth and hair, and you feel no shame.*
> *What will you do about your eye, Laelia? That cannot be bought.* <note>original: "Dentibus, atque comis... Quid facies oculo, Laelia? non emitur."</note>

*Crispis* is finally a <term>cognomen: a Roman third name or surname</term>, and indeed it is in the <term>nominative case: the subject form of a noun</term> rather than an <term>oblique case: cases other than the nominative</term>. Thus it is declined *Crispis—Crispidis*, not *Crispus—a—um*. It seems it could hardly be correctly constructed today without a preposition, whereas we commonly speak of the "de' Pepoli," the "de' Malvezzi," the "de' Bentivogli," or the "de' Gozzadini" families. However, the surname *Crispus—a—um*, or *Crispus—i*, and even *Crispa—ae* occurs frequently, as seen in Puccinelli <note>Placido Puccinelli (1609–1685), an Italian monk and historian.</note> in the *Ancient Memoirs of Milan*, page 12.

### TO P. ATTILIUS CRISPUS. <note>AD. likely stands for ADIVTORI (helper) or similar abbreviation in the original inscription.</note>

->In Apianus & Amantius, *Sacred Ancient Inscriptions*, page 430. 2.<-

### OF RAECIUS, WILLINGLY AND DESERVEDLY FULFILLED HIS VOW. <note>original: "V. S. L. M." stands for "Votum Solvit Libens Merito," a standard formula on Roman votive altars.</note>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis. <note>While the header refers to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis," a famous enigmatic inscription from Bologna, the actual text below records various Roman epitaphs for individuals named Crispus.</note>

CRISPUS, MOST CHARMING SON, ALAS, ALAS! ORCUS HAS DEVOURED YOU; <note>original: "ORCVS" — In Roman mythology, Orcus was a god of the underworld, often used as a synonym for Death itself.</note> HE HAS SNATCHED ALL DELIGHT FROM ME, THE STAFF OF MY TOOTHLESS OLD AGE NOW THAT MY MARROW IS DRAINED. HE HAS CUT SHORT THE HOPE OF GRANDCHILDREN AND DRAGGED AWAY THE GREATEST PART OF MY LIFE WITH HIM. IN SUCH GREAT BEREAVEMENT, I REMAIN SO DESOLATED THAT, HAD THE SPIRITS OF THE DEPARTED—WHO FEEL OTHERWISE—NOT FORBIDDEN IT, THEY WOULD HAVE BURIED ME ALIVE WITH YOU. HE LIVED TWELVE YEARS. CRISPUS, HIS MOST UNHAPPY FATHER, MADE THIS FOR HIMSELF, FOR HIS FREEDMEN AND FREEDWOMEN, AND FOR THEIR DESCENDANTS.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the masculine name "Crispus" to its feminine counterparts "Crispa" and "Crispis." # Not born, rising again, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens" — This refers to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle, which begins by describing the subject as someone who is "not born" but "rising again."</note>

->HAD HE LEFT HER IN MARRIAGE,<-
->HE WOULD NOT HAVE SHED SO MANY TEARS IN DEATH.<-
->CRISPA, HIS WIFE.<-

And in the works of Ausonius, in the *Epigram to Crispa*: <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD) was a Roman poet and teacher from Bordeaux.</note>
> *Some say you are ugly, Crispa; but I*
> *do not know it: to me you are beautiful; in my judgment that is enough.*

And in the *Portrait of Crispa*:
> *And though a capital luxury scorched the women of Nola,* <note>Nola is an ancient city in Campania, Italy.</note>
> *Crispa nevertheless practices every vice in a single body, etc.*

It must be noted, therefore, regarding *Crispis* (genitive *Crispidis*), that this surname was found to have occasionally crept into use for males in ancient times. Indeed, in Rome, in the Transtyberine district, <note>term: Transtyberina — the "Trastevere" region of Rome, located across the Tiber river.</note> it was read as follows on the wall of a small house, according to the witness of Mazzocchi in *Ancient Inscriptions of the City*, page 52, on the reverse: <note>Giacomo Mazzocchi was a 16th-century Italian publisher and antiquarian known for documenting Roman inscriptions.</note>

->SACRED TO THE SPIRITS OF THE DEPARTED<- <note>original: "D M S" — Dis Manibus Sacrum.</note>
->TO GAIUS MARIUS EUTACTUS. TITUS FLAVIUS CRISPIS<-
->MADE THIS FOR HIS DEAREST FRIEND.<-

Thus, Laelia Crispis is named in the same manner as AELIA PARTHENIS, CLAUDIA HELPIS, PUBLIA HELPIS, ANNIA CYPRIS, CLAUDIA PANNICHIS, DOMITIA PIERIS, and INSIDIA CHRYSIS found in the same Mazzocchi, from whom alone these and other surnames of the same measure, inflection, or termination are provided: JULIA ELPIS, STACCIA HELPIS, CONSIDIA HELPIS, TREBIA SOTERIS, AVIDIA SOTERIS, JULIA PYRALLIS, SAPPIA PIRALLIS, TEUTIA ELEUTHERIS, ULP[IA] ANTIOCHIS, CLODIA DAPHNIS, CLAUDIA LEONIS, CLAUDIA PHOTIS, ATTILIA MOSCHIS, JULIA PIERIS, JULIA THAIS, JULIA CHRISTIANTIS, CALPHURNIA ATHIS, HELVIA NEREIS, and DARDIA CYTHERIS. <note>These names all follow a Greek-influenced naming convention where the surname ends in "-is," common for women and freed slaves in Rome.</note>

It argues that the name "Laelia Crispis" follows a standard naming pattern for women in antiquity, citing numerous examples from the epigraphic collections of Giacomo Mazzocchi.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of names ending in "-is" to justify the feminine gender of "Aelia Laelia Crispis." It moves from epigraphic evidence of similar names to a philosophical and biological analysis of the "Bologna Enigma," arguing that the subject's gender ambiguity suggests a premature fetus or embryo.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...RIS. **CASSIA MOSCHIS. CATILIA THETIS. DUODINA CYNTERIS. SABAVDIA DORIS.** VITULINA TYRANNIS. Gruter <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627) was a Dutch philologist and collector of ancient inscriptions whose work was a standard reference for scholars.</note> records countless similar examples, especially those he lists on page 592, line 1; page 809, line 7; and page 1550, line 1. Among these are **AELIA CAENIS, AELIA HYMNIS, AELIA HELPIS**; to these three, it is pleasing to add three others from the many with which the Excellent Lord Gaspare Altieri <note>Gaspare Altieri (1650–1720) was a prominent Roman nobleman and nephew of Pope Clement X, known for his patronage and his villa near the Santa Croce church.</note> adorns his most elegant and magnificent residence, recently built in the vineyard near Santa Croce in Gerusalemme: **LUCCIA HELPIS. FLAVIA ATHENAIS. ULPIA SIGNORIS.** But

SEX, which is always silently and necessarily linked to the name itself, consequently reveals itself immediately by the assignment of that name. For what reason is it added here, where the name itself indicates a feminine gender three times over? If *Aelia*, I say, if *Laelia*, if *Crispis* demonstrate the female sex through their very forms, by what right is sex introduced here anew? And, what is more urgent, why are masculine, feminine, and both together emphasized in this way: *Neither Man, nor Woman, nor Androgynous*? <note>original: "Nec Vir, nec Mulier, nec Androgyna" — these are the famous opening lines of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle.</note> Shall we judge our Inscriber to have been blind, and reproach him for carelessly piling these things together? By no means; rather, I believe it must be concluded that he acted entirely on purpose. After proposing this "Aelia Laelia Crispis" at the beginning—like a white target at which the entire subsequent sequence of difficult and contradictory words is aimed—he immediately offers an "Ariadne’s thread" <note>Ariadne’s thread: a classical metaphor for a clue or logic that leads one out of a complex maze or labyrinth, referencing the myth of the Labyrinth of Crete.</note> with these three following words, which precisely (though indefinitely) connote sex, so that an exit might be found in this Labyrinth. For I most firmly believe that the entire power and strength for dissolving this Enigma lies in this hesitant repetition of a triple sex. For if this offspring—to whom this name would have been granted had it turned out female—could in no way be discerned as being of the female, male, or mixed sex, we must necessarily conclude that it was an <term>embryo</term> <note>original: "ἐμβρύον" (embryo)</note>, whether it broke forth as a violent miscarriage before the fortieth day, or

<summary>The author compares the name "Aelia Laelia Crispis" to other ancient Roman names to establish its feminine form, then argues that the riddle's denial of gender implies the subject was a miscarried embryo whose sex could not be determined.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, epigraphy, Jan Gruter, Gaspare Altieri, Roman names, gender ambiguity, embryo, Bologna Enigma</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression from the previous page's discussion on names and gender. Having established that the name "Aelia Laelia Crispis" is grammatically feminine, the author now addresses the riddle's seemingly contradictory claims about the subject's sex and age by employing Aristotelian philosophy regarding potentiality versus actuality.</meta>

# Not Born, Rising Again, etc.

...shortly after the seventh [month], that concretion of blood fell away—what they call the embryo <term>genitura: literally "begetting," used here to refer to the early-stage fetus or "seed" of a human being</term>. Thus, in the word **ALL**, we understand that in whichever of the three sexes this [identity] is admitted in potential, it is denied to them in fact. Therefore, what was said above is verified: *Neither man, nor woman, nor androgynous*, as was sufficiently demonstrated in the preceding chapter.

**AGE**, on the other hand, is very frequently found on ancient stones. For sex is rarely included, and then only when the name of the deceased is not mentioned, or if it is mentioned, it is honored with some addition, such as **TO A GOOD MAN, TO A WOMAN OF WONDERFUL FAITH, TO A MOST INNOCENT WOMAN, TO AN INCOMPARABLE WOMAN**, etc. Age, however, is not treated the same way; it is most often declared simply through these abbreviated marks: **V.** or **VIX.** or even the full **VIXIT** <note>original: "He/She lived"</note>, with months and sometimes days occasionally added.

Here, however, that threefold state is most skillfully declared, encompassing the beginning, middle, and end of age: namely, **Childhood, Youth, and Old Age**. For since this miscarriage <term>Aborsus: a term used here for a premature birth or fetus that did not survive</term> never saw the light of day for even a single moment, the time she lived could not be precisely defined. Instead, it had to be indefinitely hinted at through those three terms: Puberty, Youth, and Old Age. None of these could be reached by the unborn Laelia, and therefore, as was said above: *Neither Girl, nor Youth, nor Old Woman*. Nevertheless, she could have run through and measured all these spans of age if but a single hour of living had fallen to her, or if she had fulfilled the requirements of maturity or old age. Therefore, according to that common Scholastic distinction of "potential not yet realized into act," she is *But All*.

Indeed, Aelia Laelia lived, and lived a long time, if you consider the wishes of Agatho <note>Agatho is the figure named in the inscription as the one who dedicated the monument to Aelia Laelia Crispis.</note> who sweetly flatters himself. You find in the Poet <note>Referring to the Roman poet Virgil in the *Aeneid*.</note> a boy snatched away by an untimely fate:

> The fates will only show him to the earth, and will not allow him
> To remain longer; <note>original: "Ostendent terris hunc tantum fata, neque ultra / Esse sinent;" — from Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book 6, referring to the young Marcellus.</note>

But there you likewise find his deeds described as if he were an adult:

> Alas for piety, alas for the ancient faith, and a hand invincible in war! <note>original: "Heu pietas, heu prisca fides, invictaque bello"</note>

<summary>The author explains the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle by arguing that as a fetus or "aborted" child, she possessed the potential to be any sex or age, though she technically achieved none. He uses the Scholastic concept of "potentiality vs. actuality" and references Virgil to justify describing a short-lived life in terms of its unfulfilled potential.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Bologna Enigma, potentiality and actuality, Scholasticism, Virgil, epigraphy, Roman inscriptions, embryology, Agatho</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the three states of life—Childhood, Youth, and Old Age—mentioned on the previous page. Having explained that the miscarried fetus could not be strictly assigned an age, the author now moves to the "Condition" or moral state of the subject, specifically focusing on the concepts of modesty and chastity.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

> *With his right hand, no one would have met him with impunity*
> *when he was armed, whether he went on foot against the enemy*
> *or dug his spurs into the flanks of a foaming horse.*
<note>original: "Dextera, non illi quisquam se impune tulisset / Obvius armato, seu cum pedes iret in hostem, / Seu spumantis equi foderet calcaribus armos." — Virgil, Aeneid, Book VI, describing the young Marcellus.</note>

**CONDITION**, or Quality, is declared in these marble epitaphs of the dead even more than Age itself; for it instructs the Reader on the excellence and virtue of the deceased, exalts their name, and commits their fame to eternity, as the poet from Venusia <note>referring to Horace</note> sang:

---
> *—— marbles carved with public inscriptions,*
> *through which the breath and life return to good*
> *leaders after death ——*
<note>original: "—— incisa notis marmora publicis, / Per quae spiritus, & vita redit bonis / Post mortem ducibus ——" — Horace, Odes, IV.8.</note>
---

In our Laelia’s imperfect offspring, however—cast out before its time—what else could be fashioned or imagined besides that which holds the primary place among women, namely Modesty? The guarding of this virtue is the chief crown of the weaker sex, the loss of which harms even a man's dignity and damages his reputation among people. Furthermore, between Modesty [Pudicitia] and Chastity [Castitas] (if indeed they differ from each other, as our Advocate—who insists on three terms for everything—would have it), there is this principal difference: Modesty keeps the soul free from the heat of shameful lusts, while Chastity makes it not only free but even remote from them. Consequently, the former more frequently refers to the fidelity of a wife, while the latter refers to the constancy of a virgin; the one is religious, the other merely moral. 

Therefore, with good reason, I observe this inscription on the reverse side of a coin of Julia Maesa, the grandmother of Elagabalus, in our Museum: **PUDICITIA** <note>Modesty</note>. And in the crafted inscription of **GAPHURA, WIFE OF C. LUTIUS FAUSTUS**, which can be seen at Naples in the Spatafora collection, among other words that clearly fail due to a type of <term>Kakozelia: a Greek rhetorical term for a 'perverse affectation' or a failed attempt to appear clever or stylish</term>, she, asserting herself to be **UNWED**, boasts of being the defender of her unstained **MODESTY**, which had been unsuccessfully tempted so many times. From these examples, the Reader is warned that our Laelia could indeed have been (even though she was none of them) a Virgin, a Wife, and a Prostitute, since it is said: *Neither Chaste, nor Prostitute, nor Modest, but All.*

->DEATH,<-

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, moving from the moral qualities of the subject (Modesty and Chastity) to the nature of her death and the paradoxical location of her remains. # Not Born, Rising Again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c."</note>

**DEATH** itself is also declared to be of three kinds, so that the number three remains unshaken in every single verse here. But indeed, from the countless types of destruction, the Author chose three more noble ones: **Hunger**, by which Sisigambis the mother of Darius <note>original: "Sisigambis Arsamis"</note> perished—or, to stay with the Romans, by which Agrippina, the wife of Germanicus, died; the **Sword** <note>original: "Ferrum," literally "iron," referring to a blade</note>, by which Lucretia the wife of Collatinus and Crispina the wife of Commodus died; and **Poison**, by which Antonia the wife of Drusus and Sabina the wife of Hadrian breathed their last. Thus did the happy and unhappy Agatho <note>Agatho is the pseudonym used by the author of this commentary to refer to himself.</note> console himself, playing seriously, about a wife never "led" <note>In Latin, "ducere uxorem" (to lead a wife) means to marry.</note>—since she was removed by miscarriage before birth—yet lost, since she was promised before conception. He imagined in her a noble and threefold kind of death, by any of which she could have been snatched away; yet, being devoid of life, she was not "taken away" in the usual sense. Therefore, it is aptly written: *Taken away neither by Hunger, nor by Iron, nor by Poison, but by All.*

**PLACE**, finally, in which the corpse is situated and which is usually indicated by the <term>HERE LIES</term> <note>original: "HIC IACET"</note>, had to appear here in a similar pattern and with the same threefold division: *Neither in Heaven, nor in the Waters, nor in the Earth*. These three encompass the entire world, and thus signify nothing other than *Nowhere*. This is clearly proven by its opposite, *Everywhere*, which we defined strictly in the previous chapter. Therefore, since this place must be explained more fully, let us repeat the solution asserted there, following both the ears of the most learned Reader and the wit of the most clever Author:

---
->let us sing of slightly greater things.<-
<note>original: "paulò maiora canamus" — a famous quote from Virgil’s Eclogue IV, signaling a transition to a higher or more serious topic.</note>
---

Let us say, therefore, that these three great names of Sky, Sea, and Earth can be referred to three objects in Laelia: to the Soul alone, to the Body alone, or to both together. If to the Soul alone: since it never gave form to Laelia's Body, it could not be carried after death (which she never suffered) to the Elysian Fields—those Fortunate Isles which the raving Pagans <note>original: "Ethnici"</note> imagined to be the Heaven of the deceased. There she might have wandered at leisure and enjoyed perpetual rest, as they believed, if she had been released from the prison-house of a truly formed and properly completed body, and at last reached those same...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, focusing on the "Condition" or moral qualities of the subject. It distinguishes between Chastity and Modesty before transitioning to the theme of Death, which is noted by the catchword at the bottom of the page.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

> *In combat, no one would have faced him with impunity, whether he went against the enemy on foot, or spurred the flanks of his foaming horse.*
> <note>original: "Dextera, non illi quisquam se impune tulisset / Obvius armato, seu cum pedes iret in hostem, / Seu spumantis equi foderet calcaribus armos." — Virgil, Aeneid, Book 11.</note>

**CONDITION**, or Quality, is declared in these marble epitaphs of the dead even more than Age itself; for it instructs the reader regarding the excellence and virtue of the deceased, exalts their name, and commends their fame to eternity, as the Venusian <note>The "Venusian" refers to the Roman poet Horace, who was born in Venusia.</note> sang:

---

> — — — — *marbles carved with public inscriptions,*
> *through which breath and life return to good*
> *leaders after death* — — —
> <note>original: "incisa notis marmora publicis, / Per quae spiritus, & vita redit bonis / Post mortem ducibus"</note>

But in the case of our Laelia—an incomplete fetus, cast out before its time—what else could be imagined or devised except that which holds the primary place among women, namely **Modesty**? <note>original: "Pudicitia"</note> Indeed, the guarding of modesty is the chief ornament of the weaker sex, the loss of which also wounds manly dignity and obstructs the good opinion of men. Furthermore, between Modesty and **Chastity** <note>original: "Castitatem"</note> (if indeed they differ from one another, as pleases our persistent advocate of the "threefold word" used everywhere here), there is this primary difference: Modesty renders the soul free from the heat of shameful lusts, while Chastity renders it not only free, but even remote from them. Consequently, the former more frequently regards the faithfulness of a wife, while the latter regards virginal constancy; the one is religious, the other merely moral. 

Hence, by right and merit, on the reverse side of a coin of Julia Maesa, the grandmother of Heliogabalus, in our Museum, I observe this inscription: **MODESTY**. And in the fashioned inscription of GAPHURA, WIFE OF C. LUTIUS FAUSTUS, which is seen at Naples in the house of the Spataphori, among other words which clearly offend by a kind of <term>Affectation</term> <note>original Greek: "Κακοζηλίας" (Kakozelia), meaning a "distasteful affectation" or "bad imitation" in style.</note>, she, asserting herself to be **UNMARRIED**, boasts of being the defender of her unblemished **MODESTY**, so often tempted in vain. And from these things the Reader is advised that our Laelia could indeed have been (although she was none of them) a Virgin, a Wife, and a Prostitute, when it is said: *Neither Chaste, nor a Whore, nor Modest, but All.*

->DEATH,<-

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma, transitioning from the moral qualities of the subject to the specific nature of her death and the location of her remains. # Rising again, not born, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c."</note>

**I**
<meta>catchword: at last (tandem)</meta>

**DEATH** itself is declared to be of a threefold kind, so that the number three remains unshaken in every single verse here. Indeed, from the countless forms of passing away, the Author has selected three of the more noble types: **Famine**, by which Sisygambis, daughter of Arsames <note>Sisygambis was the mother of Darius III; after the death of Alexander the Great, she reportedly starved herself to death out of grief.</note>, or, to look to the Romans, by which Agrippina, wife of Germanicus <note>Agrippina the Elder was exiled by Tiberius and died of starvation.</note>, perished; the **Sword** <note>original: "Ferrum" (literally "Iron")</note>, by which Lucretia, wife of Collatinus <note>The legendary Roman matron whose suicide led to the founding of the Republic.</note>, and Crispina, wife of Commodus, died; and **Poison**, by which Antonia, wife of Drusus, and Sabina, wife of Hadrian, breathed their last. Thus, concerning a wife not truly married (because she was taken before birth), yet nonetheless lost (because she was promised before conception), the author Agatho <note>Agatho is the pseudonym often used in these commentaries to represent the "voice" of the riddle's creator.</note> flattered himself, playing seriously, both happy and unhappy. He imagined for her a noble and threefold kind of death, by any one of which she could have been snatched away; but since she was devoid of life, she was not truly removed. Therefore, it is aptly said: *Taken away neither by Famine, nor by Iron, nor by Poison, but by All.*

At last, the **PLACE** in which the corpse is situated—usually indicated by the phrase **HERE LIES**—ought to appear here, distinguished in a threefold way by a similar measure and the same numbering: *Neither in Heaven, nor in the Waters, nor in the Earth.* These three encompass the entire world, and thus signify nothing other than *Nowhere*. We defined this strictly in the previous chapter as being proven by its opposite, *Everywhere*. Therefore, since this place must be explained more fully once again, let us repeat the solution asserted there, following the ears of the most learned Reader as well as the wit of the most subtle Author,

---
->let us sing of slightly grander things.<-
<note>original: "paulò maiora canamus" — A famous line from Virgil’s fourth Eclogue, signaling a shift to a more serious or elevated topic.</note>
---

Let us say, therefore, that these three great names of the Air, the Sea, and the Earth can be referred to a threefold object in Laelia: To the Soul alone, to the body alone, or to both together. If we speak of the Soul alone: since the body of Laelia was never animated by it, it could not consequently be carried after death—which she never suffered—to the Elysian Fields <note>The final resting place of the souls of the heroic and the virtuous in Greek and Roman mythology.</note> or the Fortunate Isles <note>Legendary islands in the Atlantic Ocean, treated in antiquity as a paradise for the dead.</note>, which the delirious pagans imagined to be the "Heaven" of the deceased. Yet, she would have been able to wander there at leisure and enjoy perpetual rest, as those same people believed, if she had been released from the prison of a truly formed and duly completed body, and reached those same places at last...

<summary>This page analyzes the "threefold death" (starvation, sword, and poison) and the "threefold burial" (neither in sky, water, nor earth) mentioned in the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle. It uses classical historical figures and pagan mythology to explain how the subject exists in a state of being "nowhere" and "never born."</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, types of death, famine, sword, poison, Agrippina, Lucretia, Elysian Fields, Fortunate Isles, Agatho, threefold nature, soul and body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma, transitioning from the nature of the subject's death to the mysterious "triple" location of her burial. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on where her seat or resting place might be found.</meta>

...at last her resting place <note>original: "sedes"</note> had flown, where it might be said that she lay not only in those *Fields* <note>Referring to the Elysian Fields, the mythological resting place of the virtuous.</note>, but even in the **Sky** and the **Waters**; especially because there the Souls

---

> — — — — — — — — — — — some are spread out empty,
> *Suspended to the winds; for others, beneath the vast whirlpool*
> *Their stained wickedness is washed away, etc.*
> <note>These lines are from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (6.740-742), describing the purification of souls after death through air, water, or fire.</note>

If we refer to the **Body** alone; since that unformed Birth, since this putrid Embryo, proved itself unworthy and undeserving of burial, it is not falsely asserted to lie *Neither in the Sky, nor in the Waters, nor in the Lands*; for in each one, and thus *Everywhere* (as distinguished above), the lifeless little body of Laelia could have lain: **In the Sky**, I say, that is, in the Air; in the same manner that bodies were suspended from trees or from a gibbet <note>A gallows-like structure used for public display of corpses.</note>. Claudius Aelianus <note>A 2nd-century Roman author and teacher of rhetoric.</note> reports in his *Varia Historia* (book 4, chapter 1) that this was a solemn custom among the Colchians, the Tibureni, and the Scythians; and Statius <note>A Roman poet of the 1st century AD.</note> sang:

> *But among the Scythian people, the corpses fixed to tree trunks,*
> *The slow passing days bury as they melt away in putrid rot.*

Indeed, there remains no room for doubt that the term "Sky" <note>original: "Coelum"</note> is commonly both used and accepted to mean the **Air**, and not only by poets or historians; wherefore: *To predict the changing manner of the Sky* <note>original: "Varium Coeli prædicere morem"</note> appears in Virgil's *Georgics* (book 1). And Tacitus <note>A famous Roman historian and politician.</note> (book 3) used "the heaviness of the Sky" for the heaviness of the air. But also among us professors of Law, in the books of the forums <note>References to various sections of the *Corpus Iuris Civilis*, the fundamental body of Roman Law.</note>, such as in the law *Lumen* and the law *If a Tree* at the beginning of <term>Urban Servitudes</term> <note>Legal rights concerning buildings and city properties, such as the right to light or air.</note>, in the first law at the end of *On Acquiring Ownership of Things*, the law *It is a Species*, the final law, section *But at the Door* at the end of *That which by Force or Stealth*, the law *If Bees* regarding theft, and others, etc.

**In the Waters**; after the custom of the Carthaginians or the Ethiopians, whose bodies were cast into marshes and rivers; or as those still living men, full of old age and loathing further life, would throw themselves headlong into the sea. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author.</note> records that the Hyperboreans <note>A mythical people living in the far north.</note> did this, and he even dared to call this horrific type of burial "the most blessed": or whenever, according to the wishes of the dying, their ashes were cast into the sea or into a river, according to that line of Ovid <note>A Roman poet best known for the *Metamorphoses*.</note> in *Fasti* (book 5).

<summary>This page explores how the subject of the riddle can be said to lie "neither in the sky, nor the water, nor the earth," by interpreting these locations as both mythological purification sites and historical burial customs (such as Scythian tree burials and ritual drowning).</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, burial customs, Scythians, sky burial, Roman Law, Virgil, Tacitus, Hyperboreans, purification of souls</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle, specifically addressing the paradox of her burial. After discussing how the subject might be said to lie in the "Sky" or "Air," the author now moves to the possibilities of the "Waters" and the "Lands," using classical Roman burial customs and poetic precedents to explain how one can be "everywhere and nowhere" at once.</meta>

# The Unborn Rising Again, etc.
## I 2

> Yet often they are touched by the sweet love of a father,
> And some dying man gives this brief command:
> "Send me into the Tiber, so that carried by the Tiber’s waves,
> I, as empty dust, may reach the Inachian shore." <note>original: "Inachium". A poetic reference to Argos in Greece, implying the soul's return to its ancestral home.</note>
> The heir dislikes the care of the mandated tomb;
> The dead guest is buried in Ausonian <note>original: "Ausonia". A poetic name for Italy.</note> soil.
> A rush-made effigy <note>original: "Scirpea... imago". This refers to the Roman ritual of the Argei, where straw puppets were thrown into the Tiber as substitute sacrifices.</note> is thrown into the Tiber for the master,
> So that it might return to Greek homes across the long seas.

**In the Lands**, finally; how often, according to the ancient Roman rite, were the half-burnt bones or ashes of the dead dedicated "under the adze," <note>original: "sub asciâ". The phrase *sub ascia dedicare* was a common Roman funerary formula, likely meaning the tomb was consecrated while still being carved or protected from future interference.</note> replaced in urns, hidden in excavated soil, or shut away in chests or sepulchers? This was done lest unburied corpses cruelly become food for the teeth of beasts, the fish of the sea, and the birds of the Heaven—as might have happened to this putrid mass of blood <note>original: "putidæ huic sanguinis concretioni". A harsh description of the "unformed birth" or embryo that is the subject of the riddle.</note>. It might have been exposed under the open sky to be torn apart as food for birds; or thrown into the torrent of our **Aposa** <note>original: "Apofæ". The Aposa is a stream that runs through the heart of Bologna; in the author's time, it served as a collector for the city's waste.</note>—that sink for all the filth of this city—to be dissolved; or finally carried out and buried beneath the low-lying suburban fields of **Casaralta**, <note>original: "Casaraltæ". An area north of the city walls of Bologna.</note> certainly to be reduced to nothing and utterly dissolved. Thus, indeed, it could be affirmed that this premature fetus could have lain **Everywhere**—namely, in the Sky, in the Waters, or in the Lands—yet in truth it lay **Nowhere**. One might aptly apply to it what **Maro** <note>The family name of the poet Virgil.</note> sang in the 10th book of the *Aeneid* regarding a dead man deemed unworthy of burial:

> — — — — — — May no noble mother
> Bury you in the earth, nor weigh down your limbs with an ancestral tomb.
> You shall be left to the wild birds, or, submerged in the whirlpool,
> The wave shall carry you, and hungry fish will lick your wounds.

For as often as the dead were left unburied in this way, they were said to be "thrown to the beasts" or even "given to the birds," which is why **Lucan** wrote:

---

> — — — — — — Behold, a noble body
> Feeds Libyan birds; Curio lies covered by no tomb.

And **Augustus**, responding to someone pleading for burial after death, replied: *that he was already in the power of the birds.*

<note>original vocabulary: Tiber (the river of Rome), Ausonia (Italy), Roman rite, sub asciâ (under the adze), Aeneid (Virgil's epic), Maro (Virgil), Lucanus (the poet Lucan), Augustus (the first Roman Emperor), sepultura (burial/tomb).</note>

<summary>This page discusses the disposal of unburied remains, specifically referring to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" fetus. It contrasts formal Roman burial rites with the fate of being "thrown to the birds or waves," using classical quotes to explain the riddle's claim that the subject lies neither in the sky, water, nor land.</summary>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and legal analysis of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle, specifically interpreting the line "Neither in the Sky, nor in the Waters, nor in the Lands." The author transitions from a general discussion of burial to a specific application of these elements to the soul and the body, while also grounding the mystery in the local geography of Bologna.</meta>

->### Aelia Laelia Crispis.<-

If, finally, we apply these three things to both the soul and the body, it has never been expressed more aptly or truly: that Laelia lies *Neither in the Sky, nor in the Waters, nor in the Lands* in actuality <note>original: "actu"</note>, though she could have potentially <note>original: "virtualiter"</note> lain *Everywhere*. She would be **In the Sky** in respect to the Soul, which—after the daily struggle against the rebellious body and finally released from it by death—is called back to the Celestial Homeland (if only Agatho <note>A character or perhaps a previous commentator on the riddle whom the author finds insufficiently pious.</note> had been so piously learned) and would have finally lain there; that is to say, found her rest. She would be **In the Waters and in the Lands** in respect to the body, which, since it could not reach the Sky, would have occupied this terraqueous globe until, by the fate of mortality, it saw itself perpetually mixed and dissolved into that first principle from which it was originally fashioned.

Or perhaps she is **not in the Sky** because Laelia had not undergone <term>apotheosis</term> <note>original: "ἀποθέωσιν". The ancient Greek process of deifying a human being, literally "making into a god."</note>—a status attributed no less superstitiously than insanely to women like Sabina, Faustina, or Paulina <note>Roman Empresses who were declared goddesses after death.</note>—who were said to be carried through the air by a peacock and numbered among the Gods. Finally, she is **Neither in the Waters, nor in the Lands** because she was buried near the suburban Villa of Casaralta <note>A district in Bologna where the famous riddle stone was originally located at the Villa della Casaralta.</note>. This site is low-lying and not immune to flooding; thus, her ashes could easily have been swept away by passing waves, finding a tomb shared by both elements. That such a thing has happened elsewhere was well known and noted by the first lawmakers—specifically by Paulus <note>Julius Paulus, a 2nd-century Roman jurist.</note> in the first book of his *Received Opinions* <note>original: "receptarum sententiarum"</note>, under the title "On burials and mourning" (Title 21), found in the first volume of Cujacius <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), a French legal humanist who edited classical Roman legal texts.</note>; and likewise later by the Emperor Antoninus in the first law of the Code, "On religious matters and funeral expenses," etc.

<summary>The author explains the riddle's claim that the subject lies "nowhere" by contrasting the spiritual ascent of the soul with the physical dissolution of the body, while providing a practical geographical explanation involving the flood-prone Casaralta villa.</summary>

<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, soul and body, apotheosis, Roman Empresses, Casaralta, Roman Law, burial customs, Bologna geography</keywords>

# Chapter V.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring floral motifs and a central rose-like flower within a circular frame.</image-desc>As for the second main point—that is, regarding the Person who set up the Inscription—not all the same qualities are required as those we demonstrated at length in the preceding chapter to be due to the Person for whom it was set (and thus fully attributed to our Laelia). I say that Age, Condition, and Death are not required; these three things certainly befit the deceased, but not the Inscriber. Rather, one looks for his Name, his Connection to the buried person, an expression of Grief, and the Designation of the Tomb. These are the four most important qualities among the countless ones usually ascribed to an Inscriber. Indeed, those details which are most frequently and primarily added tend to be the area or measurement of the sepulcher, usually indicated by the common abbreviations: <term>IN. FR. P.</term> <note>original: "In fronte pedes". A formula used on Roman tombs to indicate the width "in feet across the front."</note> and <term>IN. AG. P.</term> <note>original: "In agro pedes". Indicates the depth of the tomb plot "in feet into the field."</note> Another is the admission or exclusion of heirs from the same tomb, indicated by that equally common formula: <term>H. M. H. N. S.</term> <note>original: "Hoc monumentum heredes non sequitur". A legal protection meaning "This monument does not follow the heirs," ensuring the tomb remained a sacred site rather than private property to be sold.</note> Horace referred to both of these in his poetry:

> A boundary stone gave a thousand feet in front, and three hundred in the field.
> It gave this: so that the monument would not follow the heirs. <note>Quoted from Horace, *Satires* 1.8.12-13.</note>

Other details include conditions or burdens imposed, and legacies to be paid out—partly for the annual distribution of <term>sportulae</term> <note>Small gifts of money or food distributed to clients or the poor in memory of the deceased.</note>, and partly for feasts and drinking sessions to be held over the tomb. There were also provisions for free public Baths, an excellent example of which is shown on our most famous stone in Bologna, located in the houses of the most noble...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the second part of the enigmatic Bologna inscription. Having previously listed the four key requirements for identifying an Inscriber (Name, Connection, Grief, and Tomb Designation), the author now begins with the first: the Name, Lucius Agatho Priscius.</meta>

### Aelia Laelia Crispis.

Of the patrician Albergati family: <term>ON THE WASHING OF THIS BATH, 400 SESTERCES</term> <note>original: "IN HVIVS BALINEI LAVATION. H. S. CCCC." An inscription recording a donation for bath maintenance.</note>, etc. This refers to the pouring of the best wine, scented oils, flowers, and roses upon the ash-urn, intended to signify the swift passing of our bodies, or rather to inaugurate the eternal spring of the soul. As three particularly famous ancient marbles testify, cited by all authors as evidence for this ceremony, and which was briefly touched upon in rhythm by Ausonius:

> Scatter the ashes with pure wine, and drench them with fragrant nard,
> O Guest, and add balsams to the crimson roses.
> The unweeping urn maintains for me a perpetual spring,
> And I have changed my era, I have not died.
> <note>original: "Sparge mero cineres..." from Ausonius, Epitaph 31. The poem reflects the Roman belief in providing comforts to the deceased's spirit.</note>

Finally, other matters of this kind are rightly omitted here, as they are superfluous for this empty, hollow, and entirely fictional tomb. It remains, therefore, for us to weigh the four aforementioned points individually (as was done above with Laelia). First, the **NAME** appears. It is a triple name: *Lucius Agatho Priscius*, representing the <term>praenomen</term> <note>The personal first name.</note>, <term>nomen</term> <note>The family or clan name.</note>, and <term>cognomen</term> <note>A secondary family name or nickname.</note> of our Inscriber. *Lucius* is the first name, so frequently recorded on scholarly stones that almost no other occurred to me more often—not only when I was surveying foreign inscriptions abroad, but even when looking only at our own. Out of the not-very-large number of those existing here, I have observed fourteen distinguished by this first name:

Dedicated to **LUCIUS AELIUS AURELIUS COMMODUS**. To **LUCIUS FURIUS SERUS**. To **LUCIUS SEPTIMIUS BERENICIANUS**. To **AURELIUS LUCIUS**, son of **MARCUS SIRSIUS LUCIUS**. To **LUCIUS UMBRICIUS SECUNDUS**. To **CAIUS TREBIUS LONGO**, son of **LUCIUS**. To **LUCIUS MUNIUS GNOMON**. To **LUCIUS TULLIUS CLEMENS**. To **LUCIUS TULLIUS EPYTINCHANUS**. To **LUCIUS AIATIUS**. To **LUCIUS LICINIUS PRISCUS**. To **LUCIUS PUBLICIUS ASCLEPIUS**. To **LUCIUS CASSIUS VERUS**. To **LUCIUS CELLIUS VESTALLUS**. Besides these, there are nine more which Mutina <note>The city of Modena.</note> likewise supplies from its own few examples, making twenty...

Just as I have laboriously gathered all these examples, so may you, most kind Reader, not be burdened to run through them; they are offered in support of the authenticity of this first name, which was most common among us in ancient times. Therefore, it is no wonder that this name, being so common, also occurred for our Agatho, and was placed before his proper name—a name derived from the Agathan, or Agathonian, or if you prefer, the Agathonic family. That this family likewise had its home and seat in the Felsinean territory 
<note>Felsina was the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna; "agro Felsineo" refers to the surrounding countryside.</note>

provides these two ossuaries

### LUCIUS FABIUS TO FABIA AGATHA.

For indeed, this lineage of the Agathos did not confine itself within the borders of Cisalpine Gaul <note>The region of Northern Italy between the Alps and the Apennines.</note> to such an extent that we should not also marvel at how widely it spread within the city of Rome, as seen from the inscriptions recorded below: In Mazocchi <note>Giacomo Mazocchi, a prominent 16th-century Roman publisher and antiquarian.</note>, page 36, item 3.

**AS MANY ACRES OF LAND AS THIS TOMB DEDICATES.**

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

->TO AUGUSTUS VULCAN<-
<note>original: "VOLKAN. AVG." Vulcan was the Roman god of fire and metalworking; "Augustus" was an honorific title applied to deities to associate them with the Emperor.</note>
->PUBLIUS ANTONIUS<-
->CALLISTO, SEVIR AUGUSTALIS,<-
<note>original: "VI. VIR. AVGEI." A "Sevir Augustalis" was a member of a college of six priests, typically wealthy freedmen, responsible for the cult of the Emperor.</note>
->CAIUS CLODIUS COMICUS<-
->AND PUBLIUS POSTUMIUS AGATHO.<-
->THE SAME DEDICATED THE GIFT,<-
->AND FOR MAINTENANCE, 401 SESTERTIA<-
<note>original: "H S CCCC I." The sestertius was a standard Roman coin; "sestertia" refers to thousands of sesterces.</note>
->WERE DEDICATED TO THE<-
->COLLEGE OF YOUTH.<-
<note>original: "COL. IVVENT." The *Collegia Iuventutis* were organizations for young men of the upper classes, intended to provide military and social training.</note>

It was decided to leave no stone unturned <note>original: "omnem movere lapidem"</note> regarding any name marked as "Agathonicus," so that we might investigate whether the family status or condition of the same Agatha or Agathonia—who, it is established, lived in Bologna at that time—could be revealed through at least one of these inscribed marbles, as the authors examined thus far have remained silent on the matter. And indeed, I believe this did not happen contrary to our hopes: for from this last inscription we have cited, it can be deduced that she was of no insignificant standing.

For if in that place, through a single legacy, Publius Postumius Agatho was able to assign four hundred and one sestertia to the College of Youth in Brescia, then this sum—which today would equal the value of twenty-five thousand Scudi <note>An early modern Italian currency unit.</note> according to the calculations of Budé, Voss, and others, and as most recently calculated regarding an equal price by the illustrious Spon in his *Travels through Italy, Dalmatia, etc.* <note>Jacob Spon (1647–1685) was a pioneering French archaeologist. Guillaume Budé and Gerardus Vossius were earlier humanists who studied ancient weights and measures.</note> concerning the most ancient Bolognese bath praised above—proves that this Agatho was not of the lowest social rank.

Furthermore, other preceding marbles confirm and strengthen this belief, especially the one that mentions Marcus Aurelius Montanus, distinguished by priestly dignity, as the father of Aurelia Agatha. Still others show various members of the Agatho family surrounded by a crowd of slaves and freedmen, and some of them designate individuals joined by marriage to the Aurelii family. Moreover, that the Aurelii—though originally plebeian—shone with the distinction of the consulship is testified by Marcus Aurelius Scaurus in the year 645 from the founding of the City <note>Approximately 109 BC. "V. C." stands for *Ab Urbe Condita* (from the founding of the city).</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's deep dive into the nomenclature surrounding the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, moving from the name "Agatho" to an analysis of the name "Priscius" vs "Priscus." # She Who Is Not Born, Rising Again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This is a reference to the paradoxical themes of the Aelia Laelia Crispis epitaph.</note>

In the year 645 <note>The author uses Roman numerals for years "Ab Urbe Condita," or since the founding of Rome.</note>, he was the suffect consul <note>A consul elected to finish the term of one who died or resigned.</note> to Hortensius; in addition to Marcus Aurelius Cotta, who was consul together with Lucius Licinius Lucullus in the year 679. He was the brother of that Gaius Aurelius who is praised by Cicero as eloquent in the *Brutus*. These details have been gathered quite extensively on behalf of the name Agatho, so that the surname <term>Priscius</term> <note>A family name or nickname.</note> might take the place of *Priscus* and serve as an explanation, since I have thus far found no monument with that same name—or rather, that same surname—even after having searched and "plundered" <note>original: "expilato," here meaning a thorough, exhaustive scholarly search.</note> the works of Gruter more than once, to no avail. Sigonius, in his *On Roman Names*, chapter 882, barely shows it inscribed as: **TO TERTIA PRISCIA IONIA SALVIA.** Likewise, that most sharp-eyed critic of inscriptions, Reinefius, in his letters on page 338, 5, and in his *Great Work* page 674, thinks that "FRISCIVM" should be corrected to "PRISCIVM," and he has accepted it. Therefore, since there is as much affection for the name **PRISCIUS** among authors as there is an abundance of the name **PRISCUS**—especially among us in Bologna, where we can count the four mentioned above—it leads me to a certain suspicion (if it were permitted) that "PRISCIUM," in place of "PRISCI," was omitted from the original manuscript by an ancient stonemason, rather than believing that so many transcribers erred. This is especially true of Turrius, Draudius, and Gevartius, who testify that they saw our most ancient original with their own eyes, observed it maturely, and described it faithfully.

**CONNECTION** <note>original: "ADHAERENTIA." This refers to the relationship or "adherence" between the person who commissioned the monument and the deceased.</note> with the deceased can occur for many reasons. Indeed, we see daily in these memorials that a husband for a wife, a wife for a husband, a son for a father, a father for a son, a brother for a brother or sister, a sister for a sister or brother, a master for a slave, a slave for a master, a freedman for a patron, an heir for the deceased, or a friend for a friend, has always performed this final gift or duty of piety, love, or necessity. In our case, however, it is clearly evident that this "Connection" is derived from a triple bond or link. Thus, it follows that the celebrated harmony of these three voices, resonating equally in Agatho, is not disturbed: namely, Marriage, Love, and Necessity, by one of which...

<summary>The author argues that the name "Priscius" in the epitaph is likely a stonemason's error for "Priscus," citing various scholarly authorities and local Bolognese records. He then begins a philosophical and legal exploration of the "triple bond" (Marriage, Love, Necessity) that connects the living to the deceased in Roman funerary tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Agatho, Priscius, Priscus, Cicero, Sigonius, Reinefius, Bologna, Adhaerentia, funerary inscriptions, Roman consuls</keywords>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<meta>catchword: wonderful</meta>

Indeed, that future Bridegroom, and Lover, and Relation <note>original: "Sponsus, et Amasius, et Propinquus"</note> desired above all to be bound by any of these roles, until, because of the unexpected sight of that foul and uncertain miscarriage <note>In the context of this enigma, the "miscarriage" or "abortion" refers to a being that was never truly born, yet died.</note>, he was frustrated in his triple hope. He immediately became the man who could truly write of himself: *Neither Husband, nor Lover, nor Kinsman.* For whether we interpret that word *Kinsman* <note>original: "Necessarius"</note> as a relative or a close associate—in which sense Festus says: *Those are 'Necessarii,' as Gallus Aelius says, who are either blood relatives, or relatives by marriage, or those upon whom necessary duties are conferred beyond all others*—and Nonius Marcellus writes: *Necessity means affinity: hence those joined by this right are called 'necessaries'*—or whether we explain it as that category of heir <term>haeres necessarius</term> <note>A "necessary heir" in Roman law; usually a slave or family member who was legally bound to accept an inheritance, often to ensure the deceased's debts were settled.</note> who is contrasted with a "voluntary" heir, and is thus called "necessary" because, whether he wants to or not, he is entirely bound to take up the inheritance (as seen in the Law *If a common slave* and the final Law, final paragraph of the *Digest* concerning vulgar and pupillary substitution; Law *A foreign slave* in the section on the institution of heirs; Law *If an option* in the section on optional legacies; and Law *These words* in the section on conditional institutions). 

This second legal definition, however, I would not willingly apply to Agatho—a man endowed with three names and distinguished by the nobility of his clan. It could in no way be adapted to him, since he was unhappily cast out from the desired kinship of the Laelii, and shut out from the family's goods, dowry, or legacies that would have come to him. Therefore, although he might have been inwardly shaken by the triple loss of his hopes (and thus, in this case alone, the phrase "but All" <note>The enigma says the subject is "neither man, nor woman... but all." Here the author applies this "all" to Agatho's total loss of roles.</note> applies perfectly), he might, I say, grieve that Laelia had not been born, rejoice only that he did not have to oversee her funeral, and mourn the loss of the inheritance or dowry snatched away from him; yet outwardly, he was never permitted to publicly display signs of either joy or...

->**SIGNS OF GRIEF**<-
<note>original: "MOERORIS ARGUMENTA"</note>

...which are appropriate only for the funerals of the dead. Agatho, I say, had to exercise restraint regarding marks of sorrow on the Stone, just as he had abstained from displays of sadness in person, lest he boast on eternal marble of a lament that he had not shown when the cause of grief was closer at hand. Indeed, we read that among almost all the Ancients, the marks of pain for a recent death were both varied and frequently practiced: specifically, the mourning cloak...

<summary>The author analyzes the legal and social status of Agatho, the narrator of the Aelia Laelia Crispis epitaph, explaining why he identifies as neither a husband, lover, nor kinsman based on Roman inheritance law and kinship definitions.</summary>
<keywords>Agatho, Roman Law, inheritance, Necessarius, Aelia Laelia Crispis, kinship, funerary customs, mourning, Festus, Nonius Marcellus</keywords>

# Rising Again without being Born, etc.
<meta>catchword: hand-</meta>

...it is a wonderful thing <note>The text continues from the previous page's catchword "mirum" (wonderful).</note> to grow one's hair even to the ankles, to tear one's clothes, to turn pale in the face, and to let the hair down over the neck in the manner of women, just as Pedo Albinovanus <note>A Roman poet of the Augustan age, friend of Ovid.</note> wrote regarding the death of Drusus:

> *We saw Nero <note>Referring to Tiberius Claudius Nero, the future emperor, mourning his brother Drusus.</note>, stunned by his brother's death,*
> *Weeping with unkempt hair hanging over his face.*
> <note>original: "Vidimus attonitum fraterna morte Neronem / Squallida promissa flere per ora coma."</note>

To foully defile that same hair by throwing ash or dust upon it, as seen in Virgil’s twelfth book of the *Aeneid*:

> *Their spirits sink; Latinus <note>A legendary King of the Latins.</note> goes with torn clothes,*
> *Stunned by his wife's fate and the city's ruin,*
> *Defiling his white hair covered in filthy dust.*
> <note>original: "Demittunt mentes, it scissa veste Latinus / Conjugis attonitus fatis, urbisque ruina, / Canitiem immundo perfusam pulvere turpans."</note>

And Catullus in *The Marriage of Peleus and Thetis*:

> *Defiling his white hair with earth and poured-on dust.*
> <note>original: "Canitiem terrae, atque infuso pulvere foedans."</note>

Or more furiously, as in Statius’s *Silvae*, book 5:

> — — — *to strike the unjust*
> *sky-dwellers <note>The gods.</note> with rabid complaints:*
> <note>original: "injustos rabidis pulsare querelis / Coelicolas"</note>

Or as in Ovid’s *Elegies*, book 1, elegy 5:

> *And to mark tender cheeks with a hard fingernail:*
> <note>original: "Et rigido teneras ungue notare genas"</note>

Or with Homer:

> — *to shear the hair, and to sprinkle the face with tears.*
> <note>original: "tondere comam, et lacrymis respergere vultus."</note>

Or finally in other ways, which are too well-known to be spoken, and which are especially cited and explained by the authors who write on the funerals of the Romans and other peoples; all this is so that it may be clear how aptly our Inscriber is called: *Neither Grieving, nor Rejoicing, nor Weeping.*

THE DESIGNATION OF THE TOMB finally excels here incomparably because of Number, because of Magnitude, and because of Congruity. Because of *Number*, because it responds to a triple kind of death: for just as death there could be inflicted by Famine, by the Sword <note>original: "Ferro" (Iron).</note>, and by Poison, so the Mound, the Pyramid, and the Tomb of the deceased Laelia are imagined to have been able to be raised. Because of *Magnitude*, because if Agrippina <note>Agrippina the Elder, who died of starvation.</note> by Famine, Lucretia <note>The Roman heroine who committed suicide with a dagger.</note> by the Sword, and Antonia <note>Antonia the Younger, rumored to have been poisoned.</note> by Poison commended themselves by a noble death, as we have already said, then this woman also ought to have been gifted with such a tomb (her death being likewise assumed) as the Ro—

<summary>This page catalogs classical literary examples of extreme mourning rituals—such as tearing clothes and defiling hair—to contrast them with the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription. It also begins analyzing the physical tomb, linking its three forms (mound, pyramid, and grave) to three modes of death: famine, sword, and poison.</summary>
<keywords>mourning rituals, funerary customs, Roman poets, Agrippina, Lucretia, Antonia, triple death, Aelia Laelia Crispis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of what kind of monument would be suitable for the mysterious Aelia Laelia Crispis, comparing her potential tomb to the grandest structures of Roman antiquity.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...as the honor and majesty of the <note>The text likely begins with a fragment of "Romanorum" (the Romans).</note> Roman Nobles might require: the **Massive Structure** <note>The *Moles Hadriani*, now known as the Castel Sant'Angelo.</note> which once held Hadrian's ashes; the **Pyramid** <note>The tomb of Gaius Cestius in Rome.</note> which held the body of Scipio; the **Tomb** which once so magnificently preserved the bones of Romulus. She ought, I say, to be decorated with a sublime **Cenotaph** <term>A "cenotaph" is a memorial monument for someone whose remains are buried elsewhere or are missing.</term>, for she could not have died by any common fate. Indeed, a humble tomb proves itself unworthy of an illustrious corpse; hence Otho is mocked by Vitellius <note>Roman Emperors during the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 AD).</note> on account of the meager stone inscribed to his memory. Galba, too, is to be mocked for the tomb raised by Argius in his own private gardens. Finally, this is due to the **Correspondence** by which these same three sumptuous buildings could be wonderfully adapted to the three-fold location where the body of Laelia might have lain, if she had existed in the nature of things: **Heaven, the Waters, and the Earth**.

The **Pyramid** is primarily attributed to **Heaven**, as it is said by Poets to seek Heaven itself with its lofty height and to climb the stars, according to that passage of Propertius, book 5:

> *For neither the costs of Pyramids raised to the Stars.*
> <note>original: "Nam neque Pyramidum sumptus ad Sydera ducti."</note>

The **Massive Structure** <note>original: "Moles"</note> is very beautifully suited to the **Waters**, as it is properly used for buildings raised on riverbanks, on the seashore, or even in the Deep, in the very depths themselves; for it is truly nothing other than *throwing piers into the sea* and building upon them, as discussed in the legal text "If an island" at the end of the Digest on acquiring ownership of things. Wherefore Ovid says in his fifth Epistle:

> *The natural Mass looks upon the immense deep.*
> <note>original: "Aspicit immensum Moles nativa profundum."</note>

<margin>Virgil, book 9 of the *Aeneid*.</margin>
> *Just as once on the Euboean shore of Baiae*
> *A stony Pier falls, which previously constructed with great*
> *Masses they cast into the Sea, etc.*
> <note>original: "Qualis in Euboico Baiarum littore quondam / Saxea Pila cadit, magnis quam Molibus ante / Constructam iaciunt Ponto etc."</note>

<margin>Horace, Odes, book 1.</margin>
> *The fish feel the seas narrowed*
> *by masses thrown into the deep.*
> <note>original: "Contracta pisces aequora sentiunt / iactis in altum Molibus."</note>

And most recently and most appropriately, as always, the Illustrious Spanheim <note>Ezekiel Spanheim (1629–1710), a famous Prussian diplomat and scholar of ancient coins and history.</note> says in his second dissertation, page 108: *To raise up the fallen provinces, [providing] for them water conduits, the diversion of rivers, and masses <note>moles/piers</note> opposed to the waves providently and solicitously...*
