->Not Born, Rising Again, etc.<-

...to provide for. <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page regarding the construction of monuments.</note> The **Tomb** is most excellently attributed to the **Earth**, for rather than by its height or depth, it was spread so widely across the fields that it became necessary, in order to restrain such great freedom of space, to establish and declare a boundary. This was done using the formula mentioned above: *In the field, etc. In the front, etc.* <note>original: "In agro, etc. In fronte, etc." These are standard Roman legal abbreviations—*In fronte pedes... in agro pedes*—used on tombstones to define the width and depth of the burial plot to prevent encroachment.</note> Cicero, in the second book of *On Laws*, and Plato, in the second book of the *Republic*, testify that in Athens, tombs were first designated and circumscribed by specific laws.

Therefore, through so many and such magnificent buildings—yet divided individually, as distinguished elsewhere and designated in that phrase *But All*—Agatho <note>Lucius Agatho Priscius, the traditional name of the person who supposedly set up the Aelia Laelia Crispis monument in Bologna.</note> desired to testify to the whole world and to every age the eternal magnitude of his gratitude toward the Laeliani <note>The family or associates of the mysterious subject, Aelia Laelia Crispis.</note>. Since he perceived with his mind rather than his senses, and through imagination rather than his eyes, the one for whom he was building this monument, he rightly says of himself: **"He knows, and knows not, for whom he has placed it."** Like fireflies <note>original: "noctilucarum"</note>, he both concealed and revealed the light of Truth. He did this so effectively that he eluded his fierce descendants, who might otherwise have thought that he had raised an **Altar to the Unknown God** <note>A reference to the Book of Acts 17:23, where St. Paul finds an altar in Athens dedicated "To the Unknown God."</note> by means of this Enigmatic and Sepulchral Program.

## Chapter VI.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring floral motifs and vines within a square border.</image-desc>
Although, according to the ancient precept of <term>Plato</term> <note>Plato (c. 427–347 BC) was a foundational Greek philosopher. The author refers to his *Republic* (Book 2) and *Laws* (Book 8).</note> in the second book, third chapter of *The Republic*, everyone ought to devote their mind to only a single Art—for as that same Philosopher testifies in the eighth book of *The Laws*, human nature can scarcely be practiced with precision in a double Art or profession—nevertheless, one may rightly wonder whether it is better to devote oneself entirely to only one faculty, or to be so engaged in many that we might be able to bring forth from the intellect, as if from a storehouse, something consistent with any question suddenly proposed. Indeed, I do not grant myself so much authority as to dare to determine what should be decided; yet I cannot help but admire men of versatile intellect. When I observe them, I see they have always been accustomed to being received with such great applause that, in comparison with them, the man who is consulted like an oracle regarding his own chosen and highly cultivated discipline, and who is celebrated by common consensus as eminent, seems somehow to grow cheap and be <term>held of no account</term> <note>original: "floccique fieri," literally "to be made of a tuft of wool," a Latin idiom for something worthless.</note>. Therefore, Agatho recognized here, not without reason, that this particular skill of his regarding Inscriptions would have to be adorned not only with an enigmatic structure, but also with an exquisite variety of learned elegance. He did this lest that which is boldly spread about by the common people concerning a "mere Philosopher" or a "pure and simple <term>pettifogger</term>" <note>original: "Leguleio," a dismissive term for a lawyer who is concerned with trivial legal technicalities rather than the spirit of the law.</note> might likewise be said of him, were he to write simply and without any ornamentation...

<summary>This page explores the tension between specializing in a single craft, as Plato advised, and the Renaissance ideal of the polymath. The author argues that Lucius Agatho Priscius used his vast knowledge to ensure his inscriptions were not just legally or philosophically sound, but also artistically and intellectually sophisticated.</summary>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<meta>catchword: hunger</meta>

Who would babble to stones? <note>original: "Lapidibus quis blateraret." A rhetorical question suggesting that the inscription is not mindless chatter but a sophisticated composition.</note> Therefore it is no wonder if he wove the admirable web of this Inscription with such various flowers of words overflowing with erudition—as if embroidered with a needle—and showed himself to be most skilled now in this Science, now in that. Hence, <term>Barnaudus</term>, <term>Gevartius</term>, and <term>Veranus</term> <note>These refer to Nicolas Barnaud, Caspar Gevartius, and potentially Giovanni Battista Verini, early commentators who tried to solve the riddle.</note> regarded him as a searcher of natural things. The same Barnaudus and <term>Kircher</term> <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit polymath who studied the inscription.</note> viewed him as a Chemist; <term>Turrius</term> and <term>Liceto</term> <note>Fortunio Liceto (1577–1657), a physician and philosopher who wrote a famous treatise on the riddle.</note> as a Philosopher; Marius Michaelangelus as a Meteorologist; Vitus as a Mythologist; Baroldonus as a follower of the <term>Cabala</term> <note>The Kabbalah; a form of Jewish mysticism often studied by Renaissance Christian scholars.</note>; Ferronius as a Musician; Montalbanus as a Botanist; Bordocchius as a Legal Expert; Mengolus as a Praiser of Chastity; and what did the remaining Authors not call him? For although none of these titles can truly be held by or lawfully applied to our Inscriber, nevertheless, both the hidden power and the numerical distribution of the marvelous words—in which the Inscription incomparably excels and surpasses all others—argue and silently proclaim him to be endowed with every Science and Art, as I was mentioning.

For indeed, if one wishes to examine the hidden power of the words from which we see it composed, who does not see that <term>Agatho</term> <note>Referring to Lucius Agatho Priscius, the purported author of the inscription.</note> was most fervent in historical erudition and philosophical doctrine? Historical erudition is found in those words: *Neither Chaste, nor Whore, nor Modest*; <note>original: "Nec Casta, nec Meretrix, nec Pudica"</note> for in women, as I suggested, praise or disgrace is added chiefly by this proof of honorable or dishonorable reputation. Accordingly, so that Lucius might grant the name of <term>Laelia</term> eternity as he desired, he wished her to be neither Chaste, nor a Whore, nor Modest, *But All Things*, and thus he equalized the fate of Laelia to the condition of illustrious women. For how many women would have suffered the loss of their name, no less than their beauty, upon dying, had they not acquired fame either from the preservation of their <term>private quarters</term> <note>original: "Gynaecaeis," the women's apartments in a Greek or Roman house.</note> or from frequenting brothels? Hence the monuments testify to the Chastity especially of Syrita, Dryas, and Marcia; the shameful acts of Crispa, Faustina, and Messalina <note>Faustina the Younger and Messalina were Roman empresses often cited in history for their alleged infidelities.</note>; and the Modesty of Penelope, Artemisia, and Portia. Nor does Agatho deserve less regard concerning History when he says: *Neither by Hunger, nor by Sword, nor by Poison*, <note>original: "Neque Fame, neque Ferro, neque Veneno"</note> which things more than others... [Hunger]

### Not born, yet Rising again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This refers to a common interpretation of the riddle's subject as a soul or a specific alchemical process.</note>

<sig>M 2</sig>

[Hunger] <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page's catchword.</note> brings to mind Sisygambis, Antonia, and Agrippina; the Sword brings to mind Calpurnia, Lucilla, and Crispina; and Poison brings to mind Livia, Sabina, and Sophonisba. <note>These women from classical history are categorized by their modes of death: Sisygambis died of voluntary starvation; others like Calpurnia or Crispina faced execution or suicide by the blade; and Livia or Sophonisba were famously associated with poison.</note> Furthermore, those words of History agree: *Neither in Heaven, nor in the Waters, nor in the Earth*, <note>original: "Nec Coelo, nec Aquis, nec Terris"</note> which, besides others foolishly elevated to Heaven—such as Matidia, Paulina, and Mariniana—recall in summary those drowned in the Waters—Anna, Sappho, and Theoxena—and those buried in the Earth while still alive—Oppia, Urbina, and Minutia.

The Author seems to look back again at *Philosophical Doctrine* in those words: *Neither in Heaven, nor in the Waters, nor in the Earth*, which together denote all that the Epicureans, Academics, Stoics, Peripatetics, and any others of that kind once investigated concerning our soul. For if Democritus wished the soul to be Fire, and if Diogenes wished it to be Air, both are wrapped up (as the nature of a riddle allows, and indeed demands) in that phrase: *Neither in Heaven*. For the sphere of Fire was anciently placed in Heaven, by reason of the container to the contained, through <term>metonymy</term> <note>A figure of speech where a thing is called by the name of something associated with it; here, "Heaven" stands for the upper elements of fire and air.</note>; and it is clear that Air is commonly called by the name of "Heaven" and used interchangeably for it, as has been said elsewhere. 

If Hippon wrote that the soul was rather Water, or if Epicurus wrote it was Earth—not explicitly, perhaps, but no less shamelessly (since truly no one is found so dull-witted as to dare say the soul is literally Earth)—by denying the immortality of man, so that with the body composed of Earth it would return to the Earth as if to its own beginning; behold, both are expressed, not obscurely (just as one or another of the points explained above), but openly in those words: *neither in the Waters, nor in the Earth*. Finally, if Empedocles was of the opinion—with whom Plato does not disagree in the *Timaeus*—that the soul is not just one of the aforementioned Elements, but rather coalesces from each of the four, and is each one of them, when he says:

> *The earthy part sees the earth, the watery part the waves,*
> *The divine Air sees the Air, and the fiery force perceives the fire.*
<note>original: "Terrea pars terras, pars undea conspicit undas, / Aethera divum Aether, vis ignea prospicit ignem." A Latin translation of Empedocles’ famous fragment on perception: "We see earth by earth, water by water..."</note>

The four Elements are sufficiently indicated here, if they are explained according to the method above.

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<sig>M</sig>
<meta>catchword: The same (Idem)</meta>

But truly, if one wishes to contemplate the numerical distribution of these same words—which appears to be strictly maintained everywhere in a triple harmony—who could deny that our Agathon <note>Likely referring to the supposed author of the riddle, whom the writer credits with profound wisdom.</note> has shown himself to be refined in every kind of knowledge, given the Ternary <term>the number three</term> so often repeated there?

> Than which no number is more sacred or divine

says Bocchius <note>Achille Bocchi (1488–1562), a Bolognese humanist and author of the *Symbolicarum quaestionum*, a famous book of emblems.</note> in book 4, Symbol 106. For what object can be imagined or devised, even if we carefully examine the arts, the sciences, and their parts or duties, that does not rejoice in the perfection of the triple number? "All things are three," says Aristotle in the first book of *On the Heavens*, and the number three itself shines forth everywhere. This very thing...

---
-> God delights in an odd number; <-
---
<note>original: "numero Deus impare gaudet," a famous line from Virgil’s *Eclogues* (8.75), often used to suggest that divine order favors the number three.</note>

Therefore, it has been recorded in memory that the ancient theologians used to offer three things in sacrifices, and the ancient philosophers used to employ three things in the worship of God: namely, adoration, incense, and a hymn. From this, no prayers were sent forth, and no libations <term>ritual liquid offerings</term> were tasted or poured, which were not repeated a third time, nor performed on the third day, or within a three-month period, as Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, an ancient mathematician who wrote on the mystical properties of numbers.</note> asserts in these words: *They pour three libations, and three times they sacrifice, who seek to have their vows confirmed by God*; hence Theocritus:

> *Three times I pour a libation, and three times I pronounce these mystic words.* <note>original: "Ter libo, Ter et haec pronuncio mystica verba."</note>

Thus, in purifications, those who were sprinkled with the water of ritual cleansing <term>lustration</term> were sprinkled three times, as in Virgil:

> *He likewise carried the pure water three times around his companions,*
> *Sprinkling them with a light dew and a branch of a lucky olive tree.* <note>original: "Idem Ter socios pura circumtulit unda / Spargens rore levi, et ramo felicis olivae" from Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note>

Indeed, in the ritual cleansings of the fields, the sacrificial victims were led three times around the crops, as in the same author:

> *And three times let the lucky victim go around the new crops.* <note>original: "Terque novas circum felix eat hostia frugis."</note>

The same applies to the binding of spells, as seen here:

> *First, I surround you with these three threads, distinct with three colors,*
> *And three times I lead this image around these altars.* <note>original: "Terna tibi haec primùm Triplici diversa colore / Licia circumdo, Terque haec Altaria circum / Effigiem duco."</note>

<meta>catchword: lum</meta>

The same was observed in funeral rites; thus Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BCE – 17 CE), a Roman poet.</note> says:
> Behold an aged woman sitting in the midst of girls,
> performs the rites silently, yet she herself is not silent,
> and with three fingers she places three pieces of incense under the threshold.
<note>original: "Ecce Anus in mediis residens annosa puellis, / Sacra facit tacitè, nec tamen ipsa tacet, / Et digitis Tria thura Tribus sub limine ponit." from Ovid's *Fasti*.</note>

So too in the funerals of the dead, as Maro <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, known in English as Virgil (70–19 BCE).</note> says regarding the funeral rites of Pallas:
> Thrice they circled the burning pyres clad in shining armor;
> Thrice they circled the mournful fire of the funeral on horses,
> and gave forth cries from their mouths.
<note>original: "Ter circum accensos cincti fulgentibus armis / Decurrere rogos; Ter mæstum funeris ignem / Lustravere in equis, ululatusque ore dedere." from Virgil's *Aeneid* (11.188–190).</note>

Therefore, the designer of the "Triple Words" on the Aelian Stone <note>Refers to the *Aelia Laelia Crispis* inscription, which contains many paradoxical triplets.</note>—who was himself surely devoted to such mysteries—could not have been ignorant of these Triple rituals of sacrifice so religiously preserved by the Ancients. Perhaps it was also not hidden from him that among the Divine Powers to whom the Pagans sacrificed with this ritual, a logic of the Triad <term>the number three</term> was always maintained. This was because they believed that three brother-gods ruled over the three kingdoms into which the world was divided: Jupiter over Heaven, Neptune over the Sea, and Pluto over the Underworld.

For this reason, a triple symbol, fashioned in a threefold manner, was assigned to each of them: the three-forked lightning to Jupiter, the Trident to Neptune, and the three-throated Cerberus <note>The mythical three-headed hound guarding the gates of Hades.</note> to Pluto. In the same way, they decreed for Mercury a winged hat, winged sandals, and a winged caduceus <term>a staff entwined by two serpents</term>. For Venus, they assigned foliage in the myrtle, flowers in roses, and fruit in the apple. To Mars, they gave the breastplate, the sword, and the shield; to Ceres, the ears of grain, poppies, and the torch; to Bacchus, the thyrsus <term>a staff topped with a pine cone</term>, the ivy, and the tiger; and they attributed three similar things of this kind to the others. 

Consider also when they enumerated the three Goddesses competing in beauty <note>The Judgment of Paris involving Hera, Athena, and Aphrodite.</note>, the three Graces <note>The Charites: Aglaia, Euphrosyne, and Thalia, who represent charm and beauty.</note> who are beneficial to mortals, the three Furies <note>The Erinyes: Alecto, Megaera, and Tisiphone, deities of vengeance.</note> who avenge crimes, the three Sirens who are hostile to sailors, the three Harpies who disturb the human race, and finally the three Fates <note>The Parcae: Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos.</note> who weave the threads of our lives.

But this mystic number is to be seen not only in these Names; it thrusts itself further upon those inquiring into the World itself. If anyone, for instance, were to contemplate this Heaven of ours under a triple distinction: as it is the mobile starry realm, it is the Firmament; as it is the mobile realm lacking stars, it is the Ninth Sphere; as it is the immobile realm without stars, it is the Empyrean <term>the highest heaven, believed to be the abode of God and the blessed</term>. If Heaven...

# Aelia Laelia Crispis
<meta>catchword: Tria</meta>

...[wor]ld itself <note>Completing the word *mundum* from the previous page.</note> as it relates to our Earth, is measured by three dimensions: namely, length from top to bottom, width from right to left, and height <note>Or depth, depending on the orientation.</note> from front to back. Likewise, in the celestial spheres, one observes a triple state: the center, the diameter, and the circumference. In the planets, there is a triple arrangement: the deferent, the equant, and the epicycle <term>terms from Ptolemaic astronomy describing the complex circular paths planets were thought to follow</term>. In these same planets, there is a triple motion: retrogradation, station, and direction <note>Referring to how planets appear to move backward, stand still, or move forward in the sky.</note>.

When one considers the three qualities in the Elements: one is light only, such as Fire; another is heavy only, such as Earth; and another is both light and heavy at once, such as Air and Water. Regarding the place of their generation where they touch one another, these same Elements do not exceed three in number—which our Riddle-maker <note>The author of the *Aelia Laelia Crispis* inscription.</note> seems to observe, as he mentions only Air, Water, and Earth, and omits Fire. But indeed, the Sophists <note>Here referring to a specific school of ancient natural philosophers.</note>, who recognized only three Elements, remained silent about Air, which they called not an Element but the "glue of the Elements" by which the others were joined. Yet in those same three elements, they again considered three things: in Fire, the bodily substance of the fire itself, its splendor, and its heat—or, as Aristotle preferred, flame, light, and coal. In Water: the spring, the river, and the sea. In Earth: the mountain, the slope, and the plain. Furthermore, the Ancients enumerated three Worlds: the first was the super-mundane <note>Beyond the physical universe.</note>, the second the celestial, and the third the sublunary <note>The region below the moon, i.e., the Earthly realm.</note>. And this sublunary world in which we dwell is governed and preserved by three things so vital that, if they failed, the world itself would perish: I mean fertility, offspring, and love.

In ancient times, the title of "Trismegistus" <note>Greek for "Thrice-Greatest".</note> was given to that Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure combining the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth.</note> who is fabled to have been the grandson of the deity of the same name; this was because he surpassed Philosophers in wisdom, Priests in religion, and Kings in the prosperity of his rule. I would call our Agatho <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary scholar or a figure in this academic circle.</note>—that distinguished devotee of this most auspicious Ternary—a "Trismegistus," or "Thrice-greatest" according to the meaning of the name, for this one reason: because he has tenaciously retained in his writing that number by which he perceived all things to exist; whether...

# Not Born, yet Rising Again, etc.
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

One might observe that there are three intervals of time: the past, the present, and the future. There are three spans of magnitude: length, width, and depth; and from these, three continuous quantities: the line, the surface, and the body. Place is divided into three: the lowest, the middle, and the highest. There are three kinds of goods: the useful, the virtuous <note>original: *honesta*; referring to things that are morally good in themselves, rather than just practical</note>, and the pleasurable. Friendship is found within us in three ways: that which proceeds from virtue, that which comes from reward <note>or mutual benefit</note>, and finally that which arises from pleasure. There are three causes for our actions, three beginnings for things to be done, and three factors that concur to produce every effect. Three powers are assigned to our soul; and again, three parts are assigned to that same soul when it is joined to the body: the **irascible** power <note>the "spirited" part of the soul associated with anger and courage</note> which they place in the heart, the **concupiscible** power <note>the part associated with base desires and appetites</note> which they place in the liver or the spleen, and **reason**, which they have stationed in the brain as if in a commanding citadel of works.

Indeed, he <note>the author or creator of the riddle</note> perhaps distinguished three things in every created thing: Substance, form, and order; Measure, number, and weight; Power, object, and act; Being, being in a certain way, and being toward something. Furthermore, there is a threefold substance: Matter, form, and the composite. There are three principles of generation and corruption: matter, form, and the efficient cause <term>the agent or force that brings something into being</term>. There are three kinds of **Wisdom**: Logic, Ethics, and Theoretical; each of which is again divided in a threefold manner. Logic is divided into Grammar, Rhetoric, and Dialectic; Ethics into Monastic <note>original: *monasticam*; in this context, it refers to "individual ethics," or the governance of oneself</note>, Economic <note>referring to the management of the household</note>, and Political. Theoretical wisdom is divided into Physics, Mathematics, and Theology. There are three kinds of Rhetoric: Judicial, Demonstrative, and Deliberative. Dialectic consists of three parts: Definition, Division, and Argumentation. Finally, all dialectical reasoning <note>the formal structure of a logical argument or syllogism</note> is composed of three parts: the major premise, the middle term, and the minor premise. But there are no more than three principal kinds of Poetry; a drawing <note>original: *graphidem*; referring to the art of drafting or sketching</note> is formed from no more than three elements; Painting consists of no more than three types; there are no more than three kinds of song; and harmony contains no more than three primary consonances <note>original: *simphonias*; referring to the fundamental musical intervals such as the octave, fifth, and fourth</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's demonstration of the "Ternary" principle (the number three) as the foundational structure of all things. ### Aelia Laelia Crispis.

But one could also observe that Law itself is not lacking in this quantity [the number three]; indeed, it exists in a **threefold manner**: Natural Law, the Law of Nations, and Civil Law. <note>original: *Ius Naturale, Ius Gentium, Ius Civile*; the classic divisions of law in Roman jurisprudence.</note> Three precepts are assigned to Law itself: to live honorably, not to harm another, and to give each his due. <note>original: *honeste vivere, alterum non laedere, suum cuique tribuere*; these are the fundamental moral pillars of the legal system.</note> These same three principles pertain to three areas: to **persons**, to **things**, and to **actions**.

Persons were once distinguished into patricians, senators, and plebeians; or into freeborn, slaves, and freedmen. Those same slaves escaped to freedom through a **triple manumission**—that is, through a formal, a lesser, or an inferior method. <note>original: *manumissio*; the legal process of freeing a slave.</note> Through the formal method, they were made Roman citizens; through the lesser, they became Latins; and through the inferior, they became *Dedititii*. <note>term: *Dedititii* — the lowest class of freedmen, consisting of former slaves who had been punished for crimes or surrendered in war; they were forbidden from ever becoming Roman citizens.</note> Furthermore, Roman citizens were created in a triple way: by a will, in a sacred temple, or before a Consul. Latins were likewise created in a triple way: by a letter, among friends, or at a banquet. The *Dedititii* were similarly formed in three ways: by subjecting them to punishment, by public beating, or by branding them with iron and fire.

**Things** are considered to belong to no one <note>original: *in nullius bonis*; property that has no legal owner, such as wild animals or abandoned items.</note> for a triple reason: by law, by nature, or by event. By law, these include sacred things, holy things, and religious things. By nature, they include fish, birds, and wild beasts. By event, they include inherited property before an heir exists, Treasure hidden for so long a time that its owner is unknown, and things held as abandoned, such as "missiles." <note>original: *missilia*; gifts or coins thrown by officials into a crowd, which became the property of whoever caught them.</note>

**Legal Actions** revolve around a triple difference: namely universal, general, and singular. Some are for the recovery of a thing, some are penal, and others are mixed. Some are based on "good faith," others on "strict law," and others are "arbitrary." <note>These represent different procedural categories in Roman court cases.</note> A triple action is given for pursuing a legacy, a triple action for obtaining universal trusts, a triple action for a loan, and a triple action for damages inflicted, to be decided by a Judge or Magistrate. The judge himself, in rendering judgment, ought to remember three things—having been taught by certain words of Agathon (as reported by Stobaeus): namely, that he rules over men; secondly, that he rules according to the laws; and thirdly, that he will not rule forever. <note>original: *Agathone, Stobeo*; Agathon was a Greek tragic poet, and Joannes Stobaeus was an author who compiled an anthology of Greek quotations.</note>

Nor should I doubt that he was aware that in ancient times marriage was established in three ways: by Usage, by the Offering of Spelt, and by Symbolic Sale. <note>original: *Usu, Farre, Coëmtione*; these were the three legal forms of Roman marriage (*conventio in manum*).</note> Furthermore, a triple fatherland flourished...

# She Who Is Not Born, Rising Again, etc.

[...threefold] power, triple guardianship, triple loss of status <note>original: *capitis diminutio*; in Roman law, this referred to a change in a person's legal status, such as losing citizenship or freedom.</note>, triple servitude, and triple donation. Three types of wills are found, three types of legacies, three types of substitutions, and three types of heirs. Legal trusts <note>term: *fideicommissa* — a legal arrangement where a person leaves property to one party for the benefit of another.</note> are left in three ways; succession from an intestate <note>A person who dies without a valid will.</note> occurs in three orders; wills are void for three reasons; and finally, a judgment is established by three persons.

But why do I delay you any longer, most kind Reader, and why do I allow my pen, immoderately abusing your patience, to pursue any further the investigation of this "Threeness" diffused everywhere? Whoever seeks praise for this number from anywhere other than the Heavenly Powers does too little; for Divinity itself has bestowed the height of dignity upon this number through Its own Persons, whom the rites of the Catholic Liturgy teach us to venerate with the holy and divinely given *Trisagion*. <note>term: *Trisagion* — the "Thrice-Holy" hymn (Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal), emphasizing the Trinity.</note>

Let it be clear from this argument, then, that Agatho <note>The supposed author of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription.</note> did not collect these things foolishly, but as I also previously thought, quite ingeniously. For this reason, he should be preferred to those other Agathos whom we read earned the friendship and praise of the ancient philosophers: whether we mention the Tragedian, to whom Plato gave first place in his *Symposium* and Aristotle in the *Ethics* (Book 2); or whether we look at the Riddle-maker, similar to our own, whom Athenaeus praises for equally fittingly composing riddles <note>original: *gryphis*; literally "griffins," used metaphorically for intricate verbal puzzles or logic traps.</note>; or finally, whether we review the Pythagorean, whom Aelian reports also excelled in antitheses (in his *Various History*, Book 10). Since our Priscius <note>A likely reference to the author himself or the character presenting the mystery.</note> has presented us with this inscription of his—at once enigmatic and overflowing with antitheses—filled with such further erudition as is evident, all other Agathos are compelled to yield to him.

Thus indeed, in the following chapter, we shall see that the most celebrated master of this Epigraphic Art, Emanuele Tesauro <note>A famous 17th-century Italian Jesuit, poet, and rhetorician, author of *The Aristotelian Spyglass* (*Il Cannocchiale Aristotelico*).</note>, judged this inscription above all others for those wishing to compose inscriptions correctly. Indeed, he valued it so highly that those very decrees which, at the dictation of Cicero himself, once came from the Senate...

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription, transitioning from a legalistic analysis of the number three to a rhetorical and philosophical one. ### Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...having been consulted, both for the public praise of Heroes and as a perpetual model of eloquence, he <note>Referring to Agatho, the supposed author of the inscription.</note> boldly and laboriously reworked them into this form. For although he doubted that the perfect Latinity of that golden age <note>The "Golden Age" of Latin literature, roughly the period of Cicero and Virgil.</note> could ever be equaled, he believed that something new could still be laudably added: namely, a harmony unknown to that age (as he thought), which he derived from <term>the repetition of the same words</term> <note>original: *ἀπὸ τῶ ταυτολογεῖν* (from tautology)</note>, and restored to the brightness and weight of Latin eloquence with a new daring and perhaps not unsuccessful result.

Behold what that seeker of more pleasant elegance, Thesaurus <note>Emanuele Tesauro (1592–1675), a famous Italian writer and rhetorician who analyzed the inscription's style.</note>, decided regarding the mere sequence of words—that is, the external harmony of the threefold terms so often repeated there. However, the deepest searcher of Philosophy, Licetus <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a philosopher who proposed that the inscription was a complex Aristotelian allegory.</note>, felt more profoundly regarding the intrinsic meaning of these same words, wrapped as they are in so many ambiguities. For he argued that this miraculous work had flowed from the books of *Parables*, or *Proverbs*, which Diogenes Laertius <note>A 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> indicated Aristotle had composed; he believed it had been selected from the enigmatic sayings which that Prince of all Philosophers, to sharpen the wits and for the sake of the mind, sometimes dictated while walking about.

Although this opinion of his—may the spirit of such a great man forgive me—is too far-fetched and opposes our own exposition (which is humble, natural, and entirely devoid of deep speculation), namely because he identifies an author for the Inscription other than Agatho, he nevertheless celebrates the doctrine of that same Agatho with us by this very argument. For Fortunius [Liceti] could hardly have judged this Laelian Epitaph to be the work of Aristotle without also indicating its legitimate author, Agatho, as being nearly equal to Aristotle himself. This is true whether in his ability to shroud a concept of the mind in the sacred mysteries of wisdom through the help of enigmas—just as we have more than sufficiently demonstrated in the preface that the Stagyrite <note>Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira.</note>, Socrates, Pythagoras, Plato, and all the rest did before him—or in that same Stagyrite's frequent habit of distinguishing the ambiguous and the obscure through "the process of becoming" and "the state of completion," or through potentiality and act, as is found throughout his books on Physics—

# ->Rising though not born, etc.<-
<sig>N 2</sig>

...we gather from the ancient authors, especially from text 45 and text 68, joined with the preceding text 67; and expressly in Book 3 of *On the Heavens*, chapter 3, text 31, when he <note>Aristotle, the "Philosopher" frequently cited in these debates</note> says that an element is present in other bodies **either in potentiality or in act** <note>original: *potentia vel actu*. In Aristotelian philosophy, "potentiality" is the capacity to become something, while "act" is the actual realization of that thing. This distinction is used here to explain how the subject of the enigma can possess contradictory traits at once.</note>. This is excellently demonstrated by Scotus <note>John Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), a highly influential High Medieval theologian and philosopher</note> in his commentary on the *Sentences*, book 2, distinction 12, question 1, letter G, where he declares how the generator does nothing other than extract that which exists in potentiality into act. More aptly still, Burley <note>Walter Burley (c. 1275–1344), an English logician known for his commentaries on Aristotle</note> in his work on Aristotle’s *Physics*, book 1, regarding text 62, provides an example in the seed <note>original: *spermate*</note> during the generation of a human: here, the parts do not cease to recede and human parts do not cease to be formed until that human form is perfected—a form that was hardly even begun in a miscarriage <note>original: *Aborsu*</note>, yet is present in our Birth. It has become sufficiently clear that by this single distinction alone—and in no other or quite different way—the implacable contradictions of our Inscription can finally be reconciled.

Or finally, in reviewing with that same Peripatetic <note>A term for a follower of Aristotle, referring to his habit of walking while teaching</note> the opinions of the ancient Sages concerning the soul, which were listed above: there it likewise appeared whatever Leucippus, Empedocles, Socrates, Democritus, Plato, and others of that kind once thought of it. We have already shown above that Lucius Agatho <note>The presumed author of the enigma</note> did not merely taste their opinions but even drank of them liberally. Therefore, one could rightly sing of him what the Poet <note>Claudian, a late 4th-century Roman poet, in his panegyric for the consul Mallius Theodorus</note> wrote concerning Manlius:

> Whatever flowed from the Socratic order, whatever
> the learned halls of Cleanthes' trumpet resounded,
> whatever was discovered, Chrysippus, in your retreat:
> whatever Democritus laughed at, and what
> Pythagoras said by remaining silent; all of antiquity
> has hidden itself within this one breast,
> and it emerges greater with its gathered strengths.

## Chapter VII.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring floral motifs and a central figure or face within the letter's structure.</image-desc>

The clever cunning of certain individuals sometimes goes so far that they not only look down with a twisted chin upon the difficult knowledge of things which they have abandoned as hopeless, but they even attack it with difficulties. They do this so that they might diminish in others the praise denied to themselves by this art, if not to overturn it entirely. In this Aelian Inscription of ours <note>The *Aelia Laelia Crispis*, a famous and enigmatic Latin inscription found in Bologna that has puzzled scholars for centuries.</note>, however, I believe this is not at all to be feared; since we have already recognized it to be composed with such great harmony, doctrine, and erudition by the most wise Agatho <note>Agatho is here presented as the creator of the riddle, though its true origin remains a historical mystery.</note>, that those who, distrusting their own ability to untie its knots, are drawn to despise or attack it, ought to be laughed at rather than heard. These critics deny that it was a work of the ancients, but instead consider it a modern fiction, with which some witty and humorous person might thus mock us <note>original: *os subliniret* — literally "to smear the face," a Latin idiom meaning to trick or make a fool of someone.</note>. 

However, lest the reasons and foundations upon which this opinion of theirs might rest be judged as craftily ignored by us, let us set forth these primary objections: First, that ancient epitaphs <note>Funeral inscriptions.</note> certainly never played with riddles or antitheses <term>Antithesis: A rhetorical device where contrasting ideas are placed side by side, such as "not a man, nor a woman."</term>. Second, that the ancients did not designate women with a first name or with three names. Third, that they did not use all the basic letters of the alphabet, but only a few,

<meta>catchword: few,</meta>

<summary>The author defends the antiquity of the famous Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle against skeptics who claim it is a modern forgery. He outlines the critics' main arguments, including the unusual use of three names for a woman and the presence of complex riddles in a funerary context.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Agatho, antiquity, forgery, epitaphs, riddles, antithesis, Roman naming conventions</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the antiquity of the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription. While the previous page addressed the "clever cunning" of critics who dismiss the riddle as a modern joke, this page shifts to a physical history of the monument itself, explaining why the current version looks "new" and providing testimony that an ancient original did indeed exist before being accidentally destroyed by a construction worker.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

...with only a few letters, or perhaps just the first letter followed by a dot, to express those same first names <note>original: "praenomina"</note>. Furthermore, they argue that the ancients never wrote out diphthongs <note>A sound formed by the combination of two vowels in a single syllable, like 'ae'.</note> in the manner seen here. It remains, therefore, to address each of these objections individually, so that our Inscription may be restored to its former glory and, as the saying goes, "stand on its own two feet" <note>original: "recto... talo," a Latin idiom meaning to stand upright or be on solid ground.</note>.

But first, we must spend this entire chapter defending the antiquity and dignity of the stone against those who contradict it. Indeed, if anyone were to conclude that the inscription never existed simply because the true, ancient stone finally perished—ruined by the envious decay of Age—they would surely be mistaken. On the contrary: the fact that we have this very recent copy before us today actually proves and demonstrates that an ancient archetype must have existed first, from which this one had to be transcribed. Certainly, our elders testified to having seen that ancient version a thousand times. Among them was my own Father, who lived nearly a century; it has been fifteen years <note>original: "tertio ab hinc lustro." A lustrum was a period of five years.</note> since he left his mortal remains behind and was gathered into what I hope is a blessed immortality. I also remember the noble patrician and most distinguished Senator, Achilles Volta—then the owner and Lord of that villa—lamenting to me more than once. He informed me of the irreparable fate, forever to be mourned by Bologna <note>original: "Felsina," the ancient Etruscan name for Bologna.</note>, of how the broken fragments of that most ancient Marble were kept in the corner of a secluded room. They were not particularly well-protected, and so an ignorant stonemason found them and mixed them with lime and old rubble to serve as the foundation for the suburban house, which was being enlarged at that time. Thus, they perished.

However, if no credit were given to this claim—on the grounds that it comes from an interested party—it certainly could not be denied when it comes from these two men. These were not men of Bologna, nor even Italians, but foreigners from across the Alps, and therefore beyond any suspicion of bias. They recorded in writing that they had looked upon the ancient stone with their own eyes when it was almost failing and consumed by extreme age, and that they were scarcely able to transcribe the letters. Regarding "eyewitnesses" <note>original: "de visu"</note> (as we legal experts <note>original: "Iurisperiti"</note> say), they must be entirely believed, for "by the mouth of two or three witnesses every word shall stand" <note>A reference to a well-known legal and biblical principle (Deuteronomy 19:15; Matthew 18:16).</note>. This is such a well-known point of law that nothing more needs to be added. The first of these was...

<meta>catchword: The first was</meta>

<summary>The author defends the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription against claims of forgery, explaining that the original ancient stone was accidentally crushed and used as construction filler, while citing eyewitness accounts from foreign scholars to prove its previous existence.</summary>

Having previously argued against those who dismissed the stone as a modern forgery, the author now provides specific historical testimonies from the 16th and 17th centuries to prove that an authentic, ancient version of the stone existed at a specific location near Bologna.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
## ->She who rises without having been born, etc.<-
<meta>catchword: disputes</meta>

...[The first to testify] was Joannes Turrius, a Belgian from Bruges, in a letter sent from that same place in January 1567 to his friend Richard White <note>original: "Riccardo Vito," referring to Richard White of Basingstoke, a British jurist and historian</note>. As we noted in the first chapter, Turrius was White's friend and a three-time competitor in interpreting this very Inscription. In this letter, he informed White that he had been deceived by a corrupt version from the Milanese <note>original: "Insubrum," the ancient Celtic people of the Milan region</note>. He then sent him the true and intact version, which corresponds entirely and literally with our modern version, using these words: 

*In this matter, after many controversies at last, we reached the conclusion that the Milanese—wishing to claim for themselves what others discovered through diligent investigation and great effort—have slightly changed that Enigma in certain places. It pleased you, therefore, that I should add at the end of your book (once I had read it through) the Epitaph which I had read with these very eyes, and brought back a transcription of for the sake of confirming its authenticity. Therefore, for those leaving Bologna by the Mascharella Gate, at the first milestone from the city, there stands the small villa of Marco Antonio de la Volta, a Bolognese Patrician, in which that Epitaph of yours is found. Indeed, several letters on the marble, corroded by the passage of time, the environment, and a kind of rust, easily demonstrate its antiquity.*

->To the Divine Shades<- <note>original: "D. M." standing for "Diis Manibus," a common Roman funerary dedication.</note>

### AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS. etc.

Another witness is Jan Caspar Gevaerts <note>original: "Io. Casperius Gevartius," a Flemish lawyer and philologist</note>, a Doctor of Law, in the third book of his *Selected Works*, chapter 7. He reports the same thing in almost the same words, only adding the most important detail: the specific place where he had seen it installed, namely the wall of the Church joined to the Villa. This is the same location indicated by Marcus Georgius Draudius of Frankfurt in his memorable notes on the works of Gaius Julius Solinus <note>Solinus was a 3rd-century Latin grammarian and compiler.</note>. When speaking of Bologna, he places it *At Peter's* <note>original: "Ad Petri"</note>, which is to say, on the walls of that Church dedicated to St. Peter and St. Paul, by the Soldiers...

<summary>The author cites 16th and 17th-century scholars, including Joannes Turrius and Jan Caspar Gevaerts, to provide physical evidence of the Aelia Laelia Crispis stone’s existence and location at a villa outside Bologna’s Mascharella Gate.</summary>

It references the location of the stone near the Mascarella Gate in Bologna and cites scholars like Fortunio Liceti to prove that such a sophisticated puzzle could only have been created by an ancient mind.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>
<meta>catchword: Not sur-</meta>

dedicated by the Joyful Friars <note>original: "Gaudentibus," referring to the Ordo Militiae Mariae Gloriosae, a chivalric lay order founded in Bologna</note> up until the year 1260, as the very ancient icons at the High Altar—later restored by our distinguished painter Pellegrino Tibaldi <note>A prominent Italian Mannerist architect and painter (1527–1596)</note>—sufficiently indicate even to the present day. For he himself speaks thus: *Outside the Mascarella Gate of Bologna, at the first milestone from the city, near the small villa of Marco Antonio Volta, a Bolognese Patrician, there exists on the wall of the temple joined to the villa a certain Epitaph carved in marble. Its antiquity is sufficiently proven by the letters, which are already worn away and fading due to the exposure of its position; it reads as follows:*

->To the Departed Spirits<- <note>original: "D. M." standing for "Diis Manibus," a common Roman funerary dedication</note>
### ÆLIA LÆLIA CRISPIS. etc.

The form of the Inscription also matches its outward structure; that is, the subject matter itself is so covered on all sides by a marvelous darkness of secrets that it wards off any suspicion of being a recent work. For it was always the peculiar custom of the Ancients—a thing not granted to more recent men—to excel incomparably in weaving riddles, as was sufficiently shown in the preface; and as the most learned Licetus <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), an Italian scholar who wrote extensively on the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma</note> copiously proves in Book 2, Chapter 2. *Since,* he added, *the elders were constantly engaged in the construction and solution of riddles, with rewards and punishments proposed for cleverness and ignorance (as is clear from Athenaeus, The Banquet of the Learned, Book 10, at the end, and Josephus's Against Apion, Book 1), therefore all the ancients, directing all the nerves of their talent to this matter, composed the most ingenious riddles that were most difficult to solve. Recent ones are in no way comparable to them, as they were fashioned by those not so practiced in creating them. Since, therefore, this monument of Aelia is the oldest, most skillful, and most difficult to explain of all the riddles <term>Griphus: a Greek term for a complex riddle or "net" of words</term> read among either Ancient or recent authors, it can undoubtedly be nothing but the work of an ancient author.*

<summary>The author argues that the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription must be ancient because its complexity exceeds the capabilities of modern forgers, citing its physical erosion and the historical tradition of classical riddles.</summary>
<keywords>Pellegrino Tibaldi, Mascarella Gate, Marco Antonio Volta, Fortunio Liceti, Athenaeus, Flavius Josephus, ancient riddles, Bologna history</keywords>

<page-num>105</page-num>
# Not born, yet Rising again, etc.
<sig>O</sig>
<meta>catchword: To whom</meta>

No less strong an argument for its Antiquity is provided by two other factors which, being inherent to this enigmatic structure, further strengthen and enlarge it. The first is the constant force of opposites, by which the obscure meanings of the Inscription seem to be entirely overshadowed; the second is the harmonious Triplicity of voices, through which a fuller harmony of the same is spread. For, that the use of Antithesis <note>The rhetorical practice of placing contrasting or opposite ideas together</note> was common among the Ancients is clearly proved by the most ancient poetess Sappho, who, when she had decided to weave a riddle <note>original: "gryphum," a term for a complex riddle or word-puzzle, literally a "net" or "knot"</note> for a letter, formed it with contrary and disagreeing sentiments in this manner:

> *A certain female nature keeps her offspring beneath*
> *her breast; but they, being mute, give forth a clear voice,*
> *both through the waves of the sea and across the entire*
> *continent,*
> *to whichever mortals it pleases, even those who are absent,*
> *though they have a deaf sense of hearing, they may hear them.*
<note>This riddle describes a letter: the 'offspring' are the written words which 'speak' to the reader despite being silent ink.</note>

Very similar to this is that of Alexis, of whom Athenaeus speaks in *The Banquet of the Learned* <note>original: "dipnos. lib. 10." referring to the 'Deipnosophistae' (The Dinner-Table Philosophers) by Athenaeus</note>, Book 10, toward the end.

> *Not mortal, nor immortal, but mixed with a certain*
> *temperament, so that it lives neither in the number of men,*
> *nor of the Gods, but is always being born,*
> *and always perishing; perceived by none,*
> *yet it is always most well-known to all.*
<note>Ancient commentators often interpreted this riddle as describing 'Sleep'.</note>

And that shameful one from Eubulus in *Sphingorion* <note>A lost work or collection of riddles named after the Sphinx</note> of this kind:

> *The tongueless one speaks, it is called by only one name, although*
> *it be both male and female.*
> *The steward of its own winds; now smooth, now shaggy,*
> *it utters foolish things to the foolish, it draws numbers from numbers;*
> *it is one, and many; invulnerable even when it is*
> *wounded.*

Likewise this one:

> *I do not wish to be joined in the marriage bed, though it pleases me to be a husband,*
> *I do not want a man in my chamber, through me my daughter is born;*
> *I do not want to endure the tomb, I know I must submerge myself in the earth.*

<summary>The author argues that the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription is ancient because it employs the classical technique of "antithesis" found in the riddles of Sappho, Alexis, and Eubulus. These ancient puzzles use paradoxical language—such as silent things speaking or things being both dead and alive—to obscure their true meaning.</summary>
<keywords>Antiquity, riddles, antithesis, Sappho, Alexis, Athenaeus, Eubulus, paradox, Aelia Laelia Crispis</keywords>

It follows from the previous page's discussion of Sappho and Alexis.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

To which this is likened:

> *Nothing was taken away, nothing was increased within,*
> *What had been, is not; what was not, has begun to be,*
> *And yet I did not find whatever I left behind before.*

Lest I pass over in silence that most common riddle of Plato in the fifth book of the *Laws*:

> *A man who is not a man, aiming at a bird that is not a bird, seeing yet not seeing, on a tree that is not a tree, sitting yet not sitting, with a stone that is not a stone, he throws yet does not throw;*
<note>This is a famous ancient riddle describing a eunuch with weak eyesight (the man not a man) hitting a bat (the bird not a bird) perched on a reed (the tree not a tree) with a pumice stone (the stone not a stone).</note>

And that other from the same Athenaeus <note>original: "Athen." referring to the author of 'The Banquet of the Learned'</note> in the same work (Book 3, Chapter 20, and Book 10, the last chapter), which is so akin and related to our own negation—"neither in the Heavens, nor in the Waters, nor in the Earths, but *it lies everywhere*"—that it altogether suggests he had seen our Enigma: namely, when he introduces the angry Cynulcus <note>A character in the dialogue known for his sharp tongue</note> railing thus: *“O pot-bellied man, devoted to the gullet, do you know nothing else? Can you not recount speeches or histories, or bring forth the beauty found in discourses? But you are always entirely occupied with this: ‘It lies, it does not lie,’ ‘It was said, it was not said,’ constantly searching.”* And further down: *“Moreover, this is the most ancient manner of Riddles* <note>original: "Griphorum," from the Greek 'griphos', meaning a fishing net or an intricate puzzle</note>*, and most suited to their nature. What is it that we do not know, and yet we all teach? What is the same thing nowhere and everywhere? Furthermore, what is the same thing in the Sky, the Earth, and the Sea?”*

That the Ancients were accustomed to arrange their speeches with a similar Triple harmony of voices is sufficiently proved by the most frequent shouts with which the Plebeians and the Roman People were moved to praise or rebuke their Leaders; as when, for example, the soldiers following the chariot of the triumphing Caesar mockingly sang that well-known urban song:

> *Caesar subdued the Gauls, Nicomedes subdued Caesar,*
> <note>original: "Gallias Cæsar subegit, Nicomedes Cæsarem." This refers to the scandalous rumor of Julius Caesar's youthful affair with King Nicomedes of Bithynia.</note>
> *Behold, Caesar now triumphs, who subdued the Gauls,*
> *Nicomedes does not triumph, who subdued Caesar.*

Or as Suetonius relates, when Germanicus <note>A popular Roman general and nephew of the Emperor Tiberius</note> had recovered from a serious illness, the people congratulated Tiberius with a single voice in this manner:

<meta>catchword: Sal-</meta>

<summary>This page compares the structure of the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription to famous ancient riddles by Plato and Athenaeus, arguing that its use of paradox and "triplicity" (triple repetition) is a hallmark of authentic classical antiquity.</summary>

# Not born, Rising again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c."</note>

->*Rome is safe,*<- <note>original: "Salva Roma"</note>
->*The Fatherland is safe,*<- <note>original: "Salva Patria"</note>
->*Germanicus is safe.*<- <note>original: "Salvus est Germanicus"</note>

Just as when the Senate itself finally—after asking Alexander Severus so many times in vain to take the name of the Antonines or the title of "The Great"—used this Triple Acclamation:
>*Antonine Alexander, may the gods preserve you,* <note>original: "Antonine Alexander, Dii te servent"</note>
>*Antonine Aurelius, may the gods preserve you,*
>*Antonine the Pious, may the gods preserve you;*
to which he added this triple negation, very much like our own riddle, as the historian Lampridius <note>A presumed author of the 'Augustan History' (Historia Augusta)</note> left written in his biography:
->*Only Elagabalus was worse than Commodus:*<- <note>original: "Pejor Commodo solus Eliogabalus"</note>

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| *Neither Emperor* | *nor Antonine* | *nor citizen* |
| *Neither Senator* | *nor Noble* | *nor Roman.* |
<note>original: "Nec Imperator nec Antoninus nec civis / Nec Senator nec Nobilis nec Romanus"</note>

So too did the Spartans once boast of their customs through a triple vaunt at Caelstium, saying that they were:
->*Educated without riches,*<- <note>original: "Sine divitiis educari"</note>
->*Living without walls,*<-
->*Conquering without life.*<- <note>likely referring to their willingness to die for victory</note>
Thus, with the triple sound of those famous words:
->*I came, I saw, I conquered*<- <note>original: "Veni Vidi Vici"</note>
Caesar boasted to adorn his triumph; and Cicero valued this triple harmony so much that he used it not infrequently. Indeed, we find he did so not only for the sake of wit and charm, as when he mocked a consul who held the office for only six hours in the afternoon with this joke:
->*We have had a consul*<-
->*So strict, and such a moral guardian,*<-
->*that during his term of office*<-
| | | |
|---|---|---|
| *no one lunched* | *no one dined* | *no one slept;* |
<note>original: "nemo pranderit / nemo cenarit / nemo dormierit" – Cicero is mocking Caninius Rebilus, whose consulship was so brief it didn't last a single night.</note>
In a similar fashion, the historian Trebellius Pollio <note>Another of the 'Augustan History' authors</note> exclaimed regarding Marius the Seventh—a blacksmith who reigned as emperor for only three days:
->*In one day he was made Emperor,*<- <note>original: "Una die factus est Imperator"</note>

->*On one day the Emperor was seen,*<- <note>original: "Alia die visus est Imperator"</note>
->*On the third day he was killed;*<- <note>original: "Tertia die interremptus est"</note>

But frequently, and in earnest during the weightier moments of a speech—whether he was offering praise or delivering a formal closing—as in this instance:

> *Have mercy on the family, Judges,* <note>original: "Miseremini familiæ, Iudices"</note>
> *Have mercy, most brave Fathers,* <note>original: "Miseremini fortissimi, Patres"</note>
> *Have mercy on the sons.* <note>original: "Miseremini filii"</note>

As in this one:

->*I warn you*<-
->*With the utmost diligence,*<- <note>original: "Pro summa diligentia moneo"</note>
->*I urge you by my consular authority,*<-
->*I implore you by the magnitude of the danger,*<-
->*That you look to the ease, the peace, and the safety*<-
->*Of your own,*<-
->*And to your own lives and the safety of all others*<-
->*that you may deliberate.*<-

And elsewhere:

->*If anything should happen,*<-
->*I shall die with a calm and prepared mind;*<- <note>original: "æquo animo paratoque moriar"</note>
->*for a shameful death cannot happen*<-
->*to a brave man,*<-
->*nor an untimely one to a consul,*<-
->*nor a wretched one to a wise man.*<-

Finally, in the speech for Milo <note>The *Pro Milone*, a famous legal defense by the Roman orator Cicero</note>, to pass over so many other examples:

->*For there is, Judges,*<-
->*this law not written, but inborn,*<- <note>original: "hæc non scripta, sed innata lex"</note>
->*which we have not*<-
->*learned, received, or read;*<-
->*but which from nature itself*<-
->*we have seized, drunk in, and expressed.*<-

So it is found on ancient stones—to stick to our physical subject matter—as seen in Gruter <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627), a scholar famous for his massive published collection of ancient Roman inscriptions</note>, page 1051, item 3.

From here, this "Triphony" of words <note>The "Triphonia" refers to the musical or harmonious arrangement of words into groups of three</note> rightly returns to what has been recalled today, having come back as if by the right of return <note>The author uses the legal term "postliminio," referring to the recovery of rights by a person returning from exile or captivity</note>. > *Greater than the France that demanded him.*

Therefore, inscribed stones now resonate with no sweeter harmony. Thus the Capitol in Rome, claiming for itself by right—according to this prescribed law—the heads of fish that exceed the established measure there:

# Not Born, She Rises Again, etc.
<note>This header likely refers to a specific theme or emblem associated with the renewal of Rome or a specific figure mentioned in the text.</note>

And more fully, with the triple-gift of words enriched, at the Hadrianic Pile, commonly called Castel Sant’Angelo:
<note>The "Hadrianic Pile" or *Adriana Moles* refers to the Mausoleum of Hadrian, which was converted into a fortress and papal residence.</note>

->THOSE THINGS WHICH ONCE WITHIN THIS FORTRESS<-
<note>original: "QVAE OLIM INTRA HANC ARCEM"</note>
->HAD COLLAPSED, BEEN OBSTRUCTED, AND DEFILED,<-
<note>original: "COLLAPSA IMPEDITA FOEDATA"</note>
->WERE<-
<note>original: "ERANT"</note>
->THROUGH PAUL III, SUPREME PONTIFF,<-
<note>original: "EA NVNC A PAVLO III. P. M."</note>
->TOWARD SOLID STRENGTH, SUBTLE BEAUTY,<-
<note>original: "AD SOLIDAM FIRMITATEM SVBTILEM VENVSTATEM"</note>
->AND CONVENIENT UTILITY<-
<note>original: "COMMODAM VTILITATEM"</note>
->CONSTRUCTED, ARRANGED, AND ADORNED,<-
<note>original: "EXTRVCTA DISPOSITA ORNATA"</note>
->AS THEY ARE NOW SEEN.<-
<note>original: "CONSPICIVNTVR"</note>

Indeed, within the Sacred Houses <note>churches</note>, by this same triple arrangement of words intermingled with other charms, even the mournful cenotaphs of the dead frequently rejoice. In the Church of Saint Philip, Founder of the Oratory, commonly called the *Chiesa Nuova* <note>The "New Church" or Santa Maria in Vallicella</note>:

->THE CESI BROTHERS<-
<note>original: "CAESII FRATRES"</note>
->PIETRO DONATO, CARDINAL PRIEST OF THE HOLY ROMAN CHURCH,<-
<note>original: "PETRVS DONATVS S. R. E. PRESB. CARD."</note>
->FOUNDED THE APSE OF THE TEMPLE,<-
<note>original: "TEMPLI APSIDEM FVNDAVIT"</note>
->PURCHASED MANY HOUSES,<-
<note>original: "AEDES MVLTAS COEMIT"</note>
->AND ENLARGED THE DWELLING OF THE PRIESTS;<-
<note>original: "SACERDOTVM HABITATIONEM AMPLIAVIT"</note>
->ANGELO, BISHOP OF TODI,<-
<note>original: "ANGELVS EPISC. TVDERTINVS"</note>
->THE CHAPEL<-
<note>original: "SACELLVM"</note>
->BUILT AND ENDOWED;<-
<note>original: "EXTRVXIT AC DOTAVIT"</note>
->HE FOUNDED, ADORNED, AND COMPLETED [IT].<-
<note>original: "FVNDAVIT ORNAVIT PERFECIT"</note>

The same is seen in the Church of Saint Mary of the Angels at the Baths, on the tombs of Cardinal Alciati and Cardinal Parisius, placed opposite each other. For while the first on the right (following the example of the most ancient epitaph of Queen Amalasuntha <note>Queen of the Ostrogoths, died 535 AD</note>, as recorded by Cassiodorus in his books...

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<note>While this header refers to a famous and mysterious Bolognese inscription, the text here continues the discussion of the "rule of three" in Roman epitaphs.</note>

...so commemorated: SHE LIVED CHASTELY, WORKED THE WOOL, AND KEPT THE HOME)
<note>original: "CASTA VIXIT, LANAM FECIT, DOMVM SERVAVIT"</note>
may these words themselves return:

->HE LIVED BY VIRTUE<-
<note>original: "VIRTUTE VIXIT"</note>
->HE LIVES BY MEMORY<-
<note>original: "MEMORIA VIVIT"</note>
->HE WILL LIVE BY GLORY<-
<note>original: "GLORIA VIVET"</note>

Another on the left, as if responding three times with this triple voice:

->THE BODY IS COVERED BY THE EARTH<-
<note>original: "CORPVS HVMO TEGITVR"</note>
->FAME FLIES THROUGH THE MOUTHS OF MEN<-
<note>original: "FAMA PER ORA VOLAT"</note>
->THE SPIRIT HOLDS THE STARS<-
<note>original: "SPIRITVS ASTRA TENET"</note>

->*In the Church of Saint Gregory on the Caelian Hill.*<-
<note>San Gregorio al Celio is a historic church in Rome, the starting point for the Gregorian mission to England.</note>

->TO GOD, THE BEST AND GREATEST<-
<note>original: "D O M" — Deo Optimo Maximo</note>

->TO FLAMINIO GUIDICCIONI, A NOBLEMAN OF LUCCA<-
<note>Flaminius Guidiccionius was a prominent legal and ecclesiastical figure from the noble Guidiccioni family of Lucca.</note>
->WHO,<-
->STRENUOUS ABROAD AND PRUDENT AT HOME,<-
<note>original: "FORIS STRENVVS DOMI PRVDENS"</note>
->WISE, DILIGENT, AND TIRELESS,<-
<note>original: "SAPIENS NAVVS IMPIGER"</note>
->WON OVER PRINCES BY HIS VIRTUE, INDIVIDUALS BY HIS COURTESY,<-
->AND HIS EQUALS BY HIS DUTIFUL SERVICE;<-
->AND BEING OVER SIXTY YEARS OF AGE,<-
->HAVING COMPLETED HIS SERVICE TO THE COURT AND TO LIFE,<-
<note>original: "EMERITIS AVLAE VITAEQVE STIPENDIIS" — a military metaphor comparing life and court service to a soldier earning his pay.</note>
->HE DEPARTED<-
->IN THE YEAR OF SALVATION 1615, ON THE 17TH OF JULY.<-
<note>original: "A. S. MDCXV. XVI. KAL. AVG." — 16 days before the Kalends of August.</note>

->*In the Church of Saint Mary above Minerva,*<-
<note>Santa Maria sopra Minerva is one of the major churches of the Dominican Order in Rome, built over the site of a temple to Minerva.</note>

# AELIA LAELIA CRISPIS
<note>This header refers to a famous enigmatic inscription from Bologna, though the text below concerns the death of a private individual and the deeds of Pope Clement IX.</note>

->DIED<-
<note>original: "OBIIT"</note>
->ON THE 23RD DAY OF DECEMBER<-
->IN THE YEAR OF OUR LORD 1642<-
->AT THE AGE OF 36<-

---

->*In the anniversary funeral procession for Clement IX, instituted within the Liberian Basilica from the year 1680.*<-
<note>The "Liberian Basilica" is another name for Santa Maria Maggiore, one of the four major papal basilicas in Rome.</note>

### TO CLEMENT IX, SUPREME PONTIFF
<note>Pope Clement IX (r. 1667–1669), born Giulio Rospigliosi, was a beloved figure known for his mediation of European conflicts and his patronage of the arts, specifically his collaboration with Gian Lorenzo Bernini.</note>

->THE BREVITY OF HIS LIFE INTERRUPTED HIS DEEDS<-
->YET IN THAT MODEST SPAN<-
->THE <term>ANNONA</term> WAS INCREASED, POVERTY RELIEVED<-
<note>The "Annona" was the Roman office responsible for the grain supply and food security of the city.</note>
->TAXATION WAS EASED<-
->THE SICK WERE VISITED, THE SUPPLICANT ADMITTED<-
->THE PENITENT WAS HEARD<-
->WAR WAS SETTLED, <term>CRETE</term> WAS SUPPORTED<-
<note>This refers to the "Aix-la-Chapelle" peace treaty and Clement's efforts to assist the Venetians against the Ottomans during the Siege of Candia in Crete.</note>
->THE BRIDGE WAS ADORNED<-
<note>Referring to the Bridge of Sant'Angelo, where Clement commissioned Bernini to design the ten statues of angels carrying the instruments of the Passion.</note>
->GENEROSITY WAS DIFFUSED EVERYWHERE<-
->AND THIS TEMPLE WAS DESTINED FOR A JEWEL-LIKE<-
->RESTORATION<-
->BUT HOPE WITHERED IN THE JEWEL<-
<note>Clement IX planned a massive renovation of the apse of Santa Maria Maggiore, but he died before the "jewel" of his architectural vision could be completed.</note>
->FOR<-
->WE SAW THE DAWN OF SO GREAT A REIGN, BUT NOT ITS NOON<-
->YET THE ONE IS A SIGN OF THE OTHER<-

To which, in a certain way, though it is playful, this follows:

> HAIL, <term>PARCAE</term>, WHO SPARINGLY SPUN THE LIFE OF THE DOCTOR
> <note>The Parcae were the three Fates of Roman mythology who spun, measured, and cut the thread of human life.</note>
> LEST YOU SHOULD SEEM TO BE MERCIFUL;
> <note>original: "NE PARCERE VIDEREMINI" — a pun on the name "Parcae" and the verb "parcere," meaning to spare or show mercy.</note>
> HE HAD ALREADY LIVED FAR TOO LONG.

<sig>SEXA-</sig>
<note>Catchword for the following page.</note>

<summary>This page records the death of an individual in 1642 and provides a detailed eulogy for Pope Clement IX, praising his civil works, his military support for Crete, and his artistic contributions to Rome. It concludes with a satirical epigram about a doctor and the Fates.</summary>
<keywords>Clement IX, Santa Maria Maggiore, Annona, Crete, Bridge of Sant'Angelo, Parcae, epitaphs, 17th-century Rome</keywords>

<page-num>115</page-num>

<header>Rising Again, Unborn, etc.</header>
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This likely refers to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma mentioned on the previous page, often called the "nobilis aenigma."</note>

<sig>P 2</sig>

<meta>Heavy bleed-through from the reverse side of the page.</meta>

->APPROPRIATELY YOU HAVE CAST DOWN THE SIXTY-YEAR-OLD FROM LIFE<-
<note>original: "SEXAGENARIVM OPPORTVNE DE VITA DEIECISTIS"</note>

->WHO, HAD HE LIVED LONGER, MORTAL HEALTH<-
->WOULD HAVE BECOME NEARLY IMMORTAL<-

->FOR HE DOUBTLESS ENVIED THE SPIRITS OF THE UNDERWORLD<-

->THE FARE FOR <term>CHARON</term>, THE SHADES FOR <term>PLUTO</term><-
<note>Charon was the ferryman of the dead; Pluto was the god of the underworld. The text suggests the doctor’s skill kept people alive so long that the underworld was being deprived of its "business."</note>

->THE PROCESSIONS FOR <term>LIBITINA</term><-
<note>Libitina was the Roman goddess of funerals and burials.</note>

->THE WAILING FOR THE MOURNERS, THE OINTMENTS FOR THE EMBALMERS<-

->THE FUNERALS FOR THE UNDERTAKERS<-

->THE BIERS FOR THE COFFIN-BEARERS, THE RANKS FOR THE FUNERAL DIRECTORS<-

->THE DIRGES FOR THE FLUTE-PLAYER<-

->YOU HAVE RESTORED<-

->EACH THING TO EACH, ALL THINGS TO ALL<-

->NOW THAT THE DOCTORS ARE DEPARTING<-

->FEVER, THE EXILE, SHALL RETURN; HEALTH ITSELF SHALL FALL ILL<-

->REMEDIES WILL LIE IDLE; DISEASES WILL RULE THE KINGDOM<-

->UNWILLING LIVES SHALL DIE<-

->GREETINGS AGAIN, O <term>FATES</term>, YOU WHO WISH<-
->NO ONE TO BE SAFE<-
<note>The "Fates" (Parcae) were the three goddesses—Nona, Decima, and Morta—who spun, measured, and cut the thread of human life.</note>

In this, our City follows the example of Rome <note>original: "Vrbi," referring to the city of Rome</note>, holding fast to a Triple Consonance, and to these two (lest I delay you with so many others, most kind Reader), written by the sweet-speaking Achillini, formerly my Master in poetry—may his bones rest softly:
<note>Giovanni Filoteo Achillini (1466–1533) was a Bolognese scholar, musician, and poet.</note>

### TO CAMILLO GESSI
<note>original: "CAMILLO GYPSIO." Camillo Gessi was a prominent Bolognese senator and legal scholar.</note>

->SENATOR, ADVOCATE, PUBLIC LECTURER<-

->UPON WHOSE DEATH<-

->THE SENATE MOURNS, THE FORUM GRIEVES, THE CHAIR IS SILENT<-
<note>The "Chair" refers to the professor's seat (cathedra) at the University.</note>

->WHOSE NAME IS MARKED WITH A WHITE STONE<-
<note>To mark a day or a person with a "white stone" (albo lapillo) was a Roman custom signifying good fortune, virtue, or a lucky day.</note>

->FOR GOOD<-

<vocab>Sixty-year-old, Charon, Pluto, Libitina, Fates, Camillo Gessi, Achillini, Senator, Advocate, Public Lecturer</vocab>

### CLEMENT IX, <term>SUPREME PONTIFF</term>
<note>original: "P. O. M." stands for "Pontifex Optimus Maximus," a title for the Pope meaning "Greatest and Best High Priest."</note>
->THE BREVITY OF HIS LIFE INTERRUPTED HIS DEEDS<-
->YET IN A MODEST SPAN OF TIME<-
->THE GRAIN SUPPLY WAS INCREASED, POVERTY WAS RELIEVED<-
->TAXES WERE LIGHTENED<-
->THE SICK WERE VISITED, THE SUPPLIANT WAS ADMITTED<-
->THE PENITENT WAS HEARD<-
->WAR WAS SETTLED, CRETE WAS SUPPORTED<-
<note>Clement IX was instrumental in negotiating the Peace of Aix-la-Chapelle (1668) and sent significant aid to the Venetians during the Siege of Candia in Crete against the Ottoman Empire.</note>
->THE BRIDGE WAS ADORNED<-
<note>The Pope commissioned Gian Lorenzo Bernini to create the ten angel statues for the Ponte Sant'Angelo in Rome.</note>
->MUNIFICENCE WAS DIFFUSED EVERYWHERE<-
->AND THIS TEMPLE WAS DESTINED FOR A SPLENDID<-
->RESTORATION<-
->BUT HOPE WITHERED IN THE GEM<-
->FOR<-
->WE SAW THE DAWN OF SO GREAT A SOVEREIGNTY, AND NOT<-
->ITS NOON<-
<note>Clement IX reigned for only two years and five months (1667–1669), leading to the metaphor of a reign that ended at "sunrise" rather than "midday."</note>
->BUT THE ONE IS THE INDICATOR OF THE OTHER<-

To which, in a way, although playful, this following inscription corresponds:

> GREETINGS, O <term>FATES</term>, YOU WHO HAVE SPARINGLY SPUN
<note>The Fates (Parcae) were the three goddesses of destiny in mythology who spun the thread of human life.</note>
> THE LIFE FOR THE DOCTOR
> LEST YOU SHOULD SEEM TO BE SPARING HIM
> HE HAD ALREADY LIVED FAR TOO MUCH

<header>Rising though not born, etc.</header>
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens." This is a continuation of the themes related to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma mentioned in the previous header, often interpreted as a soul or a chemical substance that "rises without being born."</note>
<sig>P 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: TO THE GOOD.</meta>

->YOU HAVE OPPORTUNELY CAST DOWN THE SIXTY-YEAR-OLD FROM LIFE<-
<note>This is a satirical epitaph addressed to the Fates (the Parcae), thanking them for finally killing a doctor.</note>
->WHO, HAD HE LIVED LONGER, MORTAL HEALTH WOULD HAVE BEEN<-
->NEARLY IMMORTAL<-
->HE WAS ENVYING, FORSOOTH, THE SHADES OF THE UNDERWORLD<-
->CHARON HIS FARE, PLUTO THE SHADOWS<-
<note>Charon is the mythological ferryman of the dead; Pluto is the god of the Underworld.</note>
->LIBITINA HER PROCESSIONS<-
<note>Libitina was the Roman goddess of funerals and burials.</note>
->THE MOURNING WOMEN THEIR WAILING, THE EMBALMERS THEIR OINTMENTS<-
->THE PALLBEARERS THE FUNERALS<-
->THE BIER-CARRIERS THE COFFINS, THE FUNERAL DIRECTORS THE RANKS<-
->THE FLUTE-PLAYERS THEIR DIRGES<-
->YOU HAVE RESTORED<-
->EACH THING TO EACH PERSON, ALL THINGS TO EVERYONE<-
->NOW THAT THE DOCTORS ARE DEPARTING<-
->FEVER, THE EXILE, IS SET TO RETURN; HEALTH ITSELF WILL FALL ILL<-
->REMEDIES WILL LIE IDLE; DISEASES WILL BE IN THEIR KINGDOM<-
->LIVES WILL DIE AGAINST THEIR WILL<-
->HAIL AGAIN, O FATES, WHO WISH NO ONE TO BE SAFE<-
<note>original: "Parcae." The three Fates of mythology who spin, measure, and cut the thread of life. The irony here is that by killing the doctor, the Fates have restored the "business" of death to the various funeral trades.</note>

---

In this, our City <note>Bologna</note> complies with the City <note>Rome</note>, holding fast to the Triple Consonance, and with these two (not to delay you with so many others, most kind Reader) written by the sweet-speaking Achillini, once my teacher in poetic arts, may his bones rest softly:
<note>Giovanni Filoteo Achillini (1466–1533) was a celebrated Bolognese poet, scholar, and antiquarian.</note>

### TO CAMILLO GESSI
<note>original: "Camillo Gypsio." Camillo Gessi was a prominent Bolognese jurist and senator.</note>

->UPON WHOSE PASSING<-
->THE SENATE MOURNS, THE FORUM GRIEVES, THE CHAIR IS SILENT<-
<note>The "Chair" refers to the professorial cathedra or university seat.</note>
->WHO, WITH A WHITE STONE<-
<note>original: "Albo lapillo." A Roman expression referring to marking a lucky or virtuous day/person with a white pebble.</note>

# Not Born, She Rises Again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens." This title likely refers back to the enigmatic nature of the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription mentioned on the previous page, or serves as a poetic heading for the theme of memory outlasting life.</note>

->WITH A TRUNK FOUR FEET IN DIAMETER<-

<note>original: "semiditonus." A technical term in music or prosody referring to a specific interval or rhythm, used here to describe the meter of the poem.</note>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<note>original: "Aelia Laelia Crispis." This is the title of a famous archaeological mystery in Bologna: an enigmatic inscription that has baffled scholars for centuries. Here, it serves as a general heading for this collection of epitaphs.</note>

->For Edmund Merille, Doctor of Both Laws<-
<note>original: "Edmundi Merilli I. V. D." Edmund Merille (1579–1647) was a renowned French jurist. "I. V. D." stands for *Juris Utriusque Doctor*, meaning a doctor of both Civil and Canon Law.</note>

| Bartolus | and Baldus | Jason |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *Themis* | *Eunomia* | *Peitho* |
<note>This table compares three legendary medieval jurists (Bartolus of Saxoferrato, Baldus de Ubaldis, and Jason de Mayno) to three Greek personifications of law and order: Themis (Divine Justice), Eunomia (Good Law), and Peitho (Persuasion).</note>

Indeed, just as Pentadius plays with Virgil’s style by doubling the triple-structure of the lines:
<note>Pentadius was a minor Latin poet known for his "echoing" verses where the first part of a line is repeated or mirrored in the second.</note>
