<meta>This page continues the analysis of "triad" inscriptions—epitaphs or poems where words are arranged in three parallel columns to compare different aspects of a person’s life. It shifts focus to the scholarly debate and fame surrounding a specific, mysterious inscription from Bologna known as the "Aelia Laelia Crispis."</meta>

### The Unborn Woman Rising Again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This is a cryptic phrase often associated with the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, suggesting a soul or entity that was never "born" in the traditional sense but exists or rises.</note>

| *Mind* | *Reason* | *Fame* |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *With gifts* | *With skill* | *With sounds.* |

<margin>In Bologna, in the Charterhouse Monastery.</margin>
| *Teacher* | *Leader* | *Monk* |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *I taught* | *I conquered* | *By my habit* |
| *Words* | *Sin* | *The glades* |
| *With my voice* | *With fear* | *By flight.* |
<note>This epitaph describes a man who held three roles: a teacher (Doctor), a military leader (Dux), and finally a monk (Monachus). The columns explain how he functioned in each: he taught words with his voice, conquered sin through the fear of God, and retreated to the mountain glades or "wilds" (saltus) by fleeing the world in a monk's habit.</note>

This so-often praised Triple Harmony of words—which, in our Inscription, leaps out even further from the balanced dissonance of opposing antitheses—has attracted the whole world with such great interest that the most celebrated writers of Eloquence compete to adorn it with praise. Even the most sublime wits of our age attempt to imitate it, though often with unsuccessful results.

Among others, the most renowned champions of wisdom and erudition—the Reverend Fathers of the Society of Jesus—praise this style in that golden work, adorned with so many gems, *The Queen’s Palace of Eloquence* (page 166, column 2 and following). There, after they had treated the subject of Riddles at length, they established that *while the forms of Riddles are various and manifold, those which are constructed through mere negations bring greater delight than the others.* Finally, they remind the reader that in these negations or antitheses (which lend such grace to Inscriptions), this excellent one—carved in large letters in the territory of Bologna and encountered by all who seek it—has surpassed all others and always led the way.

The highly learned and elegant writer Octavius Boldonius—the honor and splendor of the Barnabite family—agrees in his work on the method of composing Epigraphs, Elogies, and Inscriptions. While he generally banishes obscurity from inscriptions, he was not so repelled by this "Aelian" riddle that he didn't take it upon himself to explain it in Book 3, Member 10, according to the mind of Caspar Gevartius. He first celebrated it thus: *For the rest, the riddle—*
<note>original: "Octavius Boldonius" (1600–1680) was a member of the Barnabite order and a scholar of epigraphy. "Casparii Gevartii" refers to Jean-Gaspard Gevaerts (1593–1666), a famous Flemish philologist and jurist. The "Aelian riddle" refers to the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription mentioned in the previous page.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the scholarly fame of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" riddle in Bologna, citing Jesuit and Barnabite authors who admired its use of "triple harmony" and paradoxical negations.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Bologna, epigraphy, Jesuit, Barnabites, Octavio Boldoni, Caspar Gevartius, riddles, antithesis, triple harmony</keywords>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<note>This refers to a famous enigmatic inscription found in Bologna, which has puzzled scholars for centuries. It describes a woman who is "neither man nor woman," "neither dead nor alive," etc.</note>

->HE DEFILES THE SACRED RITES OF THE GODS<-
->TRAMPLES THE LAWS, STRIPS TEMPLES, BEREAVES CITIES<-
->OF THE BRAVE, THE RIGHTEOUS, THE HOLY<-
->BOTH OF BLOOD AND OF GLORY<-
->DISHONORABLE AND MOURNFUL<-
->BY WASTEFUL OUTPOURING<-
->HE SQUANDERS<-
<note>original: "DIVORVM SACRA CONTAMINAT..." This passage uses a series of strong verbs and nouns to describe a destructive figure, perhaps a tyrant or personification of War, mirroring the structure of the Aelia Laelia enigma.</note>

->CITIZENS, MAGISTRATES, GENTLE FOLK<-
->NEIGHBOR, INHABITANT, FELLOW-DWELLER<-
->WHOEVER YOU ARE, A FRIENDLY MAN<-
->PAUSE AND READ<-
->THESE POROUS HIDING PLACES SWEETLY WEEPING TOGETHER<-
->AND THESE WATERS ENTICINGLY TURNING TO HONEY<-
->THROUGH THE FLOWERY WHIRLPOOLS<-
->TO JUPITER OF THE OUTFLOWING, IN THE MANNER OF PROSERPINA<-
<note>original: "PROSERPIGINOSEI." This archaic-sounding Latin seems to describe a fountain or a lush, grotto-like setting dedicated to a deity, using rhythmic, repetitive word clusters.</note>
->A MIRROR FOR THE EYES, A DELIGHT FOR THE LIPS<-
->A HARMONY FOR THE EARS<-
->THEY GIVE WITH A GIFT OF EVERLASTING PLEASURE<-
->HERE, THEREFORE, ACCORDING TO YOUR WILL AND PLEASURE<-
->WATCH, DRINK, REST, etc.<-

---

->OMASIUS, DUKE OF FAGONIA<-
<note>original: "OMASIVS FAGONIAE DVX." This is a famous mock-epitaph often paired with the Aelia Laelia Crispis. "Omasius" refers to tripe or the stomach, and "Fagonia" suggests a land of eating (from the Greek *phagein*), making this the "Duke of the Belly."</note>
->LORD, VICTOR, PRINCE<-
->HERE I LIE<-
->LET NO HUNGRY MAN NAME ME<-
->LET THE FASTING MAN PASS BY<-
->LET THE SOBER MAN GREET ME<-
->LET HIM BE MY HEIR WHO IS ABLE<-
<note>That is, whoever has the stomach or the wealth to feast as he did.</note>
->MY SUBJECT WHO WISHES<-
->MY ENEMY WHO DARES<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma, transitioning from the previous page's mock-epitaph of Omasius (the "Duke of Tripe") to other similar rhetorical puzzles. ### The Not-born Rising Again, etc.

->LIVE, O BELLIES, AND FAREWELL!<-
<note>original: "VIVITE VENTRES ET VALETE." This is likely a humorous address to gluttons, fitting the theme of the "Omasius" (Tripe) inscription mentioned previously.</note>

If to these Triple Harmonies one adds Antitheses—which even Cicero himself sometimes found pleasing, as when he says in his book *On the Orator*:

> *Speech*
> *polished concerning rough matters,*
> *full concerning meager matters,*
> *new concerning common matters;*
<note>original: "Oratio de horridis rebus nitida..." from Cicero's *De Oratore*. The author is showing that the "neither/nor" or "opposite" style of the puzzles has roots in classical rhetoric.</note>

And through the power of these devices, these modern Inscriptions evolve more fully into enigmas, very similar to our own. This same thing occurs, as is clear from the subjects below, which are so distant and different from our own. First, from that inscription of a certain Physician, found in the work of Franciscus Sweertius <note>original: "Franciscum Suvertium." François Sweerts (1567–1629) was a Flemish historian and author of *Thesaurus Epitaphiorum*, a collection of famous epitaphs.</note> mentioned above, as well as in the *Thesaurus*:

->WAYFARER<-
->STOP, READ, WALK ON<-
->HERE LIES ANDOR VORTUNIUS<-
->NEITHER SLAVE, NOR SOLDIER, NOR PHYSICIAN<-
->NEITHER GLADIATOR-TRAINER, NOR SHOEMAKER, NOR THIEF<-
->NEITHER LAWYER, NOR USURER<-
->BUT ALL OF THESE<-
->HE LIVED HIS LIFE NEITHER IN THE CITY, NOR IN THE COUNTRY<-
->NEITHER AT HOME, NOR ABROAD<-
->NEITHER AT SEA, NOR ON LAND<-
->NEITHER HERE, NOR ELSEWHERE<-
->BUT EVERYWHERE<-
->HE WAS TAKEN AWAY NEITHER BY HUNGER, NOR POISON, NOR DISEASE<-
->NEITHER BY THE SWORD, NOR BY THE NOOSE<-
->BUT BY ALL OF THEM<-
->I PLACED THIS HERE<-
->TO HIM<-
->NEITHER DEBTOR, NOR HEIR, NOR RELATIVE<-
->NEITHER NEIGHBOR, NOR CLOSE FRIEND<-
<note>This "Andor Vortunius" inscription is a famous mock-epitaph. Like the Aelia Laelia Crispis, it uses "negation" (neither/nor) to create a riddle about a person's identity and death. These were popular intellectual games for Renaissance humanists.</note>

<summary>The author explains how classical rhetorical devices like antithesis, used by Cicero, influenced the creation of modern "enigmatic inscriptions." As an example, he provides the mock-epitaph of Andor Vortunius, which uses a series of contradictions to describe a man who was everything and nothing.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, De Oratore, Franciscus Sweertius, Andor Vortunius, antithesis, rhetorical enigmas, inscriptions, mock-epitaphs</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of paradoxical "neither/nor" inscriptions. It moves from the specific Aelia Laelia Crispis puzzle to other epitaphs that utilize similar rhetorical contradictions to describe complex or ironic lives and deaths.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

->NEITHER MASS, NOR STONE, NOR TOMB<-
->BUT ALL<-
->NEITHER FOR HER, NOR FOR YOU, NOR FOR ME<-
->NEITHER ILL-WILLING NOR WELL-WILLING<-
->BUT FOR ALL<-
<note>original: "NEQVE MOLEM NEQVE LAPIDEM..." This continues the famous Bologna Enigma, where the subject is defined entirely by what it is not, or by being multiple contradictory things at once.</note>

To which corresponds the epitaph of a Woman from Basel, from the same *Treasury*:
<note>The "Treasury" refers to the *Thesaurus Epitaphiorum* by François Sweerts, a famous 17th-century collection of tombstone inscriptions.</note>

->OF PALLADIAN VENUS OR VENERIAN PALLAS<-
<note>original: "PALLADIAE VENERIS VEL VENERIAE PALLADIS." This blends the names of Pallas Athena (goddess of wisdom/war) and Venus (goddess of love), implying the woman was both wise and beautiful.</note>
->YOU SEE A MARRIAGE CHAMBER LIKE UNTO A GRAVE<-
->WHERE A VIRGIN AND AT ONCE A MATRON, MARRIED AND UNMARRIED<-
->NEITHER STERILE, NOR FERTILE, NOR A WIFE<-
->NOR A MISTRESS<-
->A SPOUSE WITHOUT A SPOUSE, SINGLE WITHOUT CELIBACY<-
->FOR FIFTEEN YEARS WITHOUT COMPLAINT<-
->SHE LAY WITH A MAN WHO WAS HALF A MAN<-
<note>original: "cum viro iacuit semiviro." This likely implies the husband was a eunuch or impotent, creating the paradox of a "marriage" that remained a "virginity."</note>
->WHILE NATURE MARVELS THAT A WOMAN FOR SO LONG COULD<-
->SO LIE OR BE SO SILENT<-

And finally—so that I may pass over that one from the *Tears of Truth* by Alitophilus <note>Alitophilus was a pseudonym used in various 17th-century satirical or philosophical works</note>, that one from the Reverend Father Jacob Masen <note>Jacob Masen (1606–1681) was a Jesuit scholar famous for his study of enigmas and symbolic art</note>, and many others of this sort—from the papers formerly of Father Constantine of the Cross (to whose friendly ashes I, a Licentiate of Sacred Theology, add this as a finishing touch), I add another regarding a certain drunken lictor <term>Lictor: a Roman civil servant who acted as a bodyguard and attendant to magistrates</term> who was drowned in a well at the end of the year, before sunrise, while he was drawing water with which to extinguish a house suddenly burning with fire:

->A STONE FOR THE FEET OF THE GODS<-
<note>original: "DIS PEDIBVS SAXVM." This is a pun on the common Roman funerary dedication "Dis Manibus" (To the spirits of the dead). By replacing "Manibus" (hands/spirits) with "Pedibus" (feet), the author mocks the drunkard's stumble into the well.</note>
->IN THIS TOMB, NOT OF STONE, BUT OF WATER<-
->HE WAS BURIED ALIVE WHO WAS CAST OUT DEAD<-
->BREATHLESS FROM THE FIRE<-
<note>original: "EX IGNE EXANIMIS." A final paradox: though he drowned in water, it was the fire (or the effort to fight it) that took his life/breath.</note>

<meta>catchword: SOUL- [Latin: ANI-]</meta>

<summary>This page presents three paradoxical Latin epitaphs: a continuation of the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma, a riddle regarding a virginal wife from Basel, and a humorous yet tragic inscription for a drunken official who drowned while fighting a fire.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, epitaphs, paradox, Basel, Jacob Masen, Constantine of the Cross, rhetorical puzzles, funerary inscriptions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of "enigmatic epitaphs" mentioned at the end of the previous page. Having finished with the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" and the "Woman from Basel," the author now presents another famous Baroque riddle-epitaph, likely one cited by the Jesuit scholar Jacob Masen. This particular text describes a death that occurs between states—neither fully in one element nor another, at a time that is neither one hour nor the next.</meta>

# Not Born, Rising Again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens." This title often refers to emblems or riddles concerning the soul or the Phoenix, suggesting something that exists beyond the normal cycle of birth.</note>

->HE LOST HIS LIFE IN THE WATERS<-
->WHILE SEEKING TO EXTINGUISH THE FLAME FROM THE ROOF-TILE AS A PROTECTOR<-
<note>original: "flamam ex imbrice extincturus vindice." This suggests the man was on a roof trying to put out a fire using water from a gutter-tile (*imbrex*).</note>
->HE WAS EXTINGUISHED BY THE WATER<-
->FROM EXCESSIVE HEAT HE PASSED TO THE WATERS OF THE SNOWS<-
->AND EVEN UNTO THE UNDERWORLD GOES HIS SIN<-
<note>original: "et usque ad inferos peccatum illius." This is an allusion to the Latin Vulgate, Job 24:19: "Drought and heat consume the snow waters: so doth the grave those which have sinned." It implies the man’s "heat" (perhaps a metaphor for passion or sin) was met by the cold of death.</note>
->AQUARIUS, SHINING IN THE HEAVENS, WAS SETTING<-
<note>Aquarius is the "Water-Bearer" constellation; its "setting" coincides with the man's fall into the water.</note>
->WHEN THE WRETCHED MAN FELL FROM THE SKY INTO THE WATERS<-
->AND THE FISHERMAN DID NOT FIND HIS PREY IN THE WAVES<-
->BUT HAVING BECOME THE PREY OF THE WAVES<-
->HE WAS FISHED OUT WITH A HOOK<-
->HE PERISHED<-
->NEITHER BY FIRE, NOR BY AIR, NOR BY WATER<-
->BUT BY ALL<-
->NEITHER BY WINE, NOR BY WATER<-
->BUT BY BOTH<-
->NEITHER DRUNK, NOR SOBER<-
->BUT BOTH<-
->IN A YEAR<-
->NEITHER CLOSED, NOR OPENED<-
->IN A MONTH<-
->NEITHER MEASURED, NOR PASSED THROUGH<-
->ON A DAY<-
->NEITHER THE FIRST, NOR THE LAST, NOR THE MIDDLE<-
->AT AN HOUR<-
->NEITHER OF LIGHT, NOR OF SHADOWS<-
<note>This concluding series of paradoxes is a classic feature of the "Enigma" genre, where the subject dies in a transitional moment—perhaps at twilight (neither light nor shadow) on a leap day or a transitional feast day.</note>

<term>Animam in aquis amisit</term>: "He lost his life in the waters." A common opening for epitaphs of the drowned.
<term>Aquarius</term>: The Water-Bearer constellation, used here for poetic irony as the man drowns while the water-bearer sets.
<term>Piscator</term>: Fisherman.
<term>Aenigma</term>: A riddle; a popular literary form in the 17th century used to demonstrate wit and theological mystery.
<term>Epitaphium</term>: An inscription on a tomb.

<summary>This page features a highly rhetorical, paradoxical epitaph for a man who died falling from a roof into water while trying to put out a fire. The text uses "neither/nor" constructions to describe his death as a mystery of elements and time.</summary>
<keywords>Aquarius, paradox, drowning, Jacob Masen, Enigma, Job 24:19, Baroque literature, epitaphs</keywords>

FOR YEARS

And finally, as it pleases others such as the Reverend Father Jacob Malcotius <note>Jacques Malcot (1573–1653), a Jesuit writer known for his works on emblems and sacred history.</note>, and many others from here and there, especially from the papers formerly of Father Peter Canisius <note>St. Peter Canisius (1521–1597), a central figure in the Counter-Reformation; the text suggests he collected or composed these intellectual puzzles.</note>, whose anagram is inscribed here (as you will see below), concerning a certain drunken guest who died in the snow.

->SACRED TO THE FEET OF THE DEAD<-
<note>original: "Dis Pedibus Sacrum." This is a witty parody of the common Roman funerary formula "Dis Manibus Sacrum" (Sacred to the Spirits of the Dead). By changing "Manibus" (Spirits/Hands) to "Pedibus" (Feet), the author points to the physical fall or the lowly nature of the subject's death.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of enigmatic and paradoxical inscriptions. While the previous page focused on a specific riddle concerning a man who died in the snow—using the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" style of paradox—this page begins a broader defense of such intellectual play. It argues that even the serious Romans and ancient philosophers valued wit and games as a necessary balance to gravity.</meta>

->**That** this Laelian Inscription,<-
->written enigmatically, does not lack<-
->the ancient purity of speech;<-
->since the Ancients sometimes<-
->wrote Epitaphs enigmatically,<-
->and indeed even<-
->jokingly.<-

# Chapter VIII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral motifs and scrolling vines.</image-desc>
Always and everywhere, indulgence has been granted to refined wit <term>eutrapelia: a Greek term for a "well-turning" or versatile mind, often used for sophisticated humor</term>, nor did the Romans <term>Quirites: a formal name for the citizens of Rome in their civil capacity</term> always cultivate gravity so constantly that they did not at times—both for themselves and for the People, who anxiously desired nothing more than *bread* and *Circuses*—mingle playful things with their holidays, softening the accustomed and continuous severity of their discipline. The massive ruins of the Circuses, Goalposts <note>Metae: the conical pillars at each end of the Roman circus around which chariots turned</note>, Sea-battles <note>Naumachiae: staged naval battles in flooded arenas</note>, and Amphitheaters, raised for the public assembly of an exulting Nation, still bear witness to this,

> --- when the crowded people
> *Joyfully clapped three times in the galleries;* <note>original: "thesauris," likely referring to the seated sections of the theater or circus</note>
> indeed, even amidst mournful spectacles, the serious tragedian
> *Soon also stripped the rustic Satyrs naked, and, harsh as he was,*
> *Tried his hand at a joke while maintaining his dignity* --- <note>A reference to Horace's *Ars Poetica*, describing how early drama transitioned from serious tragedy to the more ribald "Satyr plays."</note>

The ancient coins <note>numismata</note> of so many Emperors suggest this, with these customary letters: **HE PERFORMED THE CENTENNIAL GAMES** <note>original: "LVDOS SAECVLARES FECIT"</note>, inscribed here and there on the reverse side. Plato commands this, advising the more serious Philosophers to sacrifice to the Graces so that they might become more pleasant. This is urged not only by the Rhetoricians—who advise that festive wordplay and joyful irony should sometimes be introduced as a relief from tedious speech—but also by the books of the Socratic Philosophers,

<meta>catchword: books,</meta>

<summary>This page provides a historical and philosophical justification for "enigmatic" or "joking" inscriptions, arguing that the ancient Romans and philosophers like Plato valued wit (eutrapelia) and public games as necessary reliefs from the severity of life and statecraft.</summary>
<keywords>eutrapelia, Quirites, bread and circuses, Roman games, Ludi Saeculares, numismatics, Plato, Horace, Satyr plays, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription. The previous page ended while discussing how even serious Roman playwrights included humor (specifically mentioning Satyr plays). This page continues that thought, citing Cicero and the Roman tradition of "apophthegms" (witty sayings) and humorous inscriptions for animals to prove that the Ancients often balanced gravity with playfulness.</meta>

...books, in which: *many witty sayings of many people are contained, which are called Apophthegms <term>Apophthegmata: from the Greek, meaning a concise, pointed, and witty saying or maxim</term>, such as those collected by Cato the Elder*, as Cicero reports in *On Duties* <note>original: "Cicer. de Offic." referring to Cicero's *De Officiis* 1.104, where he discusses refined versus coarse humor</note>. Finally, the greatest minds of that era teach us this, for they did not disdain to celebrate in verse now a cabbage, now a nettle, now fever itself, and finally even a gnat <note>This refers to a tradition of "paradoxical encomia"—poems praising trivial or unpleasant things, such as the *Culex* (The Gnat) attributed to Virgil</note>.

Therefore, as often as we find the same thing happening in Inscriptions, there is no reason at all for us to wonder, or to withdraw credit from them in such a way that, immediately banishing them from the true and ancient ones, we categorize them too severely among the spurious and apocryphal, as Gruter <term>Jan Gruter (1560–1627): a famous scholar and epigrapher known for his massive collection of Roman inscriptions; he was often skeptical of the authenticity of unusual texts</term> does. For we do not always find the Ancients to be mourning when composing Epitaphs, nor never playing. Indeed, since various kinds of Inscriptions were created, it was right that the style and words also varied, so that names would be harmonious with the subjects, and the eloquence would not be judged as mismatched to the theme. What befits a Consul or an Emperor does not suit a commoner or a freedman; nor is the same funeral eulogy that fits an INCOMPARABLE MASTER, a MOST DEAREST WIFE, or a BELOVED SON appropriate for a snatched-away puppy, a dead cat, or a deceased steed. 

Behold how the Writers, sometimes wandering from the purity of the Augustan age, claimed a certain freedom for themselves in the manner of Poets; in which way Publius Crassus and the Emperor Hadrian themselves once played, somewhat imitating Catullus on Lesbia's sparrow, or Ovid and Statius on the parrot. The former played in the burial place of his mule, and the latter in that of his horse, which were constructed with magnificent mass, as following Gruter, Porcacchi <note>Girolamo Porcacchi (1530–1585), author of *Funerali antichi*</note>, Ferretti <note>Giovanni Battista Ferretti, author of *Musae Lapidariae*</note>, and others note:

->TO THE GODS OF THE FEET, A ROCK<-
<note>original: "DIIS PEDIBVS SAXVM"—a parody of the standard funerary opening "DIIS MANIBVS" (To the Gods of the Shades)</note>
->FOR CINCIA, THE BACK-CARRIER AND RUMP-BEARER<-
<note>original: "CINCIAE DORSIFERAE ET CLVNIFERAE"—mocking epithets for a pack animal</note>
->SO THAT MOUNTING AND DISMOUNTING<-
->MAY BE MADE CONVENIENT. P. CRASSUS<-
->FOR HIS WELL-CARRYING, STOUT MULE<-
<note>original: "MVLAE SVAE CRASSAE"—a pun on the owner's name, "Crassus," and the adjective "crassa," meaning thick or stout</note>
->PLACED THIS FOOT-BLOCK WITH A LAUGH.<-
<note>original: "SVPPEDANEVM HOC CVM RISV POS."—a "suppedaneum" is a mounting block or footstool; the inscription reveals that the "tombstone" is actually just a block of stone to help him get on his mule</note>
->SHE LIVED.<-

<summary>This page argues that ancient Roman inscriptions were not always solemn, citing historical precedents for witty and playful epitaphs, including a humorous inscription by Publius Crassus for his mule's mounting block. This serves as a defense for the authenticity of the enigmatic Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription by situating it within a tradition of Roman intellectual play.</summary>

# Not Born, Rising Again, etc.
### R
<meta>catchword: Scy-</meta>

->HE LIVED ELEVEN YEARS.<-

->BORYSTHENES THE ALAN,<-
->THE EMPEROR’S HUNTER,<-
->WHO USED TO FLY<-
->THROUGH THE WATERS AND THE MARSHES,<-
->AND OVER THE ETRUSCAN<-
->AND PANNONIAN MOUNDS;<-
->NOR DID ANY WILD BOAR,<-
->WHILE HE PURSUED THEM,<-
->DARE TO DO HIM HARM<-
->WITH ITS FLASHING WHITE TUSK,<-
->OR EVEN SCATTER THE SLIGHTEST FOAM<-
->FROM ITS MOUTH ONTO HIS TAIL,<-
->AS IS WONT TO HAPPEN.<-
->BUT WHOLE IN HIS YOUTH,<-
->WITH HIS LIMBS UNINJURED,<-
->DYING ON HIS APPOINTED DAY,<-
->HE IS LAID IN THIS FIELD.<-
<note>This is a famous funerary poem for Borysthenes, the favorite hunting horse of the Emperor Hadrian. The inscription was originally located in Apt, France (ancient Apta Julia).</note>

Whatever Gruter <term>Jan Gruter (1560–1627): A Belgian-Dutch philologist and collector of Roman inscriptions who was often skeptical of unusual texts</term> may have thought of this second inscription, following his habit of immediately relegating it to the series of apocrypha <term>Apocrypha: Here meaning inscriptions of doubtful authenticity or forgeries</term>, and not—if it be permitted to say—first weighing with what funerals and inscribed sarcophagi the great Macedonian <note>Alexander the Great, who famously built a city, Bucephala, in honor of his horse Bucephalus</note>, Julius Caesar, and Octavianus Augustus were accustomed to honor their horses <term>Sonipedes: A poetic Latin term for horses, literally "sounding-feet" or "noisy-footed"</term> when they died; and furthermore, how familiar it was to the most learned Hadrian himself—no differently than once to Augustus himself—to play at times even in the midst of serious matters. For when the poet Florus, fearing too much for Caesar <note>The Emperor Hadrian</note> because of his remote expeditions, had written:

> I do not wish to be Caesar,
> To walk among the Britons,
<note>original: "Ego nolo Caesar esse, / Ambulare per Britannos". This is the start of a famous humorous exchange between the poet Florus and Emperor Hadrian preserved in the *Augustan History*.</note>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
### R

[...to endure] Scythian frosts;
the same [Emperor Hadrian] responded to him:
> I do not want to be Florus,
> To walk through the taverns,
> To skulk in the cook-shops,
> To endure fat gnats.
<note>original: "Ego nolo Florus esse..." This is part of a famous poetic exchange between the poet Florus and Emperor Hadrian. Florus had written that he wouldn't want to be Caesar, enduring the cold of the frontiers; Hadrian wittily replied by mocking Florus's own lifestyle in Rome.</note>

Nay, not even struck by the horror of impending death did he fail to pour out this charming epitaph—one which suits our purpose, composed with Triple Elegance <note>A rhetorical term for a perfectly balanced three-part structure</note>—as a consolation for his trembling soul:

> LITTLE SOUL, WANDERING AND CHARMING,
> GUEST AND COMPANION OF THE BODY,
> TO WHAT PLACES WILL YOU NOW DEPART,
> PALE, STIFF, AND NAKED,
> NOR WILL YOU GIVE JESTS AS YOU ARE WONT?
<note>original: "ANIMVLA VAGVLA BLANDVLA..." This is one of the most famous poems from antiquity, reportedly composed by Hadrian on his deathbed in 138 AD.</note>

Therefore, wit was not entirely exiled from Roman inscriptions—not only when, following the custom of the people of Agrigento <term>Agrigentum: A city in Sicily known for its grand monuments</term> who lived before them, they raised such magnificent memorials for the carcasses of horses (as recorded by Strabo in Book 15 and Pliny in Book 8, Chapter 42); indeed, they even sometimes held solemn funerals for the tiny bodies of birds, as the same Pliny mentions in Book 10, Chapter 44. But they did so even when honoring their dead kin, as if to show posterity that the most bitter stroke of Death ought to be held in contempt. This can be seen from the following examples, which Gruterus <term>Jan Gruter: The scholar mentioned previously who was often skeptical of unusual inscriptions</term> does not suggest are forgeries. From Licetus <term>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657): An Italian physician and philosopher who wrote on ancient lamps</term>, *On the Hidden Lamps of the Ancients*, Book 6, following Apian, Gruter, and others:

-> GENUCIUS ORATA <-
-> ROMAN FISHERMAN <-
-> CURSED BE THAT FISH <-
-> WHICH PIERCED ME WITH A SPINE <-

<sig>ALAS.</sig> <note>original: "HEV."</note>

<vocab>Florus, Hadrian, Epitaph, Little soul wandering and charming, People of Agrigento, Pliny, Strabo, Gruter, Licetus, Genucius Orata</vocab>

URSUS THE TOGA-CLAD, WHO WAS THE FIRST TO PLAY
GRACEFULLY WITH THE GLASS BALL WITH MY TEAMMATES,
WHILE THE PEOPLE CHEERED WITH THE LOUDEST SHOUTS
IN THE BATHS OF TRAJAN, THE BATHS OF AGRIPPA, AND TITUS,
AND OFTEN IN NERO’S TOO—IF, INDEED, YOU BELIEVE ME.
I AM HE! COME TOGETHER, REJOICING BALL-PLAYERS <term>pilicrepi: specialized athletes in Rome who played various ball games, often involving catching or striking a ball in the public baths</term>,
AND SHOWER YOUR FRIEND’S STATUE WITH FLOWERS, VIOLETS, ROSES,
AND MANY LEAVES, AND WITH FLOWING OINTMENT;
YOU LOVERS, POUR OUT PURE WINE—
DARK FALERNIAN <term>Falernum: one of the most prestigious and expensive wines of ancient Rome</term>, OR SETINE, OR CAECUBAN—
WHILE I AM ALIVE AND WILLING, FROM THE MASTER’S CELLAR.
AND SING TOGETHER WITH ONE VOICE OF THE OLD MAN URSUS:
CHEERFUL, JESTING, A SCHOLARLY BALL-PLAYER,
WHO SURPASSED ALL THOSE WHO CAME BEFORE HIM
IN THE BEAUTY OF OLD AGE AND THE MOST SUBTLE SKILL.
NOW, OLD MEN, LET US SPEAK TRUE WORDS IN VERSE:
I MYSELF WAS DEFEATED—I CONFESS IT—BY THE THREE-TIME CONSUL,
MY TRUE PATRON, NOT ONCE BUT OFTEN,
AS WHOSE COMIC PERFORMER <term>exodiarius: an actor who performed in the "exodium," a humorous after-piece or skit following a serious play</term> I AM GLADLY NAMED.
<note>original: "VRSVS. TOGATVS. VITREA. QVI. PRIMVS. A PILA..." This is a famous Roman funerary inscription for Ursus, a renowned athlete. It captures the festive atmosphere of the Roman baths and the pride of a professional entertainer.</note>

From Labbe’s *Treasury of Epitaphs*, part 9, epitaph 41.

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

AND SEVEN TIMES, IN THE PUBLIC VIEW OF THE EMPERORS SERVIUS GALBA, OTHO SILVIUS, AULUS VITELLIUS, AND THE ROMAN PEOPLE, HE DANCED, SANG, AND BROUGHT PLEASURE. BECAUSE OF THE JESTS WITH WHICH HE DELIGHTED EVERYONE—IF ANY DELIGHT REMAINS AMONG YOU SPIRITS <term>Manes: the souls of deceased loved ones, often worshipped as minor deities in Roman tradition</term>—REFRESH HIS INNOCENT LITTLE SOUL. FAUSTUS, NOW UN-LUCKY, MADE THIS FOR THE BOY AND FOR HIMSELF.
<note>original Latin: "ET SEPTIES SPECTANTIBVS... FAVSTVS NVNC INFAVSTVS PVERO ET SIBI FECIT"</note>

Faustus, now un-lucky. <note>The writer plays on the name "Faustus," which in Latin means "lucky" or "fortunate."</note> Suetonius <term>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus: a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve Roman emperors</term> writes that the Roman People once shouted similar words regarding the corpse of the Emperor Tiberius: *To the Tiber with Tiberius!* <note>original Latin: "Sic Tiberium in Tiberim"</note>
From Occone, *Ancient Inscriptions in Spain*, page 5, Inscription 5, after Apian’s *Moralia*, etc.

TO CLAUDIUS CORNELIUS SUVIANUS, SON OF CLAUDIUS AURELIUS SUVIANUS. I AM JUSTINA, HIS DEVOTED WIFE. THESE THINGS ARE DONE WILLINGLY: AS OFTEN AS I DRINK, SO OFTEN DO I TOAST TO HIM. AS MANY CUPS AS HE TAKES, I TAKE. ONCE WE LIVED IN HARMONY. O SPOUSE, MAY THE EARTH BE LIGHT UPON YOU. <term>S. T. T. L.: abbreviation for "Sit Tibi Terra Levis," a standard Roman funerary prayer wishing for the deceased to rest easy</term>
<note>original Latin: "CL. CORNELIO SVVIANO... O CONIVNX S. T. T. L."</note>

To this inscription—as if it were related, unless you prefer to call it "discordant" in such a great harmony of drinking—the following could be added, even though Gruter <term>Janus Gruter: a Dutch Renaissance philologist and collector of ancient inscriptions</term> himself was reluctant to include it.

# Not born, she rises again, etc.

From the same author, and from Ferretti in *The Museum of Stone Inscriptions*, book 1, entry 48. <term>Musaeum Lapidium: a common title for catalogs of ancient stone inscriptions (epigraphy)</term>

->TO THE SPIRITS OF THE DEPARTED<-

<meta>This page continues the exploration of enigmatic and paradoxical Roman inscriptions. # Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<note>Aelia Laelia Crispis is the name of a famous 16th-century "enigma" or riddle inscribed on a marble slab in Bologna. It describes someone who is "neither man, nor woman, nor hermaphrodite," but all and none at once. It became a sensation among Renaissance scholars.</note>

->A RAM, AND BOYS, ONE BOY, A BRIDE,<-
->A HUSBAND.<-
->BY THE KNIFE, THE WATER, THE ROPE, AND BY GRIEF, THEY FALL.<-
<note>original Latin: "VERVEX. ET. PVERI. PVER. VNVS. SPONSA. MARITVS CVLTELLO. LYMPHA. FVNE. DOLORE. CADVNT"</note>

From the aforementioned Ferretti, book 5, memorial 1, in the notes after others. <note>Francesco Ferretti was a 17th-century author known for his "Musaeum Lapidium," a collection of inscriptions.</note>

->MOTHER, SON, AND DAUGHTER<-
->FATHER-IN-LAW, SON-IN-LAW, AND DAUGHTER-IN-LAW<-
->GRANDMOTHER AND GRANDDAUGHTER, HUSBAND AND WIFE<-
->FATHER AND DAUGHTER, BROTHER AND SISTER<-
->AT ONE AND THE SAME TIME<-
->AND BY THE SAME FATE THEY WERE TAKEN AWAY<-
->IT IS ASKED HOW MANY THEY ARE<-
->THEY ARE THREE<-
<note>original Latin: "MATER. FILIVS... SVNT TRES." This is a classic kinship riddle. The solution usually involves a complex family tree where three people satisfy multiple roles simultaneously—for example, through specific remarriages or biblical scenarios like those of Lot's family.</note>

From Labbe and Ferretti, after all others. <note>Philippe Labbe was a prolific Jesuit scholar of the 17th century.</note>

->FATHER WITH DAUGHTER<-
->BROTHER AND SISTER<-
->FATHER-IN-LAW AND DAUGHTER-IN-LAW<-
->HERE LIE ONLY TWO<-
<note>original Latin: "PATER. CVM. FILIA... HIC. TANTVM. DVO. IACENT"</note>

From Apianus and others, and most recently from Ferretti, who admits that these verses savor of an Enigma and shine with very little light, book 2, memorial 45. <term>Aenigma: a riddle or mystery. Ferretti is acknowledging that these inscriptions are intentionally obscure and difficult to interpret.</term>

->TO THE SPIRITS OF THE DEPARTED<-
<term>D. M.: abbreviation for Dis Manibus, the standard Roman dedication to the spirits of the ancestors.</term>
->TAYTA<-
->TO NERO, FAME, BEARD<-
->WHICH ROME CLAIMS FOR HER FREEDMEN<-
->IT DECORATES THE LOST WITH TITLES; BEHOLD, LOOK UPON ALL<-
->WHOM.<-
<note>original Latin: "TAYTA NERONI FAMA BARBA... CVNCTOS QVOS." This transcription appears to be a fragment or a very obscure epigram regarding the Emperor Nero or a freedman of the Neronian household.</note>

<summary>This page records several kinship riddles and logical paradoxes found in ancient or pseudo-ancient inscriptions. These puzzles use family relationships (mother, son, son-in-law) to challenge the reader to calculate how many people are actually buried in a tomb.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, kinship riddles, Francesco Ferretti, Philippe Labbe, Roman epigraphy, Nero, family trees, logic puzzles</keywords>

# Not born, rising again, etc.
<note>original Latin: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This refers back to the paradoxical nature of the Aelia Laelia Crispis enigma.</note>

->THOSE WHOM SHE KEEPS SAFE, SHE CHERISHES WITH EQUAL LOVE<-
<note>original Latin: "QVOS. HABET. INCOLVMES. FOVET. AMORE. PARI"</note>
->LEST ERROR MIGHT BREAK THE FINAL WAX SEALS<-
<note>original Latin: "SVPREMAS. ERROR. NE. POSSET. RVMPERE. CERAS"</note>
->WITH HER ART SHE TOOK CARE; <gloss>thus</gloss> SHE CLOSED THE HIDDEN THINGS WITH FAITH<-
<note>original Latin: "ARTE. SVA. CAVIT. sic CLAVSIT. OPERTA. FIDE"</note>
->HE WHO LEFT HIS BELONGINGS WITHOUT DISPUTE WILL RECEIVE THE ANCESTRAL SPIRITS<-
<note>original Latin: "EXCIPIET. MANES. SVA. QVI. SINE. LITE. RELIQVIT"</note>
->HE WILL PRAISE THE EFFORT FOR WHICH YOUR CARE PROVIDES<-
<note>original Latin: "LAVDABIT. STVDIVM. CVI. TVA. CVRA. CAVET"</note>

From the same <term>Apianus</term><note>Petrus Apianus (1495–1552) was a German humanist and author of "Inscriptiones sacrosanctae vetustatis," a major collection of ancient inscriptions.</note>, page 489, Inscription 4, which he interprets as being about the Elements, and which is corrupted almost throughout:

->I AM WANDERING CONSTANTLY THROUGHOUT THE CIRCLE OF THE DEITY ONVAVA<-
<note>original Latin: "SVM VAGVS. ASSIDVE TOTO CIRCV NVMINIS ONVAVAE". Onvava is a mysterious, likely Gaulish or local deity whose name often appears in corrupted transcriptions.</note>
->NOR DOES THE DISTANCE OF A DIFFERENT WORLD COMPEL ME<-
->TO SUBMIT THE VOWS OF THINGS UNDER ANOTHER'S TITLE<-
<note>original Latin: "ALTERIVS TITVLO SVBDERE VOTA RFVM". The transcriber notes the text is corrupted; "RFVM" is likely a mistake for "RERVM" (of things).</note>
->A CERTAIN FAITH IN THE TRUTH CARRIED ME INTO THE CITADEL OF TIBUR<-
<note>original Latin: "VERI CETA FIDES TIBVRI VEXIT IN ARCE". Tibur is the ancient name for the Italian town of Tivoli.</note>
->ALSO THE DEITY OF ONVAVA WILL BE THE REASON, O DIVINE PARENT<-
->IN THE AUSONIAN LAND<-
<note>original Latin: "AVSONIA IN TERRA". Ausonia is a poetic name for Italy.</note>

From these examples (with <term>Gruter</term><note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627) was a Flemish-Dutch scholar who compiled one of the most significant corpuses of Roman inscriptions.</note> himself not disagreeing), we may gather that Enigmatic Inscriptions also appeared from time to time in antiquity. Therefore, it would cause little, indeed no trouble, if it were objected against our inscription that the Ancients did not play games or never conducted themselves enigmatically in their inscriptions, since the contrary is clear.

For why? Was it ever provided by some law, or was it ever sworn by mortals, that they must adhere so religiously to serious and grave words that turning aside sometimes to witty and playful things would be judged an execrable crime, to be entirely wiped out by the <term>interdiction of water and fire</term>?<note>In Roman law, the "interdictio aquae et ignis" was a formal decree of exile, stripping a person of citizenship and property.</note> How many of them do we encounter daily, sporting with the urban novelty of figures and tropes? And indeed, are they not almost superior to those which shine with pure seriousness? Many indeed delight in Metaphor.

<summary>The author cites several cryptic and corrupted ancient inscriptions to argue that the Romans frequently used riddles and metaphors. He defends the idea that ancient monuments were not always strictly serious but could be playful and enigmatic.</summary>
<keywords>Petrus Apianus, Jan Gruter, Onvava, Tibur, Ausonia, Roman law, epigraphy, metaphors, enigmatic inscriptions, Aelia Laelia Crispis</keywords>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<note>This header refers to a famous and enigmatic inscription found in Bologna, which served as a famous intellectual puzzle for centuries. The author is using it as a category or starting point for discussing unusual epigraphs.</note>

->THE SONS, BOUND<-
->BY THEIR MOTHER’S MERITS,<-
->DEDICATED THIS.<-
<note>original Latin: "FILII. DEVINCTI MATERNIS. MERITIS DEDICAVERUNT."</note>

From the same work <note>referring to Jan Gruter’s collection of Roman inscriptions</note>, page 97, number 1.

->TO THE DIVINE FEVER.<-
->TO THE HOLY, GREAT FEVER.<-
->CAMILLA AMATA, FOR HER<-
->ILL SON, PLACED THIS.<-
<note>original Latin: "FEBRI. DIVAE. SANCTAE. FEBRI. MAGNAE..." In ancient Rome, diseases like Fever (Febris) were sometimes worshipped as minor deities to appease them and seek healing.</note>

Other inscriptions play with *Commutatio* <term>Word Reversal: a rhetorical figure where words are repeated in reverse order, also known as chiasmus</term>, using the same figure of speech once applied to Caesar: *the woman of all men, and the man of all women*, as found in Suetonius <note>The Roman historian Suetonius recorded this jab at Julius Caesar’s reputation</note>; from the same source, page 706, number 5:

->SEIA MARCELLINA, DAUGHTER OF TITUS, FOR HERSELF AND<-
->FOR HER SON VIBENNIUS MARCELLINUS,<-
->SET THIS UP WHILE ALIVE.<-
->WHAT SHE WANTED, SHE COULD DO; AND WHAT SHE COULD DO, SHE WANTED.<-
<note>original Latin: "QUOD. VOLUIT. ET. POTUIT QUOD. POTUIT. ET. VOLVIT." A playful way of saying she had both the will and the means to build the monument.</note>

Others delight in *Antithesis*, or opposition, in this manner: From the same Gruter, page 994, number 3.

->TO SEPTUMIA SEVERA, FREEDWOMAN OF LUCIUS,<-
->LUCIUS PUBLICIUS ABSCANTUS [DEDICATED THIS].<-
->FOR HER MERITS, A SMALL GIFT;<-
->FOR MY MEANS, ENOUGH.<-
<note>original Latin: "PRO. MERITIS. PARVUM / PRO. FACULTATE. SATIS." This is a humble formula often used to suggest the deceased deserved more than the survivor could afford.</note>

Thus, it was said elsewhere of Galba and Cotta: *One has nothing; for the other, nothing is enough.* So that I may pass over countless other examples, among which one might observe *Prosopopeja* <term>Personification: a device where the tombstone or the deceased speaks to the passerby</term>, wailing or Exclamation, Greeting, Command, Prayer, Curse, Scolding, and similar very common forms, which...

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic qualities of inscriptions. Having previously explored wordplay and rhetorical devices like chiasmus, the author now shifts focus to the "Notae Romanorum"—the cryptic abbreviations and shorthand used in ancient Roman epigraphy, moving from "playful" inscriptions to those that appear "obscure" to the modern eye.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>Rising Again, Unborn, etc.</header>
<meta>catchword: vione</meta>

...qualities, which bestow all the beauty, grace, and wit upon **Inscribed Marbles**.

If one wishes to turn from playful matters to the obscure, who would not find the various ancient **Roman Abbreviations** <term>Latin: *Notae*; a system of shorthand where single letters represented whole words</term> more mysterious than the enigmas themselves? In these, sometimes a single letter denotes an entire word; indeed, when several are grouped together, they signify a complete thought or a full speech. Such were certainly those seen carved by order of the Sibyl <note>The Sibyls were legendary prophetesses in ancient Greece and Rome; here, the author refers to a prophetic inscription</note> on an arch of the **Janiculum** <note>One of the hills of Rome</note>, which read: **P. P. P. E. V. V. V. V. V. V. V. F. F. F.** These were interpreted as: *The Father of the Fatherland has departed; he has come, a powerful conqueror; he conquered the strength of your city by sword, by hunger, by fire, and by cold.* <note>original Latin: "Pater Patriae Profectus Est, Venit, Victor Validus, Vicit Vires Urbis Vestrae Ferro Fame Flamma Frigore."</note>

Similar examples are noted by Mazochius <note>Giacomo Mazzocchi, a 16th-century Italian publisher and antiquarian</note> and Apianus <note>Petrus Apianus, a German humanist who published a famous collection of inscriptions in 1534</note> on page 260, and others: **P. I. C. P. P. R. V.** which means: *In peace he closed the doors; peace for the Roman people everywhere.* <note>original Latin: "Pace Ianuas Clausit, Pace Populo Romano Ubique." This refers to the closing of the gates of the Temple of Janus, symbolizing a time of total peace.</note> Finally, there are those used by the Romans in **Civil Law**, in public statutes, in the decrees of the people, in legal actions, and in perpetual edicts, as the same Mazochius and others observe and teach. I shall pass over those that are perhaps too common, being known to anyone who has even slightly touched upon the study of inscribed stones—such as **V. F.** (*While living, he made this*), **D. S. P.** (*From his own money*), **S. T. T. L.** (*May the earth be light upon you*), or **S. A. D.** (*Dedicated under the adze* <note>The phrase "sub ascia dedicavit" is a common but mysterious funerary formula found in Roman Gaul, likely signifying the tomb was dedicated while still being worked on or as a protection against desecration</note>). Also, **E. T. H. F.** (*The heir made this according to the will*) and **L. D. D. D.** (*A place given by the gift of the Decurions*). Innumerable and nearly infinite examples of this kind can be found, both in their recording and their explanation, in the recent and most absolute commentary on **Roman Abbreviations** by the author **Sertorius Ursatus** <note>Sertorio Orsato (1617–1678), an Italian antiquarian whose "De notis Romanorum" was the definitive guide to these abbreviations</note>.

However, I personally believe that most of these ancient notes of the **Romans** <term>Latin: *Quiritum*; a formal term for Roman citizens in their civil capacity</term> were introduced for the sake of brevity and common habit, rather than for the purpose of hiding the meaning. Therefore, if their sentiments seem so remote and obscure to us today, it does not arise from their original nature, but from the vast distance of ages and the forgetting of ancient customs and affairs.

<summary>The author discusses the use of abbreviations (notae) in Roman inscriptions, explaining that while they seem like cryptic puzzles today, they were actually a standard shorthand for legal, civic, and funerary declarations. He cites several famous scholars and provides examples of how long strings of single letters were used to record complex historical and religious messages.</summary>

<keywords>Roman abbreviations, epigraphy, Janiculum, Giacomo Mazzocchi, Sertorio Orsato, funerary formulas, Latin shorthand, civil law, Petrus Apianus</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Roman shorthand and abbreviations. The previous page ended mid-word with the prefix "obli-", which here completes as "oblivione" (forgetfulness), as the author explains how the meaning of inscriptions is lost over time.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>
<meta>catchword: IOAN-</meta>

I would rather think this occurs because of forgetfulness. Indeed, there was a time when the five letters **L. L. L. M. M.**—signifying *Mordant Maevius Lacerated the arm of Largius* <note>original Latin: "Laceravit Lacertum Largii Mordax Maevius"</note>—were so well known that no house in Tivoli was seen without them; yet today they would be utterly unknown if the Father of Roman Eloquence <note>A reference to Cicero</note> had not committed the event to posterity in the second book of *On the Orator*.

The same thing happens very often with Inscriptions: either through ignorance or the forgetting of customs and events—which are described in them too briefly or sometimes even mystically—the inscriptions are rendered obscure and enigmatic to us, even though they were not truly so at the beginning. Thus, a riddle elegantly composed by Paschasius <note>likely Paschasius Cordatus, a 16th-century poet</note> according to the ancient style would have become entirely mysterious:

> *The earth which nature ordered to be a place of rest*
> ->*for other mortals, for me alone is a punishment.*<-
> *Stop, and behold this thing, both wonderful and pitiable; a virgin,*
> ->*yet not a virgin, I am covered by a tomb, yet not entombed;*<-

if he had not ordered the solution to precede these opening verses:

> *I, the Vestal Virgin Oppia, violated the camp of modesty,*
> ->*alas, I am pressed alive beneath the dreadful marble.*<-
> <note>Vestal Virgins who broke their vows of chastity were famously punished by being buried alive.</note>

So it is, as Quintilian notes in his eighth book: after the well-known habits of that "Farthing Clytemnestra" <note>A derogatory nickname for Clodia, a Roman socialite, comparing her to the murderous Queen Clytemnestra but suggesting she was cheap, costing only a 'quadrans' or farthing</note> had faded—she who had nothing of the prostitute about her but her words, for she refused no one at the banquet but refused everyone in the bedroom—the joke of Caelius became an enigma. He wittily called her by the names of two cities, Cos and Nola: "a woman of Cos" in the Dining Room and "a woman of Nola" in the Bedroom. <note>This is a double pun. "Coam" (from Cos) refers to the transparent silk worn by prostitutes, while "Nolam" puns on the city of Nola and the Latin word "nolle," meaning "to be unwilling" or "to refuse."</note> Hence, quite rightly and skillfully, the one who composed a thoroughly enigmatic inscription—comparable to our own—and placed it on the statues of the Holy Apostles in Rome, ensured that the memory of its meaning would never perish by such a mishap. While he enclosed the event in opposites, he simultaneously revealed it in this manner:

<vocab>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Inscriptions, Paschasius, Quintilian, Clytemnestra, Cos, Nola, Rome</vocab>

### TO GIOVANNA CAPUTA OF PALERMO
### FOR HER LINEAGE, PIETY, AND PATIENCE
->REMARKABLE<-
->TO WHOM A MARRIAGE SOUGHT OUTSIDE HER HOMELAND<-
->UNFORTUNATELY BROUGHT<-
->EXILE IN THE MIDST OF LIBERTY<-
->CELIBACY WITHIN MARRIAGE<-
->SERVITUDE WHILE IN COMMAND<-
->POVERTY AMIDST RICHES, AND DEATH IN THE MIDDLE<-
->OF HER LIFE’S COURSE.<-
->IN THIS ONE THING SHE WAS HAPPY:<-
->THAT ROME, WHICH SHE WAS NOT PERMITTED TO SEE WHILE LIVING,<-
->BESTOWED A TOMB UPON HER IN DEATH.<-
->SHE LIVED 44 YEARS, 2 MONTHS, AND 8 DAYS.<-
->SHE DIED AT TIVOLI.<-

---

Thus, when in former times the slaughter of a domestic ram was followed by the mournful death of three children, to which was then added a mother who took her own life with a noose; and finally, when the master of the house himself—at once a father and a husband—having witnessed this funereal and tragic display, died of grief (being unable to bear the weight of such sorrow), that clever verse we just mentioned was not at all enigmatic:

> *A ram, the boys, one boy, a wife, a husband,*
> *Fall by the knife, the water, the rope, and grief.*
> <note>This "riddle" is actually a summary of a specific family tragedy: the ram was butchered (knife), the children drowned (water), the mother hung herself (rope), and the father died of a broken heart.</note>

However, it took on the nature of a true Enigma after the passage of time erased the memory of the event. Similarly, as long as that famous couple was celebrated among the people of Tournai <note>original Latin: *Tornacenses*</note>—those who gave their name to Religion, where the wife joined the Order of Saint Clare <note>The Poor Clares</note> and the husband joined the Franciscans <note>The Order of Friars Minor</note>, and each presided over their own monastery—it was not an enigma, but a humorous saying:

->*Here lies the father, here lies the mother*<-
->*Here lies*<-
<note>original French: "Cy gist le pere, cy gist le mere". This refers to the fact that while they were husband and wife, they were also the spiritual "Father" and "Mother" (Superior and Abbess) of their respective religious houses.</note>

<summary>The author explains how historical inscriptions and riddles, such as the epitaph of Giovanna Caputa or the tragic "Ram Riddle," only become mysterious "enigmas" once the specific historical events they describe have been forgotten by later generations.</summary>
<keywords>epitaphs, Giovanna Caputa, Palermo, riddles, Tournai, Poor Clares, Franciscans, Tivoli, Roman history</keywords>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

> *Here lies the sister, here lies the brother*
> *Here lies the wife, and the mother*
> *And there are only two bodies here.*
> <note>original French: "Cy gist la soeur, cy gist le frere / Cy gist la femme, et le mere / Et ny a que deux corps icy." This classic riddle describes a situation—such as a mother marrying her son—where two people hold multiple family titles simultaneously.</note>

Until it became known among us Bolognese that a certain man named Proculus was cast headlong from the Bell Tower of Saint Proculus <note>The Church of San Procolo in Bologna</note> and was buried behind that same tower, the following verse would not have been counted among the Enigmatic ones:

->IF THE BELL OF PROCULUS HAD BEEN FAR FROM PROCULUS,<-
->NOW PROCULUS WOULD BE FAR FROM PROCULUS HIMSELF.<-
<note>This is a play on words in the original Latin: "SI PROCVLA PROCVLO PROCVLI CAMPANA FVISSET / NVNC PROCVLA A PROCVLO PROCVLVS IPSE FORET." It puns on the name "Proculus" and the Latin word "procula," meaning "far away." The sense is: "If the bell had been further away, the man would still be alive (distant from his own grave)."</note>

The same must be concluded regarding that Neapolitan Epitaph:

->STORAX, WHO WHILE LIVING ENTERED THE TOMB,<-
->MARVELOUS TO SAY, WHEN DEAD, LACKED A TOMB.<-
<note>original Latin: "STORAX QVI VIVVS SVBIIT SEPVLCRVM / MIRVM DEFVNCTVS CARVIT SEPVLCRO." This likely refers to a man who lived as a hermit in a cave or tomb-like cell, but whose body was never recovered or was moved after death.</note>

The same for that one shouted even to the point of nausea:

->BECAUSE OF "IT IS, IT IS,"<-
->MY MASTER IS DEAD HERE.<-
<note>original Latin: "PROPTER EST EST / DOMINVS MEVS HIC MORTVVS EST." This refers to the famous legend of a German bishop's scout who would write "Est!" (It is!) on the doors of inns with good wine. At Montefiascone, he found the wine so good he wrote "Est! Est!! Est!!!" The bishop drank so much of it that he died there.</note>

Finally, whenever a distance of place or time is introduced (as I was warning), the subject of an inscription is shrouded in darkness—though elsewhere or in former times it stood out clearer than the midday light. We know this to have happened with these few examples:

<margin>Following Mazochi.</margin>
From Apianus, page 241, item 3.

->I GROW, YOU RATTLE; I REJOICE,<-
->YOU WAIL; ENVIOUS ONE,<-
->DIE!<-
<note>original Latin: "CRESCO PERCREPAS GAVDEO PERPLORAS INVIDE MORERE." This is a defiant address from the deceased to those who were jealous of their success in life.</note>

From the same, page 99, Inscription 5.

<header>Aelia Laelia Crispis.</header>

...granted by name; it is observed that **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius of Halicarnassus, a Greek historian of Rome.</note> on the other hand, **Livy**, and the other ancient founders of Roman history, never—or at least very rarely—recorded women as being adorned with a <term>praenomen</term> <note>A personal first name, like "Marcus" or "Gaius," which was standard for Roman men.</note>. But if the question is brought to more recent authors, who are nonetheless powerful in ancient learning, they are found struggling against each other in a no less doubtful battle, despite whatever the illustrious **Spanheim** recently inserted for the sake of their agreement by distinguishing between time periods, as did **Ursatus** in the *Monuments of Padua*, after **Panvinio**, the first author of this same distinction in his work *On Ancient Names*. For when **Alexander ab Alexandro** had left written in his *Genial Days*: <note>original: "Romanas Mulieres sine cognomento, solis praenominibus, adiecto nomine denominari"</note> "Roman women were named without a surname, using only personal first names with the family name added"; **Robortello**, on the contrary, in his work *On Roman Names* (unless he felt otherwise in his revised books) wrote that "women were called by the name of their clan, and by one name only, and therefore lacked a personal first name." And when **Sigonio** rose up against Robortello, as if to fight on behalf of Alexander, proving by the testimony of both authors and stones <note>Refers to "lapidum," or stone inscriptions found on ancient ruins.</note> that in ancient times women were called not only by a name but with a first name added to the family name, the legal scholar **Giuseppe Castalio** rushed against Sigonio himself, denying entirely that women ever took a personal first name, but only a surname <note>cognomen</note> added to the family name. In support of this, he submitted so many inscribed marbles that he seemed to wish, with the Poet,

> --- to pile Pelion upon Ossa. <note>A classical metaphor for a monumental or redundant effort, referring to the giants of myth who tried to climb to heaven by stacking mountains.</note>

**Vossius** alone seems to settle the dispute in the first book of his *Analogy*, where, favoring both opinions while rejecting both, he offers his own judgment in these words: <note>original: "Quòd quemadmodum in eo falluntur multi, quòd foeminas praenomine caruisse existimant, ita in illo quoque lapsi sunt, quòd eas cognomen habuisse non arbitrentur."</note> "Just as many are mistaken in thinking that women lacked a personal first name, so too have they slipped up in believing that they did not have a surname." **Servius** agrees in his *Miscellany*, weighing the matter more fully and explaining it more clearly through three distinct...

# ->She Who Was Not Born, Rising Again, etc.<-

...proving distinct sections: first, that women were provided with a <term>praenomen</term> <note>A personal first name, usually reserved for men in standard Roman practice.</note> and a <term>nomen</term> <note>The clan or family name.</note>; second, with a clan name and a <term>cognomen</term> <note>An additional surname or nickname.</note>; and finally, with a personal first name, a clan name, and a surname. 

Regarding the first case—a personal first name added to the family name—we here add examples of women taken from the collectors of inscriptions, following those cited by **Servius** <note>A 4th-century grammarian.</note> from ancient stones. It is therefore clear from what has been cited so far that, in ancient times, women were frequently called by three names. Consequently, Agatho <note>The presumed author/dedicator of the mysterious Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription.</note> was unjustly criticized for honoring his Laelia with three names in a similar fashion. This remains true whether the situation was as we believe—that she truly had three names—or whether, for the sake of maintaining a "Triple Harmony" <note>An aesthetic or philosophical preference for things in groups of three.</note> throughout the text, he called her "Aelia Laelia" (using the rhetorical figure of pleonasm <note>A pleonasm is the use of more words than are necessary to convey meaning, often used for poetic or rhetorical emphasis; original: "figurâ Pleonasmo".</note>) instead of just "Aelia," as has been mentioned elsewhere.

Should it be objected once more that the examples cited thus far do not entirely fit the case—on the grounds that while they prove women had three names, the first was always either a proper first name <term>praenomen|The first of the three names in a Roman citizen's name, like Gaius or Marcus. It was usually abbreviated.</term> or appeared in that form, indicated only by a single capital letter, whereas the name "Aelia" is written out in full and takes the form of a family name <term>nomen|The second name in the Roman tria nomina, indicating the clan or "gens."</term> rather than a first name—I could provide countless <note>original: "sexcenta" - literally "six hundred," but used idiomatically in Latin to mean an indefinitely large number.</note> examples to dismantle this weak objection. These would show instances where the first of the three names is written not with truncated or mutilated letters, but in its entirety. For this purpose, let the following inscriptions suffice:

->**AND**<-

### Not born, but rising again, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens." This title likely refers to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma, which describes the subject as "not born" (non nata). The author uses these inscriptions to breathe new life into the historical debate over the riddle.</note>

->IN THE CONSULSHIP OF JUVENTIUS SECUNDUS.<-
->TO PAULA:<-
->VIBIA SALVIA VARIA, DAUGHTER OF LUCIUS,<-
->ALONG WITH THE NUMMII,<-
->ALBINUS AND VARIA,<-
->AND LUCIUS ROSCIUS AELIANUS PACULUS<-

...and whenever it might be shaken by a new objection. If, I say, it were objected once again that Aelia and Laelia are two different family names <note>original: "Gentium nomina." In ancient Rome, the *nomen gentile* indicated the clan or family one belonged to, such as the Julia or Cornelia families.</note>; and that it is entirely inconsistent with any correct system of names distributed on sarcophagi for a single woman to be called by two family names; for the stones already mentioned could immediately plead the contrary case in silence, by briefly running through them once more. For they promptly show us **AELIA FLAVIA MELITANA**, who enjoys two family names, namely those of the Aelii and the Flavii. Also the already praised **AURELIA STATILIA CLAVDIANA**, who was honored with the naming of the Statilia and Aurelia families together. They show **VIBIA SALVIA VARIA**, of the Vibia and Salvia families; and **VIBIA AURELIA SABINA**, distinguished by the names of the Vibia and Aurelia families. They show **MARIA CAECILIA PROCILLA**, boasting not just two, but three family names: Maria, Caecilia, and Procilla; to say nothing of the countless others that a wise reader could extract for himself from the collectors of ancient inscriptions.

This would be no less fully evident from the works recently to be published, which my illustrious master Fabretti <note>Raffaele Fabretti (1618–1700) was a renowned Italian antiquarian and the supervisor of catacombs in Rome; he was famous for his meticulous recording of ancient inscriptions.</note> has laboriously gathered to the number of two thousand, and skillfully supplemented and corrected, if only those I have selected with his kind permission were to be perused. And these are they:

Nor, if I were to assert that this occurred very frequently not only among women but equally among **men**, would Panvinus allow me to lie in his *On Ancient Names*, page 35, before the middle, <margin>*observing that after the time of Augustus, many people made use of two family names <term>gentilitia: the name of the Roman clan or gens</term>, as seen both in Authors and in ancient inscriptions, such as Appius Annius Trebonius Gallus, Marcus Atilius Metilius Bradua, and Publius Coelius Balbinus Vibullius Pius;*</margin> this author certainly offers two reasons for the doubling of the family name: namely, adoption, as well as the granting of citizenship; and, according to his custom, he most learnedly illustrates this with reasons and examples. 

But since I have recognized that these same men, though augmented by a second family name, never abandoned their ancient or personal first name, I have decided to take others once more from the marble inscriptions who, having cast off whatever first name they had, took a family name in its place—as in the case of the women mentioned above—following the example of Aelia Laelia. They will be, for example, from the Aelian and Julian families: Aelius Julius; from the Sergian and Afinian: Sergius Afinius; from the Aemilian and Valerian: Aemilius Valerius; from the Claudian and that same Julian: Claudius Julius; from the Flavian and Furian: Flavius Furius, as is clearly evident from the following:

## Chapter 10.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral motifs and vines within a square border.</image-desc>

The structure and symmetry of the human body are commended by the study of nature through a certain harmonious conforming of all parts to the dimensions of the head, and a balanced harmony of the limbs. But—in matters concerning pure and corrected speech—not all Cities and Regions in antiquity responded in this way to Rome, their own head, and indeed the head of the whole world. At that time, <term>Cicero<gloss>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and orator considered the gold standard for Latin prose</gloss></term> was not heard in every place to drive out barbarism even from the furthest borders. Indeed, that golden river of eloquence, troubled by many windings and impure rivulets gushing forth from here and there, and flowing far from the clear purity of its source, has been brought down to us (as the <term>Panegyrist<gloss>A writer of formal public speeches of praise; likely a reference to the 'Panegyrici Latini' collection</gloss></term> says) by a late derivation.

Indeed, care had been taken, as <term>Suetonius<gloss>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman historian who wrote 'The Twelve Caesars'</gloss></term> reports in his life of Augustus, that inscriptions intended for publication in the Colonies should first be sent to the most learned men of the City <note>meaning Rome</note>, lest anything creep into them that might offend the ears of that Emperor, whom they knew to be a severe judge of words. However, this rule applied to public decrees, not private ones; and it prevailed in that so-called Golden Age, not the Silver or Bronze ages. Since, therefore, from whichever of the ancient ages the In-

<summary>The author argues that minor linguistic irregularities do not diminish the value of an inscription, noting that Latin style varied by region and era, even during the Golden Age of Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Latin epigraphy, Cicero, Augustus, Suetonius, Golden Age of Latin, linguistic purity, Roman colonies</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the linguistic anomalies found in the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription do not disprove its antiquity. The author shifts from a general discussion of Roman eloquence to a specific defense of the inscription's "vulgar" or non-standard Latin, comparing it to other private and provincial records.</meta>

170
### Aelia Laelia Crispis.
<meta>catchword: [r mum,</meta>

Even if we are unable to define our inscription (though we conjectured above that it was not unknown to <term>Athenaeus<gloss>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian, circa 170–230 AD</gloss></term>), and we all recognize that it was composed outside of the region of Latium, on private land, and by private families, it is equally reasonable to believe it exempt from the laws and decrees that constrained those official inscriptions. Those were the ones mandated to be erected on Latin soil—or, if you prefer, on the imperial throne—for Emperors, Consuls, Censors, Tribunes, and others who deserved well of the Republic.

Therefore, whenever its structure, so celebrated and elegant, might be accused of some slight fault regarding pure <term>Latinity<gloss>The quality of using correct, classical Latin style and grammar</gloss></term>, it would protect itself by the example of a thousand private inscriptions. In these, one frequently reads "OPSECRO" for *obsecro* <note>I beseech</note>, "CONIVNX" for *coniux* <note>spouse</note>, "MI" for *mihi* <note>to me</note>, "OSSVA" for *ossa* <note>bones</note>, "OXORI" for *uxori* <note>to the wife</note>, "SECOS" for *secus* <note>otherwise</note>, "VIBVS" for *vivus* <note>living</note>, "AVONCLO" for *avunculo* <note>to the uncle</note>, "POPOLVS" for *populus* <note>the people</note>, and countless other common examples. These are noted by the illustrious <term>Spanheim<gloss>Ezekiel Spanheim (1629–1710), a diplomat and expert on ancient coins and inscriptions</gloss></term> in his second Dissertation, by <term>Boldoni<gloss>Ottavio Boldoni, a 17th-century scholar of epigraphy (the study of inscriptions)</gloss></term> on pages 76 and 77, and by others—to review all of which, a lifetime would not suffice.

Therefore, let one example suffice for all, dedicated to <term>Lucius and Gaius<gloss>The grandsons and adopted heirs of Augustus Caesar</gloss></term>, the children of Augustus himself, during the age of purer eloquence. Because it was crafted outside of Roman territory—specifically in <term>Pisa<gloss>A city in Tuscany, Italy</gloss></term>—as the same Boldoni observes, it displays many words clearly reeking of such thick barbarism that even the most robust scholarship would labor in vain to redeem them from this author’s objections. Moreover, these are mere blemishes, not deep stains; we would say our Inscription is lightly sprinkled with them, rather than shamefully defiled. This is true whenever we do not attribute the use of <term>the letter Æ<note>original Greek: τὸ Æ</note></term> for AE, or <term>the letter OE<note>original Greek: τὸ OE</note></term> for AE, to the carelessness of <term>Agathon<gloss>Likely a reference to a known stone-cutter or a previous transcriber of the text</gloss></term>, or to the decline of a later age, or to the ignorance of a former stone-cutter, or to the negligence of a recent transcriber. I mean, for instance, writing <note>original: ÆLIA LÆLIA</note> "Ælia Lælia" instead of "Aelia Laelia," or <note>original: COELO MOERENS</note> "Grieving in Heaven" instead of "Caelo Maerens."

Indeed, marked with a similar diphthong of joined letters, we can bring forth from our <term>Cabinet of Curiosities<gloss>original: Cimeliarchio; a private museum or collection of rare antiquities</gloss></term> these consular coins, which we place before the eyes of the readers as follows...

<summary>The author defends the linguistic irregularities of the Aelia Laelia Crispis inscription by noting that private and provincial Roman inscriptions often deviated from the strict "Golden Age" standards of Rome. He cites other historical examples of "barbaric" spelling found in respected locations like Pisa to argue that such errors are mere blemishes rather than proof of a forgery.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Latinity, Spanheim, Boldoni, Pisa, Lucius and Gaius Caesar, epigraphy, linguistics, numismatics, orthography</keywords>

SI.

->*of the lower level of the royal Barberini Palace at the Four Fountains*<-

This **Imperial Inscription** features letters a foot high, drilled through in various places; this indicates that their cavities were once filled with gilded bronze, as is observed on the arches of Titus, Constantine, and other Emperors. This is true unless some flaw or defect lies hidden in the middle of the placement, which I greatly fear. This assessment is supported by the most learned Bellorio <note>Giovanni Pietro Bellori (1613–1696) was a celebrated antiquarian and biographer, serving as the curator of antiquities for both the Pope and Queen Christina of Sweden.</note>, Antiquarian to the Popes and the Queen, whom I wished to mention here for the sake of friendship, honor, and commendation:

### TO TIBERIUS CLAVDIVS CAESAR
### AVGVSTVS
### PONTIFEX MAXIMVS, IN HIS NINTH YEAR OF TRIBUNICIAN POWER,
### FIVE TIMES CONSUL, SIXTEEN TIMES ACCLAIMED EMPEROR, FATHER OF HIS COUNTRY.
### THE SENATE AND THE PEOPLE OF ROME, BECAUSE
### HE SUBDUED THE KINGS OF BRITAIN WITHOUT
### ANY LOSS,
### AND WAS THE FIRST TO BRING THE BARBARIAN
### PEOPLES UNDER SWAY. <gloss>thus</gloss>
<note>The original inscription reads *INDICIO* (by evidence/disclosure), though scholars note that on the actual Arch of Claudius, this word is usually reconstructed as *OCEANVM* (the Ocean). The author uses <gloss>sic</gloss> to acknowledge this unusual reading.</note>

->*on a very beautiful little altar.*<-

->TO THE SPIRITS OF THE DEAD<-
<note>original: *DIS M* for *Dis Manibus*, a standard Roman funerary dedication.</note>
->SEXTVS POMPEIVS<-

<term>Imperial Inscription</term> (Inscriptio Imperialis): A formal dedication to a Roman Emperor, often characterized by monumental scale and high-quality craftsmanship.
<term>Tribunician Power</term> (TR. P.): A key legal power of the Roman Emperors, used here as a way to date the inscription to approximately 49–50 AD.

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion on Latin orthography—specifically the interchangeable use of the "ae" and "oe" diphthongs—as it relates to the spelling of names and words found in ancient inscriptions, leading back to the central mystery of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

It is very well established that the Greek *oi* is transferred into the Latin *oe*, not *ae*; therefore, what prevents them from being able to write *Coelum* [heaven] just as well as *Caelum*? <note>The author is arguing about the fluid nature of Latin spelling in antiquity, specifically how the "ae" and "oe" sounds were often swapped.</note> That it can be written both ways, just like the name *Maecenas*, is taught by Moretus in his commentary on Horace, page 149. I shall set aside those points which Meibomius cleverly gathered regarding this same diphthong (specifically concerning its placement in the second syllable) in his *Life of Maecenas*, pages 24 and 25.

Thus, one can see *Moerens* [mourning] written in both ways among the collectors of inscriptions. This was also observed on the Pisan Stone—so learnedly explained according to custom—by the most distinguished and never-sufficiently-celebrated Father Noris, in these words: <note>Father Enrico Noris (1631–1704) was a famous church historian and custodian of the Vatican Library. The "Pisan Stone" refers to the *Cenotaphia Pisana*, Roman funerary inscriptions found in Pisa.</note> *“Writers vary the diphthong, some using OE and others AE in that word; indeed, ancient inscriptions support both. While AE is read on page 717, last line, and 799, line 8, on page 607, line 4, this epitaph is placed:”*

> Spare, O spouse, from shaking your youth with weeping,
> And from troubling my fate with your **MOURNING**.
<note>original: *MAERENDO*. The verse encourages a surviving spouse to cease their grief.</note>

And on page 939, line 1, in another:

> Not yet secure while she weeps with a **MOST SORROWFUL** mind
> She died, and deceived the **SAD ONES** by her nursing.
<note>original: *MAESTISSIMA* and *MAESTA*. These are variations of the word for "sad" or "sorrowful."</note>

In other epitaphs, however, the diphthong *OE* is seen, such as on page 669, line 10; 673, line 12; 712, line 4; 719, line 9; 832, line 8; 851, line 12; and page 1003, line 4:

> **SORROWFUL** sister, **sorrowful** brothers, and **sorrowful** parents.
<note>original: *MOEsta soror, mOESti fratres, mOEStique parentes*.</note>

And on page 556, line 1, we read:

->AELIA &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; AELIANA<-
->**MOURNING** PARENT &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<-
<gloss>so written: MOERens</gloss>
->TO THE **DESERVING** DAUGHTER<-
<gloss>so written: MERenti</gloss>
->P. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; C<-
<note>original: *P. C.* usually stands for *Poni Curavit* (Took care to place this).</note>

Certainly, that such a usage existed in Bologna is indicated by this new inscription from the illustrious Reinesius, which is the second in his supplement, and which I do not doubt was faithfully cited by such a diligent man: <note>Thomas Reinesius (1587–1667) was a German physician and scholar known for his massive collections of ancient inscriptions.</note>

It cites various classical authorities and funerary epitaphs to prove that spellings of names like "Aelia" and words like "mourning" were not fixed in antiquity.</summary>
