<meta>This page continues the philological defense of the Latin phrasing used in the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle. The previous page discussed the interchangeable use of diphthongs (AE/OE); this page moves to the grammatical omission of prepositions in ancient inscriptions and classical literature to justify the riddle's phrasing.</meta>

# Not born, Rising again, etc.
<note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This likely refers to the paradoxical nature of the riddle's subject.</note>

->*Found in a field at Bologna.*<-

->TO ALL THE CELESTIAL GODS<-
->TO PUBLIUS GAISIDIUS SABINIANUS, SON OF QUINTUS,<-
->PIPE-PLAYER, SINGER, SCULPTOR<-
->OF IMAGES, ARITHMETICIAN. HE LIVED 43 YEARS,<-
->10 MONTHS, 3 DAYS. IN FRONT 30 FEET, AT THE BACK<-
->40 FEET. WHOSE TALENT NO ONE [CAN DESCRIBE]<-
<note>This is a funerary inscription for a multi-talented man. The measurements at the end refer to the dimensions of the burial plot.</note>

->GAIUS TIPHERNIUS, SON OF GAIUS, PRIEST<-

But just as we say that Agatho was by no means in error regarding these three diphthongs, so too we believe that nothing was amiss in the preposition ***IN***, which was omitted three times when he asserted that his Aelia Laelia Crispis lies ***NEITHER IN HEAVEN, NOR IN THE WATERS, NOR IN THE LANDS***. <note>original: "NEC COELO NEC AQVIS NEC TERRIS". The author is defending the grammar of the riddle against critics who might expect "In Coelo," etc.</note> For besides other examples to be gathered or brought forward from Gruterus in defense of this omission, the same is supported and protected—joined to our "Triple Harmony"—by a certain concise elegance, through which that preposition is often cast aside by <term>ellipsis (original: ἔκλειψιν)</term> <note>An ellipsis is the intentional omission of a word that is still understood by the reader.</note>. Nevertheless, it is imagined that the ablative case is governed by that same suppressed, yet implied, preposition; as when something is said to happen, for example, "at Sulmo" or "at Athens."

Thus that prodigy of intellect, Vossius, <note>Gerardus Johannes Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch classical scholar and theologian.</note> in *On the Construction of Speech*, Book 7, *On the Art of Grammar*, Chapter 44, adds: *this also takes place in those words which seem to many to be adverbs, when in truth they are ablatives: such as, "He comes in the morning" (Mane venit); instead of "from the morning" (de mane). Likewise, to come "at light," "in the evening," "in time" (luci, vesperi, tempori); which are ancient ablatives, and in full would be: in the light, in the evening, in the time.* So too Cicero in the second *Philippic*: *And now he wanders through all Asia.* <note>original: "Et nunc tota Asia vagatur." Note the missing "in" before "tota Asia".</note> Suetonius in his *Life of Domitian*: *He was born in the sixth region of the City.* Horace in Book 1, Satire 9:
> *I was walking by chance along the Sacred Way, as is my custom.*
<note>original: "Ibam fortè via sacra."</note>
And finally, in our specific case regarding "lying" [jacendi], Virgil in the *Aeneid*, Book 12:

<summary>The author uses classical Latin authorities like Cicero, Horace, and Suetonius to defend the grammar of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, specifically the omission of the preposition "in" before "heaven," "water," and "earth."</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Latin grammar, ellipsis, inscriptions, Vossius, philology, Bologna, funerary epitaphs</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the grammatical and philosophical analysis of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle, specifically defending the phrasing "lies neither in heaven, nor in the waters, nor in the lands." While the previous page focused on the omission of the preposition "in," this page examines the suitability of the verb "lies" (iacet) for all three locations.</meta>

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

> *An ancient stone, huge, which by chance lay in the field,*
> *placed as a boundary for the land.*
<note>original Latin: "Saxum antiquum, ingens, campo qui fortè iacebat / Limes agro positus." This is a quote from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 12.</note>

And elsewhere:

> *Alas, you lie in an unknown land, given as prey*
> *to Latin dogs and birds.*
<note>original Latin: "Ehu terra ignota canibus data præda latinis, / Alitibusque jaces." A common poetic sentiment regarding the lack of a proper burial.</note>

Therefore, it is not without consideration that the uncomposed and unformed little body of Laelia is said here to be able to "lie" by land or sea; for that word ***LIES*** <note>original: "IACET"</note> must be referred only to these two, as they are lower places and more familiar nouns, whenever it is taken strictly—insofar, I mean, as it properly and truly signifies being stretched out on the ground.

But if it were to be accepted in a broad sense—namely, according to <term>Eustathius</term> <note>A 12th-century scholar and archbishop known for his extensive commentaries on Homer.</note>, as signifying anything set aside and hidden away—then, according to <term>Budaeus</term> <note>Guillaume Budé (1467–1540), a French humanist who helped establish classical studies.</note>, it denotes a certain assigned site where something is placed and there comes to rest. This is what Ulysses looked to in the *Iliad* when asking Dolon: *where not only the arms, but the horses were lying*.

In that case, what is to stop this word from also being consistent with the part about ***HEAVEN***? In this way, the Author, with a more elevated thought, would be looking toward the soul (unless Laelia had been devoid of one) which, after the death of the body, would seek Heaven, where it is granted to rest perpetually. Indeed, the blindness of the <term>pagans</term> <note>original: "Ethnica caecitas." The author refers to pre-Christian classical philosophers.</note> was once not far from this correct thought. The Light of Eloquence <note>A common title for the Roman orator Cicero.</note> surely directed his vision here (albeit with a slanted and therefore pitiable gaze) when, in the sixth book of *On the Republic*, he introduced the elder Scipio speaking thus to the younger:

*But so that you may be more eager, Africanus, to protect the Republic, know this: for all who have preserved, aided, or enhanced the Fatherland, there is a fixed and defined place in Heaven where the Blessed enjoy eternal life.*

And in the first book of the *Tusculan Disputations*:

*This may be understood from priestly law and the ceremonies of the tombs; men endowed with the greatest talent would neither have observed these with such care, nor sanctioned them with such inexpiable religious dread when violated, unless there had remained in their minds the conviction that death is not a destruction that takes away and blurs everything, but a kind of migration and change of life. In the case of famous men and women, this change is accustomed to be a guide into heaven; for others, it remains in the ground, and yet it endures.*

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the verb "lies" (iacet) in the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle can apply not just to the earth, but also to heaven. Drawing on Homer, Budaeus, and Cicero, he explains that "lying" can signify the soul's resting place or a "migration" to the celestial realm.</summary>
<keywords>Aelia Laelia Crispis, Cicero, Scipio Africanus, Eustathius, Budaeus, afterlife, philology, Roman law, immortality of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of using the word "lies" (iacet) in relation to heaven, citing classical poets to show that the souls of heroes and demigods were often described as inhabiting the celestial spheres. It then transitions to a grammatical defense of why the name "Lucius" is written in full on the Aelia Laelia Crispis stone rather than abbreviated.</meta>

# The One Not Born Rising Again, etc.
## Aa 2 D. D.

...man. From this comes the opinion of our own people:
> *Romulus spends his time in heaven with the gods.*
<note>original Latin: "Romulus in cælo cum dijs agit ævum." A reference to the deification of Rome's founder.</note>

<term>Lucan</term> <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet famous for his epic *Pharsalia*.</note> agrees excellently when, describing such a defined place according to the mindset of the <term>Platonists</term> <note>Followers of Plato who believed the soul ascended to the stars after death.</note>, he toyed with these verses regarding the deceased Pompey in Book 9:

> *He leaped from the pyre, leaving behind the half-burnt limbs*
> *and the unworthy funeral fire; he follows the vault of the Thunderer*
> *where the dark air is joined to the star-bearing axes,*
> *and where the space between the lands and the paths of the Moon*
> *is inhabited by the half-god spirits, those whom fiery virtue,*
> *innocent in life, made capable of enduring the lower ether,*
> *and gathered their souls into eternal spheres.*
<note>original Latin: "Prosiluit busto, semustaque membra relinquens... et æternos animam collegit in orbes." This passage describes the soul of Pompey the Great ascending to the heavens.</note>

Nor does <term>Papinius</term> <note>Publius Papinius Statius (c. 45–96 AD), a Roman poet.</note> write much differently concerning Menoeceus, that most brave avenger of Thebes, in the *Thebaid*, Book 10:

> — — — *now his spirit, long before Jove,*
> *already demands for itself a place among the highest stars.*
<note>original Latin: "jam spiritus olim / Ante Iovem, et summis apicem sibi poscit in astris."</note>

Finally, as a concluding flourish, our **LUCIUS** <note>Referring to Lucius Agatho Priscius, the name often associated with the dedication of the Aelia Laelia Crispis riddle.</note> remains to be defended from an objection that could be raised against him: namely, that he neglected in his own name that custom about which <term>Ausonius</term> <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD), a Roman poet and rhetorician.</note> wrote:

> *Lucius is indeed one letter, but separated by two points:*
> *thus a single mark makes the forename.*
<note>original Latin: "Lucius una quidem, geminis sed dissita punctis / Litera: prænomen sic nota sola facit." In Roman inscriptions, the name Lucius was almost always abbreviated as a single "L."</note>

The objection is that he himself—contrary to the nature of forenames, which are usually marked only by the first letter with a point added (in this manner: **L.**), and contrary to the long-standing custom of inscriptions—is read written in full on our Stone. This is true unless that same Triple Harmony, taking up the patronage of its author, granted him this silent permission. For this is entirely required by the harmony of three words everywhere, which must not be disturbed, and by which this numerical Stone wonderfully agrees with itself throughout; just as the <term>prosody</term> <note>The patterns of rhythm and sound used in poetry.</term> of verses requires the same **Lucius** to be written in full for correct <term>scanning</term> <note>The act of determining the rhythm of a line of verse.</note>, as is clear from the metrical inscription written below, cited by <term>Ferrettus</term> <note>Gabriele Ferretti, an author who collected inscriptions in his work "Musae Lapidariae" (Stone Muses).</note> in *Stone Muses*, Book 3, memorial 32:

<meta>Catchword: cited (adductâ)</meta>

<summary>The author uses classical poetry to justify the soul's residence in heaven and defends the unusual choice to write the name "Lucius" in full on the riddle's inscription, citing the internal harmony of the text and the requirements of poetic meter.</summary>
<keywords>Lucan, Pompey, Platonism, Papinius Statius, Menoeceus, Ausonius, Lucius, Roman inscriptions, prosody, Triple Harmony, Ferrettus</keywords>

Indeed, that which was anciently written sometimes in one way and sometimes in another was observed by us in Rome this year on these two related Arches: one in the Roman Forum, the other in the <term>Boarium</term> <note>The Forum Boarium was the ancient cattle market of Rome, located near the Tiber river.</note>, dedicated to Septimius Severus, and thus public; indeed, from the Imperial Inscriptions therein, which exempt themselves from any slight suspicion of error, and rather attribute this kind of negligence in the name to the usage, or perhaps the misuse, of that later century in which they felt the hand and the chisel. ON ACCOUNT OF THE RESTORATION OF THE REPUBLIC AND THE EXPANSION OF THE EMPIRE OF THE ROMAN PEOPLE

TO THE EMPEROR CAESAR LUCIUS SEPTIMIUS SEVERUS, PIOUS, PERTINAX, AUGUSTUS, CONQUEROR OF THE ARABS, CONQUEROR OF THE ADIABENI, GREATEST CONQUEROR OF THE PARTHIANS, MOST STRONG, MOST FORTUNATE,

For although [Aelia] now fills the place of a first name <term>praenomen</term>, and thus it might seem that it should have been written with only the first letters as *AEL.*, as is done everywhere; nevertheless, it is written in full several times, as seen below. # Not born, but rising again, etc. <note>original: "Non nata Resurgens &c." This likely refers to the "Aelia Laelia Crispis" enigma which the author is interpreting.</note>

->FREEDWOMEN, AND THEIR DESCENDANTS<- <note>original: "SIBI. ET. SVIS. LIBERTIS, LIBERTABVS. POSTERISQ. EORVM." This is a standard Roman legal formula ensuring the tomb remains in the family and their household staff.</note>

Even if a third name is added to two others, the first name—acting as a personal name <term>praenomen|The first name in a Roman's three-part name system (e.g., Marcus in Marcus Tullius Cicero).</term>—is not always dropped, as was sufficiently shown in the preceding chapter. That this was similarly observed among men will be clear from the two following examples, provided so as not to burden the most cultured Reader with further trouble.
From Mazzochi, as cited above, page 31. <note>Giacomo Mazzochi was a famous 16th-century Italian antiquarian and publisher known for his collections of Roman inscriptions.</note>

But enough regarding our inscription. It remains for me to earnestly beg the benevolent reader to forgive the excesses, as well as the shortcomings, of this little work, in accordance with the favor he may feel toward me (if he is perhaps so inclined). Indeed, from the beginning, I was not planning a formal treatise but a letter; yet the material gradually grew into a book. For it was not fitting to proceed without a prefatory explanation, nor should the learned interpreters of the inscription be kept silent—

# Aelia Laelia Crispis.

...interpreters. From this point, it was worthwhile to rescue the inscription itself from the corrupted readings of certain authors. Next, our own opinion must be revealed—an interpretation sought not from celestial or ideal realms, but from earthly matters and common occurrences <note>original: "non ab Aethereis, atque Idealibus, sed à Terreis, à Communiter Accidentibus." The author is arguing for a grounded, historical interpretation rather than a mystical or alchemical one.</note>. Following that, the names of the celebrated Laelia and the dedicator Agatho must be explained. I further deemed it wise to add some remarks concerning the erudition and craftsmanship, as well as the antiquity and purity of this same work. Finally, I intend to defend the arrangement of the three names, as well as the method of writing (supported by ancient precedents), against any objections. If the reader finds me unequal to the subject, and especially in my eloquence—as I have already confessed—he will not, I believe, dare to condemn my dedication. Through this effort, I have sought to honor the kindness of my excellent patron <term>Maecenas|Originally the name of Gaius Maecenas, a Roman counselor and famous patron of the arts; here the author uses the name as a title for his own benefactor.</term>, the monuments of my most beloved homeland, and the memory of learned antiquity to the best of my ability.

---

->TO THE CELESTIAL TRIPLE ENIGMA<- <note>original: "COELESTI TERNO AENIGMATI." This refers to the famous and mysterious "Aelia Laelia Crispis" inscription found in Bologna, which has puzzled scholars for centuries.</note>

->TO THE THREE AND ONE<- <note>original: "TRINO ET VNI." A traditional Christian reference to the Trinity.</note>

->TO GOD AND MAN<- <note>original: "DEO ET HOMINI." Referring to the dual nature of Christ.</note>

->TO VIRGIN AND MOTHER<- <note>original: "VIRGINI ET MATRI." Referring to the Virgin Mary.</note>

->PRAISE, HONOR, AND GLORY<-
